Jung Alchemy

1Is This a Breakdown or the Start of Something2The Word You Use for the Bad Months3The Book Nobody Was Allowed to Read4The Men Whose Language You Borrowed5Why Kings, Why a Corpse, Why a Wedding6The Man Who Could Not Have Known7Does It Count That It Is Old8The Ancient Tradition That Was Sixty Years Old9The Sealed Book That Was a Seance10What Would Have to Happen for This to Be Wrong11The Year the Vocabulary Stopped Describing You12Is There Anything in You That Makes These Pictures13Does It Matter How a True Thing Was Found14A Name Without a Date

0:00:00Cold open

In 2009 a painted red leather book that nobody outside one family had been allowed to read was published for the first time. It had spent about twenty-five years in a safe deposit box in a Zurich bank vault. Carl Jung worked on it for sixteen years, then abandoned it for alchemy. The story says the alchemists taught him the stages. The dates run the other way. The visions came first, in 1913, and the proof arrived in 1928: a Chinese text dictated at a spirit-writing altar in 1668.

What survives of all of it is a vocabulary. It is still in use. Mostly by people who cannot say where it came from.

00:01:01Part 1: Is This a Breakdown or the Start of Something

It is two in the morning of month fourteen, and you are doing the thing you always do. You are telling yourself which part of it this is. Nigredo. The blackening. The stage where the material goes dark and rots, and that is supposed to happen, and it comes before anything else can. You did not learn that word by accident. You went looking for it. You have gone looking for every other thing that might make the last year hold still long enough to be looked at.

Be exact about what that word is doing for you, because it is doing one enormous thing and only one. It says this is a passage and not a verdict. Now try to say that in English. Breakdown. Episode. Relapse. Crisis. Every one of those words arrives with the answer already inside it. A breakdown is a machine that has stopped working, and the word carries the judgement before you have finished saying it. An episode is an interruption in a life that was going fine. A relapse is a return to a worse condition you had already escaped once. There is no ordinary English word for an involuntary collapse that is building something. The language simply does not carry one.

So the word you reached for at two in the morning is load-bearing. It is holding open a question that your own language closes automatically. It came out of a pot.

Why the question is open at all is where this has to begin, because that is the part most people assume is settled. The reasonable assumption goes like this. Medicine has instruments. Somebody who knows what they are doing can look at what is happening to a person and say which way it is going. That assumption is not stupid. It is how we treat almost every other part of the body.

Here is the actual state of it. The nearest thing psychiatry has built to a forward-looking test is the set of instruments for what is called clinical high risk of psychosis. These are structured interviews. They are designed to find the people heading toward a first psychotic episode before it arrives.1 In 2022 a team led by Dominic Oliver pooled twenty clinical samples, nearly five thousand people. The results were followed out to a mean of about thirty-four months.2 These were not people off the street. Every sample consisted of people already referred to a specialist high-risk service, or already carrying a known genetic diagnosis.3 The population was pre-selected for risk before the instrument was ever applied.

10:03:25fact
Clinical high risk of psychosis instruments are structured or semi-structured clinical interviews designed to identify people who may be heading toward a first psychotic episode before it occurs.
Oliver, Arribas, Radua et al., 'Prognostic accuracy and clinical utility of psychometric instruments for individuals at clinical high-risk of psychosis: a systematic review and meta-analysis', Molecular Psychiatry 27 (2022), 3670-3678
Introduction / Methods (instruments included)
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. The CHR-P instruments pooled in the Oliver meta-analysis are interview instruments of this kind - the Comprehensive Assessment of At-Risk Mental States and the Structured Interview for Psychosis-Risk Syndromes are the two best known - administered by a clinician and scored against threshold criteria. The video needs only that the test is an interview, not a scan or an assay, because that is what makes the later multimodal recommendation intelligible.
What it does not say
Not read against the paper's Introduction; no instrument name, no psychometric property and no number may be spoken on this source. 'Designed to identify' is a statement of intent, not of performance; the accuracy figures live in Part 1 with their own limits.
20:03:45statistic
In twenty clinical samples covering 4,819 people, psychometric instruments for clinical high risk of psychosis pooled a sensitivity of 0.93 (95% CI 0.87-0.96) and a specificity of 0.58 (95% CI 0.50-0.66), at a mean follow-up of 34.4 months.
Dominic Oliver, Maite Arribas, Joaquim Radua, Gonzalo Salazar de Pablo, Andrea De Micheli, Giulia Spada, Martina Maria Mensi, Magdalena Kotlicka-Antczak, Renato Borgatti, Marco Solmi, Jae Il Shin, Scott W. Woods, Jean Addington, Philip McGuire, Paolo Fusar-Poli, 'Prognostic accuracy and clinical utility of psychometric instruments for individuals at clinical high-risk of psychosis: a systematic review and meta-analysis', Molecular Psychiatry 27(9): 3670-3678 (2022)
Results section, pooled prognostic accuracy in clinical samples: '20 clinical samples contributed data on CHR-P psychometric instruments for a total of 4819 individuals'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the Results of the Oliver et al. meta-analysis on PubMed Central. The paper states that 20 clinical samples contributed data for a total of 4819 individuals (CHR-P positive = 2333, CHR-P negative = 2486, proportion positive 48%), and reports the pooled accuracy as excellent for AUC, outstanding for sensitivity 0.93 (95% CI 0.87-0.96) and poor for specificity 0.58 (95% CI 0.50-0.66), at a mean follow-up time of 34.4 months (SD 25.5, median 27.0). This is the exact shape the Gap Argument's premise 2 needs: an instrument that rarely misses and rules very little in.
What it does not say
These are not accuracy percentages and they are not about the general public. Sensitivity 0.93 means that of those who went on to develop psychosis, 93% had tested positive; specificity 0.58 means 42% of those who did NOT develop psychosis were also flagged. The samples are people already referred to specialist high-risk services, a population enriched for risk before any test was applied, so the figures cannot be transferred to a person in crisis who has not been referred. The mean 34.4 months has a standard deviation of 25.5 and a median of 27.0, so follow-up length varies enormously between samples. Do not say 93% accurate.
30:04:01fact
Every clinical sample in that meta-analysis consisted of people already referred to clinical-high-risk services or already diagnosed with 22q11.2 deletion syndrome.
Oliver et al., 'Prognostic accuracy and clinical utility of psychometric instruments for individuals at clinical high-risk of psychosis', Molecular Psychiatry 27(9): 3670-3678 (2022)
Results section, description of included clinical samples: 'These samples all consisted of individuals referred to CHR-P services or diagnosed with a 22q11.2 deletion syndrome'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the Results of the same paper, which states that these samples all consisted of individuals referred to CHR-P services or diagnosed with a 22q11.2 deletion syndrome. This is the sentence that makes the sensitivity and specificity figures speak about a referred population rather than about a person alone in a room.
What it does not say
This does not mean the instruments are worthless; inside the clinic they carry real information. It means the population they were measured in was pre-selected by referral, so nothing in the paper licenses applying the figures to an unreferred person. The 22q11.2 subgroup is a genetic syndrome cohort and is not typical of help-seeking young adults.

In that population, the instruments caught about ninety-three of every hundred people who did go on to develop psychosis. That is the sensitivity, and it is genuinely good. The other number is the one that matters to you. Of the people who did not go on to develop psychosis, the instruments correctly cleared only about fifty-eight in a hundred. So more than forty in every hundred people who were going to be fine got flagged anyway. A positive result raises the odds of psychosis by roughly a factor of two. That is a test which is excellent at missing nobody and close to useless at ruling anything in.

Those numbers only bite with a person standing inside them. A twenty-two-year-old, fourteen months into something she cannot name. Not referred anywhere, not in a specialist clinic, not on anybody's list. She takes the best validated instrument the field has. It returns positive. What has she learned about herself? Essentially nothing. The instrument's performance was measured in a population she is not in. Applied outside that population, its own authors report that it has only limited clinical utility. That is their phrase, in the paper, about the general public.4 She has not been told what is happening to her. She has been told which door of a clinic she might usefully be pointed at.

40:05:40quotation
The authors of that meta-analysis state that Fagan's nomogram revealed only limited clinical utility for CHR-P psychometric instruments in the general population.
Oliver et al., 'Prognostic accuracy and clinical utility of psychometric instruments for individuals at clinical high-risk of psychosis', Molecular Psychiatry 27(9): 3670-3678 (2022)
Results section, clinical utility: "Fagan's nomogram revealed only limited clinical utility for CHR-P psychometric instruments in the general population"
Read the passage
How it holds
The sentence was read on the PubMed Central full text of the paper. It is the authors' own qualitative verdict on what happens when the pooled likelihood ratios are applied outside a specialist clinic, and it is the safe way to voice the collapse of the test's usefulness at population level.
What it does not say
The authors reach this verdict by feeding an assumed general-population incidence into a nomogram, and the specific post-test percentages that calculation produces (0.0017% and 0.0001%) are not credible as absolute risks: published incidence of psychotic disorders is roughly two orders of magnitude higher than the base rate the nomogram assumes. Voice the authors' qualitative conclusion; do not speak the percentages. 'Limited clinical utility' is a statement about a test, not a statement that psychosis is rare or unreal.

That is what converts "nobody can tell me what this is" from a complaint into a citation. The clinicians here are not being evasive. The instruments carry real information and the disorders they track are real. The thing that does not exist is the forward-looking verdict on one person, on a Tuesday, in month fourteen.

The pieces go in order here, because the argument is short and it should be checkable. First, there are episodes of severe involuntary destabilisation whose ending is not fixed when they start. Some of them end in a chronic disorder. Some of them end in a life that has been durably rebuilt. Second, there is no reliable instrument that sorts an individual episode into one of those before it has run. Third, ordinary English offers only words that describe the bad ending, and every one presupposes deficit. Fourth, a word that presupposes deficit does not answer the constructive question. It removes the words in which the question could be asked.

Which leaves a person facing a genuinely undecided question in a language that cannot state it. That is why there is a real and standing demand for a different kind of vocabulary. One that names phases, asserts that they follow one another, and refuses to say how it ends.

You can measure the size of the gap by looking at what the profession officially conceded. In 1994 the fourth edition of the diagnostic manual added a category called Religious or Spiritual Problem. It did not file it among the mental disorders. It filed it in the section for other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention.5 That is the part of the manual for things a clinician may legitimately spend an hour on without asserting that anything is broken. So the concession was real. The manual admitted that this kind of crisis exists, and that misreading it as illness is a failure.

50:07:48fact
DSM-IV, published in 1994, added a category called Religious or Spiritual Problem, and filed it not among the mental disorders but in the section for other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention.
American Psychiatric Association, Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, 4th edition (Washington DC: APA, 1994); with the APA's own derivative volumes Diagnostic Criteria from DSM-IV and DSM-IV Coding Update (M. B. First)
chapter 'Other Conditions That May Be a Focus of Clinical Attention' (contents entry '.... 675'); entry 'V62.89 Religious or Spiritual Problem', p. 685
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. DSM-IV introduced the V-code Religious or Spiritual Problem, proposed by Lukoff, Lu and Turner, and placed it in the chapter of conditions that are not mental disorders but may bring a person into clinical contact. The placement is the whole point of the beat: the manual concedes that the experience exists and simultaneously declines to say what it is, what causes it, where it goes or how to tell it from an illness. No diagnostic criteria are supplied for it and no course or prognosis is asserted.
What it does not say
Not checked against a copy of DSM-IV, so no page and no verbatim wording may be used. The exact descriptive sentence in the manual, and its page, are not in hand. Do not treat the addition as the DSM recognising spiritual emergency: it is a much smaller administrative move, and the proposers' own later title, From Spiritual Emergency to Spiritual Problem, records the retreat. The V62.89 number is DSM-IV's; current coding conventions differ, so do not imply the number is permanent.

And then it stopped. The category supplies no criteria for telling this crisis from that one. It supplies no account of causes. It supplies no prognosis, and, most importantly, no claim that the thing goes anywhere or builds anything. The official position is that the experience exists and that the manual has nothing to say about it. That is the exact shape of the hole, drawn by the people best placed to fill it.

One historian did try to name the missing thing, and he did it from the outside. Henri Ellenberger, writing a history of dynamic psychiatry, proposed a pattern he called creative illness. His definition opens like this. A creative illness follows a period of intense preoccupation with an idea, and a search for a certain truth.6 Then comes a condition he calls polymorphous, meaning an illness that takes no single form. Then he turned to the training analysis, the long analysis an analyst goes through before being allowed to practise. That, he wrote, was a kind of initiatory malady comparable to the shaman's.

60:09:06fact
Henri Ellenberger proposed a historical pattern he called creative illness, in which a period of intense preoccupation is followed by a prolonged, polymorphous illness through which the sufferer never loses hold of the preoccupation, ending in a rapid recovery and a permanent transformation, and he aligned that pattern with the initiatory illness of the shaman.
Henri F. Ellenberger, The Discovery of the Unconscious: The History and Evolution of Dynamic Psychiatry (Basic Books, 1970; Fontana Press reprint 1994)
the passage defining creative illness (immediately following a reference to C. G. Jung), and the passage on training analysis and the shaman
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. Ellenberger set the concept out in a 1968 article in the Psychoanalytic Review and expanded it in The Discovery of the Unconscious (1970), where he applies it to Fechner, Freud and Jung and explicitly places it alongside the shaman's initiatory sickness, the illness that qualifies rather than disqualifies. This is the historian's version of the video's undecided episode: a description that asserts sequence and holds the outcome open, which is exactly what the clinical vocabulary refuses to do.
What it does not say
Paraphrase only. No page was verified in either the 1968 article or the 1970 book, so none of Ellenberger's defining sentences may be quoted, and the frequently repeated wordings about intense preoccupation, never losing the thread and termination marked by exhilaration must not be put in quotation marks. There is no verified canonical list of four cases; say including Freud and Jung. The concept is also fatally exposed to survivorship bias: Ellenberger's sample is people who afterwards wrote books, so everyone who did not recover is absent from it by construction.

That is a historian of psychiatry saying in print that the older vocabularies were tracking something the clinical vocabulary drops. Which puts two facts side by side. There is no test. And every word English hands you for this already assumes you are broken. Which means the word you reached for at two in the morning was the only instrument available to you. And it also means that nobody has ever checked it.

So turn on it now, hard, and start with Ellenberger. Ask where his cases came from. They came from people who wrote books afterwards. The whole pattern is guaranteed by how the sample was assembled. Preoccupation, then an illness, then a person still able to write the book that put him in the sample. A man who had the identical experience and never came out of it did not write a chapter about it. The data cannot contain him. This is survivorship bias with a theory attached, and it is fatal to the strong version. Creative illness cannot be evidence that breakdown produces insight.

So retreat, out loud, to the weaker claim, which is all the argument ever needed. Some severe destabilisations are followed by durable reorganisation. That is enough. It does not say yours will be. It only says the prospective question is genuinely open, and an open question is the one thing English cannot express.

The second attack is worse, because it is not about evidence at all. It is about consequences. There is a long literature on the duration of untreated psychosis, the gap between when symptoms start and when treatment begins. A meta-analysis of thirty-three samples found that a longer gap tracked a worse long-term outcome. That held for symptoms and for remission alike.78 The correlations were small. The same analysis found no significant relationship with employment or quality of life. But it points in one direction and it does not reverse.

70:11:53statistic
In the same meta-analysis the pooled area under the curve was 0.85 (95% CI 0.81-0.88), a positive result carried a likelihood ratio of 2.17 (95% CI 1.81-2.60) and a negative result a likelihood ratio of 0.11 (95% CI 0.06-0.21).
Oliver et al., 'Prognostic accuracy and clinical utility of psychometric instruments for individuals at clinical high-risk of psychosis', Molecular Psychiatry 27(9): 3670-3678 (2022)
Results section, pooled accuracy: 'AUC 0.85 (95% CI: 0.81-0.88)', 'Being CHR-P + was associated with a small likelihood ratio LR + (2.17, 95% CI: 1.81-2.60)'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the same Results section. The authors describe the AUC as excellent, the positive likelihood ratio as small, and the negative likelihood ratio as large. A positive likelihood ratio of 2.17 means a positive result roughly doubles the pre-test odds; a negative likelihood ratio of 0.11 means a negative result cuts them by about nine tenths. That asymmetry is the whole point: the test is far better at excluding than at confirming.
What it does not say
A likelihood ratio is a multiplier on odds, not a probability of anything. LR+ 2.17 does NOT mean a positive result gives a 2.17 times higher chance of becoming psychotic in absolute terms, and it does not mean anyone has a 217% risk. What the multiplier does to an individual depends entirely on their starting risk, which for someone never referred to a clinic is very low. AUC 0.85 is a ranking statistic across a whole sample, not a statement about any one person's episode.
80:11:44statistic
A meta-analysis of 33 samples found that a longer duration of untreated psychosis correlated with worse long-term outcome, with correlations of about minus 0.13 to minus 0.15 for general symptomatic outcome, positive symptoms, negative symptoms and remission, and no significant correlation with employment, quality of life or hospital treatment.
M. Penttila, E. Jaaskelainen, N. Hirvonen, M. Isohanni and J. Miettunen, 'Duration of untreated psychosis as predictor of long-term outcome in schizophrenia: systematic review and meta-analysis', British Journal of Psychiatry 205(2): 88-94 (2014), doi 10.1192/bjp.bp.113.127753
Results section, meta-analysis of DUP against outcome categories: 'Thirty-nine articles analysing 33 different samples met our inclusion criteria'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the full text of Penttila, Jaaskelainen, Hirvonen, Isohanni and Miettunen's British Journal of Psychiatry meta-analysis. Thirty-nine articles analysing 33 different samples met inclusion. The reported correlations were general symptomatic outcome r = -0.15 (95% CI -0.22 to -0.09), positive symptoms r = -0.14, negative symptoms r = -0.13, remission r = -0.14; employment r = -0.05, quality of life r = -0.10 and hospital treatment r = -0.09 were not significant. This is the empirical leg of the mechanism-of-harm objection: delay is associated with worse outcome, so a vocabulary that postpones help-seeking has a traceable path to harm.
What it does not say
These are correlations of roughly 0.15, which is small: duration of untreated psychosis explains on the order of two per cent of the variance in outcome. The design is observational, so the association may partly reflect that insidious, poor-prognosis illnesses are the ones that go undetected longest, rather than delay causing the damage. The authors themselves describe the effect as small but mostly consistent and say only that early intervention may have at least subtle positive effects. Three of the outcomes measured showed no significant association at all. Do not convert this into a claim that delay ruins lives.

Then there is what a person's explanation of their own illness does to their behaviour. A systematic review published in 2025 collects this. In one study inside it, patients who explained their illness in magical or religious terms had significantly worse attitudes toward their medication. They also had significantly more severe positive symptoms.9 In another, higher religiosity tracked a lower preference for psychiatric treatment and lower functioning.10 Chain those together and you get something that is not a debating point. A vocabulary telling a person in a first episode that this is an initiation is an explanatory model. It changes what they do. And there is a traceable path from that to a longer delay and a worse life. That chain is an inference across two literatures rather than a measured pathway, and it should be stated as one. It is still the strongest objection in this video.

90:12:28statistic
A 2025 systematic review of spirituality and religiosity in psychosis reports that in one included study patients holding stronger magical-religious causal beliefs about their illness had significantly lower attitude-to-medication scores, with a standardised coefficient of minus 0.27, and significantly more severe positive symptoms, with a coefficient of 0.17.
M. Westhead and A. Georgiades, 'The role of spirituality and religiosity in the maintenance and recovery of psychosis: a systematic review', Early Intervention in Psychiatry 19(7): e70061 (2025)
Results, findings by study: 'patients with higher magical-religious causal beliefs had significantly lower DAI-10 scores' section reporting Caqueo-Urizar et al. 2015
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the findings-by-study section of Westhead and Georgiades's systematic review on PubMed Central, which reports these figures from Caqueo-Urizar and colleagues (2015) with p < 0.05 in both cases. It is the closest thing the literature has to a measured link between holding a religious explanation of one's illness and behaviour that worsens outcome.
What it does not say
These are standardised regression coefficients within one cross-sectional sample, not effects on a person. A beta of 0.17 on a symptom scale is a weak association, and the direction of causation is not established: people who are more symptomatic may be more likely to reach for a magical-religious explanation. The figures are read here inside a review, not in Caqueo-Urizar's own paper, which was not opened. Do not say that believing this makes you sicker.
100:12:33statistic
The same 2025 review reports that in another included study higher religiosity scores correlated with a lower preference for psychiatric treatment, correlation minus 0.54, with longer duration of illness, correlation 0.37, and with lower functioning, correlation minus 0.39.
Westhead and Georgiades, 'The role of spirituality and religiosity in the maintenance and recovery of psychosis: a systematic review', Early Intervention in Psychiatry 19(7): e70061 (2025)
Results, findings by study: 'Higher scores of religiosity were found to significantly correlate with, longer duration of illness (r = 0.37, p < 0.05), lower preference of psychiatric treatment (r = -0.54, p < 0.05)'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the same findings-by-study section, reporting Huang and colleagues (2011), all at p < 0.05. Together with the previous claim this is the middle link of the harm chain: an explanatory model that is religious rather than medical tracks with not wanting psychiatric treatment, and delay in treatment tracks with worse outcome.
What it does not say
Correlational, cross-sectional and read inside a review rather than in Huang's own paper, which was not opened. A correlation of minus 0.54 between two questionnaire scores is not a measurement of anyone's behaviour, and longer duration of illness may raise religiosity as easily as the reverse. This corrects an error in the project's own research files, which had reported Huang as being about magico-religious healing; what the review reports is treatment preference, duration and functioning.

The same 2025 review carries the other side, and it is sharper than anybody arguing about this wants it to be. In one of the studies it includes, participants who scored high on spirituality were in remission at about eighty-six percent. Among those who scored low, the figure was about fifty-three. Lifetime suicidal thoughts ran at about one in six in the high-spirituality group, and about one in three in the low.11 These are correlations in single samples, and the direction is not established. People who are recovering may well score higher on spirituality because they are recovering. But they point the opposite way from the harm finding, inside one review.

110:13:42statistic
The same 2025 review also reports the opposite side: in one included study 85.7 per cent of participants with high spirituality were in remission against 53.3 per cent of those with low spirituality, and lifetime suicidal thoughts were reported by 33.3 per cent of the low-spirituality group against 16.3 per cent of the high.
Westhead and Georgiades, 'The role of spirituality and religiosity in the maintenance and recovery of psychosis: a systematic review', Early Intervention in Psychiatry 19(7): e70061 (2025)
Results, findings by study: 'participants with high spirituality (85.7%) were more likely to be in remission than those with low spirituality (53.3%)'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the same findings-by-study section, reporting Esan and Lawal (2021). It is the reply the video has to voice at full strength: the same review that supplies the harm mechanism supplies the protective association, and the defensible position is the split between a religious framework used as an explanation of the illness and one used as a resource for enduring it.
What it does not say
Cross-sectional and correlational, from a single study inside a review, not a pooled estimate; the primary paper was not opened. The direction is unestablished and plausibly runs backwards, since people already in remission have more capacity for religious practice. A 32-point difference in remission rates between two self-selected groups is not a 32-point improvement produced by spirituality. Say in one study inside a 2025 review, or do not say the numbers.

The distinction that reconciles them is the one this whole video runs on. A religious or spiritual framework used as an explanation of the pathology tracks worse outcomes. The same framework used as a resource for enduring the thing tracks better ones. An explanation tells you what is wrong and what to do about it, and it competes with the medical account. A resource tells you nothing about causes and only helps you stay in the room. That split is the difference between a word that helps you survive month fourteen and a word that keeps you out of a clinic.

There is also a live question about whether this boundary can be measured at all. The honest answer is embarrassing for both camps. The construct built specifically to name the non-pathological version of this experience has one main scale.12 That scale moves in the same direction as the very thing it was invented to be told apart from. Meanwhile the prediction researchers agree the forward-looking test does not currently exist. Their own recommendation is that the symptom interviews be supplemented with information from other modalities beyond symptomatology. Proteomics, neuroimaging, and clinical and neurocognitive data.13 Nobody on either side is claiming to have the answer you needed at two in the morning.

120:15:21fact
The construct of spiritual emergency has a main measurement scale, and scores on it were found to correlate positively with positive psychotic symptoms and with positive schizotypy.
Kylie P. Harris, Adam J. Rock and Gavin I. Clark, 'Spiritual Emergence(y), Psychosis, and Personality: Investigating the Role of Schizotypy', International Journal of Transpersonal Studies 38(2) (2019)
Results, Table 4 (correlations), N = 250
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled as to the finding; the article's landing page was opened but the full text was refused (Cloudflare 403 on both the direct PDF and a text proxy), so only the abstract was visible, and an abstract is not a source. The finding itself is the one the video needs: the instrument built to mark this experience off from psychosis turns out to track the psychotic end rather than to separate from it.
What it does not say
No copy of Method or Results was read, so the sample size, the scale's name and every correlation coefficient are OUT: they must not be spoken. The direction of association is all this source carries, and a positive correlation in a cross-sectional online sample is not evidence that spiritual emergency is psychosis. The authors' own reading is more sympathetic than the bare correlation sounds.
130:15:43fact
The authors of the 2022 CHR-P meta-analysis conclude that the psychometric instruments are not sufficient on their own and must be combined with other kinds of evidence.
Dominic Oliver et al., 'Prognostic accuracy and clinical utility of psychometric instruments for individuals at clinical high-risk of psychosis: a systematic review and meta-analysis', Molecular Psychiatry 27(9): 3670-3678 (2022)
Discussion, on refining risk estimates: 'CHR-P psychometric instruments should be supplemented with information from other modalities beyond symptomatology (e.g. proteomics, neuroimaging and clinical/neurocognitive data)'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the Discussion of the Oliver et al. full text on PubMed Central. The authors write that to refine estimates to the individual level, CHR-P psychometric instruments should be supplemented with information from other modalities beyond symptomatology, naming proteomics, neuroimaging and clinical/neurocognitive data, and they give the Psychosis Polyrisk Score as an example of the environmental side. This is the source for the script's line that the prediction researchers themselves say the forward-looking test does not yet exist.
What it does not say
This is a recommendation for research, not an admission that the instruments are useless: the same paper reports high sensitivity inside specialist services. Do not report the multimodal programme as already built or validated; the authors are describing what would be needed. No effect sizes or accuracy figures may be taken off this source.

So the question is not whether you were right to reach for it. Given all of that, reaching for it was the only rational move available. The question is what you picked up. That vocabulary was not invented for you, or for anyone like you. It was invented for something in a pot.

00:16:28Part 2: The Word You Use for the Bad Months

A shallow bronze platform, perforated like a colander, resting on a pedestal inside a sealed pot. Underneath it sits a liquid that carries sulphur, and underneath that sits the fire. In Attic the word for brimstone and the word for the divine are written the same.14 Two different entries in the lexicon, one spelling, and the later writers made the pun. A piece of base metal lies on the platform. The vapour comes up through the holes, condenses on the cooler metal above, and leaves a film across the surface. Sulphides and oxides.15 The film is black. That is melanosis. Blackening. The word underneath your word. It is not a mood and it is not a season of the soul. It is a discoloured skin on a piece of cheap metal. A competent chemist could put one in front of you this afternoon.

140:16:41fact
The Greek word for sulphur and the Greek word for divine are the same word, theion, which is why the sulphur-bearing liquid of the Greek alchemists is called divine water.
Liddell, Scott and Jones, A Greek-English Lexicon, entries θεῖον (A) and θεῖον (B)
s.v. θεῖον (A) and θεῖον (B), Perseus entry ids qei=on1 and qei=on2
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. In Greek theion is both 'brimstone, sulphur' and the neuter of theios, 'divine', and theion hydor is accordingly read both ways, as sulphur water and as divine water. Werrett's edition, which WAS read, does render the phrase as 'Divine Water or sulphurous water', which is consistent with the pun, but it does not contain a passage stating the etymology, so the philology is carried here as a recalled lexical fact and not as something read in that thesis.
What it does not say
No lexicon page was opened: no dictionary entry, no citation line and no accent may be quoted, and the claim must not be dressed up as a discovery by any named scholar. The identity of form does not settle whether any given alchemist intended the pun; that is an interpretive claim this passage does not carry.
150:17:01fact
The blackening on the kerotakis is a film of metal sulphides and oxides: sulphur-bearing vapour rising from below condenses in the reflux chamber and bathes the base metal, which oxidises and darkens.
Joshua Werrett, The Visions of Zosimos of Panopolis: Text, Translation, and Commentary, DPhil thesis, University of Oxford, 2024, doi 10.5287/ora-gab6xyvky
commentary, section on the Divine Water and the kerotakis, on the formation of the black layer
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the commentary of Werrett's 2024 Oxford DPhil edition of the Visions of Zosimos, in the discussion of the Divine Water and the kerotakis: 'As the Divine Water boiled and volatilised, its caustic vapours ascended through the main alembic, around the kerotakis, and into the reflux chamber. At this stage, the vapours condensed and the resulting liquids bathed the base metal, causing the substance to oxidise, and form on its surface a black layer of metal sulphides and oxides.' The nigredo therefore has a chemistry before it has a symbolism, which is Part 2's whole point.
What it does not say
This is Werrett's reconstruction of the chemistry behind Zosimos's description, not a laboratory replication reported in the thesis, and it is the mechanism for this apparatus, not for every blackening anyone in the tradition called nigredo. Do not present the kerotakis as the standard alchemical apparatus. The thesis PDF was read through a text-extraction proxy, so the locator is section-level and no printed page is claimed.

So the word travelled. It began as a report on a surface and ended as a report on a person. Somewhere on that road it picked up everything that makes it useful at two in the morning. The oldest alchemical writings we have are, in origin, books about dyeing. How to dye metal so that it goes the colour of gold. How to dye wool so that it goes purple.16 Has it gone the right colour yet is a workshop question for centuries before anyone asks it about a human being. That sets the real problem. If the word now names a state of a soul, something licensed the transfer. The honest question is what survives the transfer besides the feeling.

160:17:57fact
The oldest surviving alchemical writings were in origin books of recipes for dyeing: colouring metals to look like gold and silver, and dyeing wool purple.
Matteo Martelli, 'Pseudo-Democritus' Alchemical Works: Tradition, Contents and Afterlife' (Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin paper, 2016)
Section on the contents of the four books; discussion of the surviving first book
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. The four books ascribed to Democritus, the oldest substantial Greek alchemical text, are organised as recipe collections for making gold, making silver, working precious stones and dyeing purple, and Martelli's edition treats the corpus as descended from craft dye-books rather than from a mystical doctrine. It is the load-bearing fact for Part 2's opening move, that the tradition begins in a workshop.
What it does not say
No page of Martelli's edition or introduction was opened, so nothing may be quoted and no manuscript, date or recipe number may be stated. 'Dyeing' is the ancient category, which covers surface colouring and alloying together; do not translate it as 'faking', which is the polemical reading Part 2 explicitly refuses elsewhere.

But one word is not what anybody actually leans on. A single name for a bad month is a label, and a label does not get a person through a Tuesday. What does the work is the ORDER. Black, then white, then red. A place in a sequence, and therefore a direction, and therefore an implied later. And that order is genuinely old, which is the strongest thing that can be said for it. The Greek alchemical corpus runs a fourfold colour progression. Melanosis, the blackening. Then leukosis, the whitening. Then xanthosis, the yellowing. Then iosis, which gets rendered as purpling or as reddening.17 Latin Europe inherited all four and gave them Latin names. Nigredo, albedo, citrinitas, rubedo.18 Nobody in a wellness catalogue invented that. It is in the record, it is inherited across languages, and the black comes first. One thing is not in the record, while we are here. The line the whole tradition is remembered for, as above so below, is in none of these Greek authors.19 Where it does come from is a later part of this story.

170:19:15fact
The Greek alchemical corpus works with a fourfold colour progression, usually ordered as melanosis or blackening, leukosis or whitening, xanthosis or yellowing, and iosis, which is rendered as purpling or reddening.
Jennifer M. Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71(1): 73-97 (2024), doi 10.1080/00026980.2024.2309059
Introduction section: 'The usual order is melanosis (blackening), leukosis (whitening), xanthosis (yellowing), and iosis (purpling)'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the full text of Jennifer Rampling's 2024 Ambix article, which states that the usual order is melanosis (blackening), leukosis (whitening), xanthosis (yellowing) and iosis (purpling). This is the scheme the whole three-stage popular arc is descended from, and it has four terms, not three.
What it does not say
Rampling says the usual order, not the only order; the Greek corpus is not a single doctrine and the fourth term's translation is itself unsettled, which is why iosis is given as purpling here and reddening elsewhere. Do not say the Greeks had four stages as a fixed doctrine. The article was read through a text-extraction proxy of the Taylor and Francis full text, which does not preserve page numbers, so the locator is section-level.
180:19:20fact
Latin alchemists inherited that fourfold progression as nigredo, albedo, citrinitas and rubedo.
Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71(1): 73-97 (2024)

Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the same article, which states that Latin alchemists eventually inherited the fourfold colour progression of black (nigredo), white (albedo), yellow (citrinitas or citrinatio) and red (rubedo). The Latin West therefore starts with four, and the three-term version popular today is a later contraction of it.
What it does not say
Inheriting a scheme is not the same as agreeing on it, and the rest of Rampling's article is about the Latin authorities failing to agree. Do not present nigredo-albedo-citrinitas-rubedo as the standard medieval process either. Section-level locator: the copy read was a text proxy without pagination.
190:19:48fact
The line the whole tradition is remembered for, as above so below, is not in any of these Greek authors: it belongs to the Emerald Tablet, whose oldest surviving text is Arabic rather than Greek.
Julius Ruska, Tabula Smaragdina: Ein Beitrag zur Geschichte der hermetischen Literatur (Heidelberg: Carl Winter, 1926)
Übersicht des Inhalts, pp. V-VII: chapter I sub-heading 'Die Tabula Smaragdina nicht griechisch', p. 37; chapter VI 'Das arabische Original der Tabula Smaragdina', pp. 107-122
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. The Emerald Tablet has no known Greek original. Its earliest attested form is the Arabic text embedded in the Kitab Sirr al-khaliqa, the Book of the Secret of Creation attributed to Balinas, the pseudo-Apollonius of Tyana, and the Latin versions that carried the tablet into Europe are translations from Arabic. This is the one-sentence forward pointer Part 2 needs, and its whole force is that the most famous sentence in Western alchemy is absent from the corpus the colour scheme comes from.
What it does not say
Recalled, not verified against a copy. No date, no dating window and no translator may be stated on this source, and the wording of the tablet in any language must not be quoted from it; Part 7 owns the dating and must source it separately. The claim is only that the tablet is not attested in Greek and that the oldest text of it is Arabic.

Now watch what happens to the fourth colour. This is where the ladder stops behaving like a ladder. A treatise circulating under the name of Ramon Llull states flatly that there are only three principal colours. Black, white and red. Yellow, on that account, is not a colour of the work at all.20 It is a compound, a mixture of whiteness and redness, with no rank of its own. John Dastin keeps four on his list and then guts the fourth. Yellow, for him, ranks well below the other three. It is transient and unstable. It is worth something only as a sign that you should keep working.21 Then the English canon of Bridlington, George Ripley, refuses it outright. Ripley will not have citrination as a principal colour at all. He writes instead of a false citrine, and files it among the imperfect colours of the work.2223

200:20:13fact
The pseudo-Lullian Testamentum claims that there are only three principal colours, black, white and red, and treats yellow not as a principal colour but as a compound produced by mixing whiteness and redness.
Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71(1): 73-97 (2024)
section 'The decline of citrinitas in alchemical philosophy': 'claims that there are only three principal colours: black, white, and red'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in Rampling's article, in the section on the decline of citrinitas. She names the anonymous author, following Michela Pereira, as the Magister Testamenti, and reports that he admits only three principal colours and explains citrine away as a compound. This is the single clearest instance of a major authority cutting the scheme to three, and it is a fourteenth-century move, not a modern one.
What it does not say
This is one treatise's position, not a consensus; the article's whole argument is that the authorities split. The exact wording of the Testamentum's explanation of yellow could not be pinned down: two extractions of the article returned different formulations, one saying compounded of much white and the purest red and one saying compounded of much white and very little black in a passage attributed to pseudo-Arnald. Do not quote the phrase. State only that yellow is treated as a compound rather than a principal colour.
210:20:40fact
John Dastin, while nominally listing four principal colours, treated yellow as decidedly inferior and as a transient and unstable appearance valuable only as a sign to keep working.
Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71(1): 73-97 (2024)
section 'The decline of citrinitas in alchemical philosophy', on Dastin: 'yellow occupying a decidedly inferior position'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the same section of Rampling's article, which states that Dastin at first seems to follow the Codicillus by including yellow among four principal colours but that further examination reveals he did not view all of these colours as equal, with yellow occupying a decidedly inferior position, and that the appearance of this transient, unstable yellowness is valueless except as a sign to keep working. Dastin is the middle position between keeping yellow and refusing it.
What it does not say
Dastin does not abolish yellow; he demotes it while still using it. Do not report him as saying there are three colours. The article was read through a text proxy without pagination, so the locator is section-level and the sentences must not be read aloud as verified verbatim quotation.

Read in Rampling's article, which devotes a whole section to this, headed with Ripley's own phrase, and quotes his verse line listing pale and black with falce citryne alongside unperfect white and red. Ripley is the hardest case for the popular three-stage ladder, because he is a canonical English authority who says the yellow you are looking at is not a stage at all.
What it does not say
The Middle English spelling is reported here from a text-extraction proxy of the article, so treat the spelling as Rampling's transcription and do not present the verse line as a verified verbatim quotation from Ripley's own text. Ripley's refusal is a polemical position within a dispute, not the settled medieval view.
230:20:53fact
George Ripley, the English authority who refused citrine as a principal colour, was a canon of Bridlington.
Jennifer M. Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71(1): 73-97 (2024), doi 10.1080/00026980.2024.2309059
section "George Ripley's 'falce citryne'", citing 'The Bosome-Book of Sir George Ripley, Canon of Bridlington'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in Rampling's Ambix article, which cites his work under the title 'The Bosome-Book of Sir George Ripley, Canon of Bridlington' and treats him as a fifteenth-century English alchemical authority (fl. 1470s). The video needs the institutional fact only so that the listener hears a churchman, not a back-street operator, saying the yellow is false.
What it does not say
What was read is 'Canon of Bridlington' in a title Rampling quotes; the fuller standard description, an Augustinian canon regular of the priory of Bridlington, was not read and should not be elaborated on camera. Ripley's biography is thin and much of what circulates about him is legend.

Put that in a room. Two competent readers of the same tradition stand over the same pot. The matter in it has gone yellow. One of them says citrinitas. Third colour. You are nearly there, hold your heat, the red is coming. Ripley looks at the identical yellow and says false citrine. The counterfeiter's colour, the thing that resembles gold and is not gold. And on that reading, taking it for a stage is the error that ruins the work. They are not disagreeing about the yellow. Both men see yellow. Neither is colour-blind and neither is lying. They are disagreeing about whether the thing they are both looking at is a stage.

The popular explanation is numerology, three colours for the three of the Trinity. The manuscripts give a duller reason. Jennifer Rampling, in a 2024 study, puts it on suspicion. A yellow product is what a forger produced, the brass that passes at a market stall and fails an assay. So a serious adept had to disown the very colour his own scheme had promoted.24 And the same man who threw the yellow out counts the work a third way entirely. Ripley's own long poem sets out the opus as twelve gates, each gate a chemical operation, organised by operations rather than by colours. The same tradition, inside the same two centuries, counts the work as four colours, as three, and as twelve gates.25 And Rampling's suggestion is that yellow was dropped because yellow is the colour a forger's gold goes.

240:22:16fact
Rampling argues that yellow's own closeness to the colour of gold is what hastened its downfall as a philosophically respectable colour, because a merely yellow product was what a counterfeiter produced and what a serious adept therefore had to disown.
Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71(1): 73-97 (2024)
section 'Signs of completion: imitating gold': 'Yellow's very proximity to gold may have hastened its downfall as a philosophically-respectable hue'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the article, which states that yellow's very proximity to gold may have hastened its downfall as a philosophically-respectable hue, sets this against contemporary anxiety about fraudulent multiplied gold, and distinguishes citrination, which made metal appear golden, from transmutation, which made metal gold. This replaces the popular numerological story about the Trinity with a practical and polemical explanation from the current scholarship.
What it does not say
Rampling hedges: she writes that proximity to gold MAY have hastened the downfall. Voice it as her proposed explanation, not as an established cause. It is also a claim about why a colour lost philosophical standing among certain authorities, not a claim that alchemists in general were counterfeiters. The competing Trinity-and-quaternity explanation is a popular-source story with no scholarly support and must not be voiced as history.
250:22:45fact
The same late medieval English tradition also organised the work by twelve operations rather than by colours: George Ripley's Compound of Alchemy sets out the opus as twelve gates, each one a chemical operation.
George Ripley, The Compound of Alchymy. Or The ancient hidden Art of Archemie: Conteining the right & perfettest meanes to make the Philosophers Stone, Aurum potabile, with other excellent Experiments. Divided into twelue Gates. First written by the learned and rare Philosopher of our Nation George Ripley, sometime Chanon of Bridlington in Yorkeshyre... Set foorth by Raph Rabbards (London: Thomas Orwin, 1591)
Title page; and the gate headings running through the poem: 'Of Calcination. The first Gate.' through 'Of Firmentation. The ninth Gate.'
Read the passage
How it holds
Ripley's Compound of Alchemy, or the Twelve Gates leading to the Discovery of the Philosophers' Stone, written in the 1470s, structures the whole work as a castle with twelve gates, the operations running from calcination and solution through putrefaction and sublimation to multiplication and projection. It is load-bearing for Part 2's conclusion: the same author, in the same country, in the same decades, counts the work as twelve and also refuses one of the four colours, so no count was canonical.
What it does not say
Read: the 1591 London printing (Orwin for Rabbards) in the Internet Archive's Early English Books scan - the title page and the gate headings one to nine, in the original spelling. Ashmole's 1652 text was NOT reached: the archive.org djvu text of Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum truncates inside Norton's Ordinall of Alchimy, well before Ripley, so nothing may be cited to Ashmole 1652 and the alchemywebsite transcription remains unchecked against its own exemplar. Gate headings ten to twelve are not sourced. No line of Ripley's verse beyond the title page may be quoted.

Here is where it would be easy to overcorrect. The overcorrection is a worse mistake than the thing it corrects. The line to take is not there were four, not three. That sentence is wrong in precisely the way the original is wrong. It picks a count and crowns it. The finding is narrower and harder. There was no canon. Saying there was no canon is not saying three was rare. Three may well have been the commonest settlement of the late Middle Ages. Both of those can be true at once. It is also no evidence at all that nothing was being tracked.

That last point is the strongest thing anyone can say against the whole of this part. While the theorists were busy demoting yellow, the practitioners went on using yellowing. It stayed in service as a working token that the matter was moving. Rampling's article is not organised as a ladder at all.26 It is organised around signs. Signs of readiness in the calcining of vitriol. Signs of progress in the kindling of lead. Signs of completion in the imitation of gold. A sign that experts quarrel over is still a sign. The film really does form on the metal. Its forming really does tell the man at the furnace something true about what is happening in his pot. So the quarrel over how many stages there are does not touch the question of whether anything is being described. And if nigredo has a real referent in a vessel, the possibility to face squarely is that your word has a real referent in you. Something IS happening. Two late medieval readers disagreeing does not make your fourteen months imaginary. Nothing here entitles anybody to tell you it does.

260:24:11fact
While the theorists were demoting yellow, practitioners went on using yellowing as a working sign that the process was moving, and Rampling's article is organised around signs of readiness, of progress and of completion rather than around a ladder of stages.
Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71(1): 73-97 (2024)
sections 'Signs of readiness: calcining vitriol', 'Signs of progress: kindling lead', 'Signs of completion: imitating gold', and 'Conclusion: reconstructing alchemical signs'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the article, whose section structure runs Signs of readiness: calcining vitriol; Signs of progress: kindling lead; Signs of completion: imitating gold; and whose conclusion is headed reconstructing alchemical signs. The Dastin passage in the same article makes the same point directly, treating yellowness as valuable as a sign to keep working. Rampling's contribution is to stop asking whether the colours were symbolic and ask what practitioners did with them.
What it does not say
A sign read by a practitioner under dispute is not a stage in a process, and this source should not be used to restore a ladder by another name. Section-level locator only: the copy read was a text proxy without pagination.

What the disagreement shows is the exact shape of what a sign can and cannot do. Take the fortnight just gone and hand it to two readers who both know this vocabulary. The first says nigredo. This is the dissolution, it is supposed to feel like this, stay in it and do not interfere. The second looks at the same fortnight and says relapse. Intervene now, and the staying-in-it is the thing making it worse. Neither of them is inventing what they see. The fortnight happened, and both are describing it accurately. The scheme they share cannot settle which of them is right. It never settled it in the pot either.

So the arc is a settlement and not a finding. One late medieval compromise, and this video's argument is that it was promoted centuries later into THE alchemical process. As though the men in the workshops had signed something. Which leaves a question with a name attached to it. Because Jung did not merely borrow that ladder. In his own late work he distinguishes four stages, characterised by the colours melanosis, leukosis, xanthosis and iosis. And he reports that the fourfold division had a technical name of its own.27 He read further into these texts than almost anybody then alive. And he spent the second half of his working life and two enormous volumes on a sequence the tradition itself never agreed on.

270:26:50fact
Jung's own late work sets out four colour stages of the alchemical process, named from Heraclitus as melanosis, leukosis, xanthosis and iosis, and reports that the division of the process into four had a name of its own, the quartering of the philosophy.
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, Collected Works vol. 12, Part III, the section on the work and its colours
scan p. 243: 'Four stages are distinguished (fig. 114), characterized by the original colours mentioned in Heraclitus: melanosis (blackening), leukosis (whitening), xanthosis (yellowing), and iosis (reddening)'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the scanned full text of Psychology and Alchemy through Archive.org's search-inside, which returned the sentences verbatim: 'Four stages are distinguished (fig. 114), characterized by the original colours mentioned in Heraclitus: melanosis (blackening), leukosis (whitening), xanthosis (yellowing), and iosis (reddening)' and 'This division of the process into four was called the [tetramerein ten philosophian], the quartering of the philosophy.' This is Jung himself supplying the ladder the popular version inherited, in his own book, and it is four terms, not three.
What it does not say
CORRECTION FOR THE WRITERS. The formulation used in three drafted parts, that Jung 'reports that all the alchemists posited them in the beginning', is NOT in this volume: a search of the whole scanned text for the word 'posited' returns no match at all, while every other phrase searched for was found. Do not put that sentence in Jung's mouth. What he says is that four stages are distinguished and that the fourfold division had a technical name. The page numbers here are the scan's page indices (p. 243), not the Collected Works paragraph numbering, and the Greek phrase came through OCR garbled, so it must not be read aloud as transcribed.

00:27:24Part 3: The Book Nobody Was Allowed to Read

It was a November morning in 2007, and the bank on Bahnhofstrasse in Zurich was opening for the day. A red leather book came up out of the vault. It had been in a safe deposit box at the Union Bank of Switzerland for about a quarter of a century.28 The magazine account of that morning has it wrapped in black cloth, loaded into a padded suitcase on wheels, and rolled past the guards.29 Over six hundred pages, bound to order by a bookbinder called Emil Stierli, the spine lettered Liber Novus. It was published in 2009, forty-eight years after the Swiss psychiatrist who made it died. Nobody outside one family had been allowed to read it. His heirs refused scholars access to it until 2001.30

Sara Corbett, 'The Holy Grail of the Unconscious', The New York Times Magazine, published online 16 September 2009 (print issue 20 September 2009)

300:28:08fact
The private folio was the part that stayed shut: Jung's heirs denied scholars access to the book until 2001, and it was not published or shown to the public until 2009, forty-eight years after his death in 1961.
Assad Meymandi, review of Liber Novus (The Red Book) by C. G. Jung, ed. Sonu Shamdasani, Psychiatry (Edgmont) 7(10): 52-54 (2010)
review text: 'until 2001, Jung's heirs denied scholars access to the book' and 'it was not published or shown to the public, however, until 2009'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in Meymandi's review of Liber Novus in Psychiatry (Edgmont) on PubMed Central, which states that until 2001 Jung's heirs denied scholars access to the book, that it was not published or shown to the public until 2009, and that Jung lived 1875-1961. Set beside the 1944 and 1955-56 publications, this is the precise shape of the non-suppression fact: the alchemy was public, the private folio was withheld by a family, and hidden root means unread and untaught rather than concealed.
What it does not say
This is a short review in a minor journal, not an archival study, and it names no publisher and gives no account of the family's reasons. The further vault details in circulation, that the folio was moved to a Zurich bank safe-deposit box in 1984, rest on journalism that could not be opened and are not sourced here. Denied access is a description of a family's decision, not evidence that anything in the book needed hiding.

Before that lock does more work than it should, walk into any serious university library. Go to the psychology shelf. Psychology and Alchemy is on it, and Mysterium Coniunctionis is on it beside that. Both went out under his own name, in his own lifetime, at enormous length, through commercial publishers. Nothing in the alchemical work was ever hidden by anybody.31 It has stood on open shelves for decades, and anybody could take it down. What the heirs kept shut was the private record, the one hand-lettered book, and that book is now in print too. There is no conspiracy in this story at any point. So when you hear this tradition called a hidden root, hidden can only honestly mean unread. It means two long books nobody was ever stopped from opening, and nobody opens.

310:28:35fact
Jung published his alchemical work openly and under his own name: Psychology and Alchemy in 1944 and Mysterium Coniunctionis in 1955 and 1956.
Wagner, Of Books and Fire: Approaching the Alchemy of Carl Gustav Jung, PhD thesis, University of Cambridge, 2018, doi 10.17863/CAM.37801
p. 2, introduction, dating Psychology and Alchemy to 1944 and Mysterium Coniunctionis to 1955-1956
Read the passage
How it holds
Read at p. 2 of Wagner's Cambridge thesis, which dates Psychology and Alchemy to 1944 and Mysterium Coniunctionis to 1955-1956 against the corresponding Collected Works volumes 12 and 14. This is the source that kills the conspiracy framing inside the argument rather than as an aside: nothing about the alchemy was concealed, it was two of the longest books Jung ever published, and it sits on the shelf of every university library.
What it does not say
1944 and 1955-56 are the German publication dates; the English Collected Works translations came later, CW 14 in 1963, so do not date Mysterium Coniunctionis to 1963 simpliciter. The thesis text was read through a text-extraction proxy of the repository PDF, so the p. 2 locator is the thesis's own pagination as reported by that extraction and was not confirmed against a rendered page image.

For the decades it stayed shut, the fact that nobody could read it had been doing work. Critics of Jung drew the obvious inference: a man locks up what would damage him. His followers drew the opposite one, that what a family holds back must be the thing worth having. Both sides were reading the lock. Neither was reading the book.32

320:29:57fact
Liber Novus is not a diary: it is a redacted, calligraphed and illustrated literary composition in three parts, worked up out of the Black Book notebooks.
C. G. Jung, The Red Book: Liber Novus, ed. Sonu Shamdasani, trans. Kyburz, Peck & Shamdasani (W. W. Norton, 2009), Preface by Ulrich Hoerni (Foundation of the Works of C. G. Jung), dated April 2009
Preface, pp. viii-ix (OCR of the Norton edition; Shamdasani's introduction 'Liber Novus: The Red Book of C. G. Jung' begins at p. 193 and lies beyond the OCR text reachable in this fetch)
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. Shamdasani's introduction describes the work as composed in three parts - Liber Primus, Liber Secundus and the Scrutinies - built by copying the notebook material, adding a layer of commentary, and then lettering and painting it. Hanegraaff's chapter, read for Part 3, establishes the two-layer composition and the 1914 start; the three-part structure is what this source adds.
What it does not say
Recalled, not read: nothing from the introduction may be quoted and no page given. The Scrutinies were never transferred into the calligraphic volume, so 'three parts' describes the work as edited in 2009, not three finished painted sections.

When it opened, both turned out to be wrong in the same way. What was inside is a made object, an edited, hand-lettered and illustrated composition, worked up out of private notebooks over years.33 Nobody had guessed at that, on either side. And what it damaged in the received story was never a sentence inside it. What it damaged was the calendar around it.

330:30:02fact
Richard Wilhelm's German version of The Secret of the Golden Flower was first published in 1929, and both the German and the English editions carried an extensive commentary by Jung.
Thomas Cleary, The Secret of the Golden Flower: The Classic Chinese Book of Life (HarperCollins, 1991)
p. 4 of the scan, translator's introduction: 'A German version by Richard Wilhelm was first published in 1929'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read at p. 4 of Thomas Cleary's 1991 translation, in the full-text scan on the Internet Archive: a German version by Richard Wilhelm was first published in 1929, an English translation of that German rendition followed shortly after, and both editions included an extensive commentary by Jung. Cleary adds that Jung credited the text with having clarified his own work on the unconscious. This dates the public hinge of the story.
What it does not say
1929 is the publication date of Wilhelm's German version. It is not the date the manuscript reached Jung; the 1928 date for Wilhelm sending Jung the text rests on Jung's own epilogue and is sourced separately and from memory. Cleary is a translator with a polemical case against the Wilhelm and Jung edition, so use him for the bibliographic fact and not for a verdict on Jung. Page markers in the scan are OCR positions, not a printed pagination that has been checked against a physical copy.

Here is the story that the vocabulary reaches you inside of, and it deserves its best form. A doctor in serious personal crisis goes down into a tradition far older than his own discipline. He reads the alchemists, and finds that they had been tracking the exact process he was living through, centuries before him, under other names. The blackening, the whitening, the wedding of opposites. On that story the tradition is the discovery and his psychology is only the report of it. The age of the tradition is what makes the report trustworthy. It is a good story, most people who tell it tell it in good faith, and the dates do not fit it.

November 1913. The record opens with a man calling on his own neglected soul, asking where it has gone and whether it can still hear him. That call is where the deliberate self-experiment starts.34 He keeps up a series of disciplined meditations through the winter and into April 1914. The last of that material to reach the finished book is dated the nineteenth of April. From November 1913 to June 1916 he fills five notebooks with close descriptions of what he sees, and those notebooks are the raw record.35 In 1914 he starts using that material for something else. A large and carefully edited book of visions, copying the accounts over faithfully and adding commentaries of his own.3637 That is the folio that came up out of the vault.

No count of active imaginations may be given - the only figure near it in the Introduction is 'about thirty-five new sections of commentary' in the Draft, which counts something else. 360:31:54fact
From November 1913 to June 1916 Jung filled five notebooks with descriptions of his inner experiences; these are the Black Books.
Wouter J. Hanegraaff, 'The Great War of the Soul: Divine and Human Madness in Carl Gustav Jung's Liber Novus', in Greisiger, van der Haven and Schuler (eds), Religion und Wahnsinn um 1900 (Ergon, 2017), 101-135
p. 104: 'From November 1913 to June 1916, Jung filled five notebooks with meticulous descriptions of his vivid inner experiences. They are known as his Black Books'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read at p. 104 of Hanegraaff's chapter on Liber Novus in the open PDF of the Ergon volume. The sentence gives the start date, the end date and the count of notebooks, which is the whole spine of the order-of-discovery argument: the record of the experience is complete years before the alchemical programme begins.
What it does not say
The five notebooks to June 1916 are not the whole Black Books: Jung went on writing in them into the 1930s, and the published edition runs to 1932 in seven volumes. Do not say the Black Books stop in 1916. The claim that over thirty active imaginations were recorded by April 1914 is NOT supported by this passage and is not sourced anywhere on this page.
370:32:08fact
In 1914 Jung began using the notebook material as the basis for a large, carefully edited book of visions and reflections, copying the experiential accounts and adding commentaries.
Hanegraaff, 'The Great War of the Soul: Divine and Human Madness in Carl Gustav Jung's Liber Novus', in Religion und Wahnsinn um 1900 (Ergon, 2017), 101-135
p. 104: 'In 1914 he started using these materials as the basis for a large and carefully edited book of visions and reflections, faithfully copying his experiential accounts and adding commentaries'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read at p. 104 of the same chapter. It establishes that Liber Novus is a made composition built out of the diaries rather than a diary itself, and dates the start of that work to 1914, fourteen years before Wilhelm's parcel.
What it does not say
Hanegraaff's chapter as read does not say when Jung broke off the folio, so the date around 1930 is not carried by this source. The distinction the video must keep is that the Black Books are the raw record and Liber Novus is the redacted composition; conflating them is the commonest error in popular treatments.

Now the other column. In 1928 a Chinese alchemical text reaches him from a sinologist, and that text has its own story. Around 1930 he stops work on the folio, and never finishes it.38 The sustained alchemical programme begins with two lectures, in 1935 and 1936, on dream symbols of the individuation process and on notions of redemption in alchemy.39 Psychology and Alchemy follows in 1944. Mysterium Coniunctionis, the last of them, comes out in the mid-1950s.

380:32:36fact
Jung stopped work on the calligraphic folio around 1930 and never finished it.
C. G. Jung, The Red Book: Liber Novus, ed. Sonu Shamdasani (W. W. Norton, 2009): Preface by Ulrich Hoerni; with Shamdasani, Introduction p. 218

How it holds
Shamdasani's editorial introduction records that Jung abandoned work on the calligraphic volume around 1930, after the encounter with the Chinese material, and never completed it. Hanegraaff's chapter, which was read for Part 3, does not date the break, so this source is what carries the date.
What it does not say
Read: Ulrich Hoerni's Preface to the Norton 2009 edition and Shamdasani's Introduction p. 218, in the archive.org full scan. The date is Hoerni's flat '1930', not an approximation, so 'around 1930' may now be tightened to 'in 1930'. Hoerni also states that the insights 'directly informed his subsequent writings' - abandoning the folio is explicitly not abandoning the material, as the existing caveat already warned.
390:32:41fact
Jung's founding alchemical essays were the Eranos lectures of 1935 and 1936, Dream Symbols of the Individuation Process and Notions of Redemption in Alchemy.
Christopher Franklin Wagner, Of Books and Fire: Approaching the Alchemy of Carl Gustav Jung, PhD thesis, University of Cambridge, 2018, doi 10.17863/CAM.37801
abstract and p. 2: 'his founding alchemical essays of 1935 and 1936, respectively entitled "Dream Symbols of the Individuation Process" and "Notions of Redemption in Alchemy"'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in Christopher Wagner's Cambridge doctoral thesis, in the abstract on the university repository record and again at p. 2 of the thesis text, both of which name the founding alchemical essays of 1935 and 1936 under those two titles. It fixes the start of the sustained alchemical programme more than twenty years after the self-experiment began.
What it does not say
Founding is Wagner's characterisation, not a neutral bibliographic fact; Wagner's own thesis argues that Jung's engagement with alchemical literature runs back to 1911-12, so 1935 is when the programme starts, not when Jung first read an alchemist. The essays were later revised into Psychology and Alchemy, so the published texts are not identical to the lectures as delivered.

The two columns side by side leave a gap of about fifteen years. The experience is first. The framework arrives afterwards, and it arrives after the private book has already been written and abandoned.40 The sentence you will hear is that Jung got this from the alchemists. The dates will not carry that sentence. They will carry a weaker one. He recognised it in the alchemists. The first sentence says the tradition taught him something. The second says he found his own material already written down. The first is a discovery. The second is a match reported by the one man who needed a match.

400:33:07fact
Liber Novus is a layered text: a first layer of original visions, and a second layer of commentaries on those visions written afterwards.
Hanegraaff, 'The Great War of the Soul: Divine and Human Madness in Carl Gustav Jung's Liber Novus', in Religion und Wahnsinn um 1900 (Ergon, 2017), 101-135
p. 112, on the layers of the text: the assassin-in-the-desert passage in the second layer of commentaries, 'written after the summer of 1914'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read at p. 112 of Hanegraaff's chapter, which distinguishes the first layer of original visions from the second layer of commentaries written after the summer of 1914. It sets the limit on every sentence beginning what Jung saw in 1913: what survives is a redaction of that, not the thing itself.
What it does not say
The layering is a claim about the text's composition, not about the visions being invented. Hanegraaff's point is methodological. Do not use it to suggest the record is fabricated.

He states the motive himself, in the memoir Aniela Jaffe recorded near the end of his life. His own later account is that he had to find evidence that his inner experiences had been prefigured in history.41 The question he says he put to himself was this. Where have my particular premises already occurred in history. Take the premise sitting under that, because he is right about it. A private vision cannot function as evidence in a science. A psychiatrist who offers his own night visions as data has not offered data. He has offered a symptom, and he knows precisely how it will be read.

410:33:56fact
Jung states in his memoir that he had first to find evidence for the historical prefiguration of his own inner experiences, and that without such evidence he could never have substantiated his ideas, which is why the encounter with alchemy was decisive for him.
C. G. Jung, Memories, Dreams, Reflections, recorded and edited by Aniela Jaffé (Pantheon, 1963)
chapter 'The Work', the passage on finding historical precedent for his psychology
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. In the Jaffe-edited Memories, Dreams, Reflections, in the chapter on his work, Jung sets out exactly this motive: he had to ask where his own premises had already occurred in history, and the alchemical corpus supplied the historical basis he had hitherto lacked. It is the source the whole confirmation-pressure argument rests on, because it is Jung stating the motive rather than a critic imputing it.
What it does not say
Recalled, not verified against an edition: no page may be stated and the sentences must not be read aloud as a quotation. Memories, Dreams, Reflections is a Jaffe-mediated retrospective composition that Shamdasani argues is not straightforwardly Jung's autobiography, so it is admissible as Jung's own later self-presentation of his motive and not as a contemporaneous record. Having a motive to find something is not evidence that what he found is not there.

So he needs a witness, and the material he goes to is the most permissive in Europe. Latin compendia printed in the 1500s, allegorical by design, written by men who wanted to be understood by a few readers and misread by everyone else. An investigator who already knows what he needs to find is a familiar problem in any field. An investigator who already knows what he needs to find, searching a corpus built to be ambiguous, is that problem at full strength. None of which shows the readings are wrong. What it does is move the burden of proof squarely onto them.

Which brings up the fact that is in his own hand. A man spends years lettering and painting his own visions into a book, one page at a time, for no money and no reader. Then he puts it down. He picks up sixteenth-century Latin compendia instead, and writes two of the longest books of his life on those. In his own epilogue to the folio he names 1928, the year the Chinese text arrived, as the beginning of the end of it.42 What he says there is that the work had by then passed over into his living, so there was no going on with the page.43 The last page was never completed. He came back to it near the end of his life and left the sentence where it had stopped.44
