The two figures now in hand are Fischer's: 'almost 5,000 titles' for the whole library and 'over 200 works' for the digitised alchemical collection - the collection size may be stated only in those words. 'Cornerstone' is Fischer's own word, at p. 172; it must not be upgraded into a claim about which text Jung cites most often, which this article does not measure. The rest of the article (pp. 173-180) was not read.
430:35:54fact
In his own epilogue to Liber Novus Jung names 1928, the year Wilhelm sent him the text of the Golden Flower, as the beginning of the end of his work on the book, saying that its contents then found their way into actuality and he could no longer go on with it.
C. G. Jung, epilogue to Liber Novus, as quoted in Sonu Shamdasani, C. G. Jung: A Biography in Books (Norton, 2012), and in J. R. Harris & T. Woolfson (eds), The Quotable Jung (Princeton)
the epilogue quotation as printed in Shamdasani's biography (chapter on the Red Book / alchemy)
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. Jung's short epilogue to Liber Novus states that the beginning of the end came in 1928 with Wilhelm's alchemical treatise, and that he could no longer continue working on the book. This is Jung himself dating the moment the private work stopped and the historical programme took over, in his own hand, and it is the hinge of the order-of-discovery argument.
What it does not say
Recalled, not verified against the Norton facsimile, so the epilogue must not be quoted verbatim on camera and no page may be stated; the frequently cited p. 360 locator is unverified. The epilogue is Jung's retrospective account of his own motives, written later, and is evidence of what he said about the break, not an independent record of it.
440:36:14fact
The last page of the folio was never completed: Jung came back to it near the end of his life and left it where it stopped.
C. G. Jung, The Red Book: Liber Novus, ed. Sonu Shamdasani (W. W. Norton, 2009): Preface by Ulrich Hoerni; with Shamdasani, Introduction p. 221
Preface (front matter, archive.org scan leaf 8); Introduction p. 221 (leaf 251)
Read the passage
How it holds
The calligraphic volume breaks off unfinished, and the editorial apparatus of the 2009 edition records that Jung added a late note to it long after he had stopped work, without carrying the text to an ending. Part 3 uses it as the image of an abandoned private project, against the two large public volumes he did finish.
What it does not say
Read: Ulrich Hoerni's Preface and Shamdasani's Introduction p. 221 in the archive.org full scan of the Norton 2009 edition. The date 1959 and the phrase 'break off in midsentence' are now sourced and may be spoken; the earlier prohibition on saying the text breaks off mid-sentence is withdrawn. No words from the final page were read, so nothing from the last page may be quoted.

That abandonment is not a detail beside the argument. It is the argument. The tradition did not give Jung the map. He had already drawn it, in a book he kept private, and then went looking for somebody who had drawn it before him. Because a man who has had visions cannot cite himself.

A historian of Western esotericism counted the vocabulary of the Black Books. The word unconscious appears in them exactly once, and is never used at all in the commentaries. Anima, archetype, shadow, collective unconscious: completely absent.45 The document that is supposed to be the origin of the theory is written in a language the theory does not speak.

450:36:50fact
The word unconscious appears in the Black Books exactly once and is never used at all in the commentaries, and the concepts central to later Jungian psychology, the anima, the archetypes, the shadow and the collective unconscious, are completely absent from them.
Hanegraaff, 'The Great War of the Soul: Divine and Human Madness in Carl Gustav Jung's Liber Novus', in Religion und Wahnsinn um 1900 (Ergon, 2017), 101-135
p. 105: 'The very word "unconscious" appears in the Black Books just one single time, and is never used at all in the commentaries'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read at p. 105 of Hanegraaff's chapter, in two consecutive sentences, and confirmed on a second independent extraction of the same page. This is the finding that damages the tidy version most: the document supposed to ground the theory is written in a language the theory does not speak, and it comes from a historian of esotericism reading the notebooks rather than from a critic of Jung.
What it does not say
It is a lexical finding, not a refutation. Shamdasani's claim about Liber Novus is genetic and biographical, not lexical, and the absence of the later vocabulary is exactly what a later public re-description would predict. The page number differs by one between two records of this passage in this project, one giving p. 105 and one p. 106; both fall inside the chapter's pp. 101-135. The copy read is an open PDF hosted on an aggregator, not the Ergon printing.

Now the case I have just built has three good answers, and they have to be heard at full strength. The first is that the missing words prove nothing about origin. Nobody ever claimed the folio contains the terminology. The claim is genetic: the experience came first, and the public vocabulary was built afterwards to describe it in medical language. If that is true, the terminology is exactly what you would expect to find absent from the source. The counting I just used as a knife fits the position it was aimed at.

The second answer is about how Jung actually argued. The scholar who edited the folio reads the later work as one long amplification of it, and shows how little of that reached print directly. Much of it went out schematically, or by allegory and indirect allusion, and where his own material did appear he took his name off it. Three paintings out of the folio were printed in 1929 as examples of European mandalas, with nothing to say whose they were. And in 1930 he began a seminar series on another person's visions, to demonstrate that the process he had been through was not unique.46 That is the conduct of a man who understood the evidential rule perfectly well, and who kept his own case out of the witness box deliberately. What that buys him is the charge of manufacture. A man conducting a search this scrupulous is not obviously a man arranging the evidence. He may simply have recognised something and known he could not testify to it himself.

460:38:13fact
Little of the Red Book material reached print directly: Jung published it schematically, by allegory and indirect allusion, and anonymously - three of its paintings appearing in 1929 as examples of European mandalas, with nothing to say whose they were - and in 1930 he began a seminar series on another person's visions to demonstrate that the process he had been through was not unique.
C. G. Jung, The Red Book: Liber Novus, ed. Sonu Shamdasani, trans. Kyburz, Peck & Shamdasani, preface by Ulrich Hoerni (New York: W. W. Norton, 2009); Shamdasani, Introduction, 'Liber Novus: The Red Book of C. G. Jung'
Introduction, pp. 218-220 (archive.org scan of the Norton 2009 edition, item the-red-book-liber-novus-carl-gustav-jung, leaves 248-250; leaf/page mapping from the item's own _page_numbers.json, leaf 233 = p. 203)
Read the passage
How it holds
The observation is a commonplace of the post-2009 scholarship: the published psychology argues from patients' dreams, from historical texts and from third-party material, and almost never cites the private visionary record, which is precisely why the folio's publication was a shock rather than a footnote. Part 3 uses it to explain why the private book and the public books look unrelated.
What it does not say
Read: Shamdasani's Introduction, pp. 218-220, in the archive.org full scan of the Norton 2009 edition, via the item's own full-text index. Do not say Jung 'very rarely drew on' or 'never used' the material - the editor's own claim is about HOW it was used (indirect, schematic, anonymous), not about frequency. No count exists in the source and none may be given.

The third answer takes the clean 1928 watershed apart, using the dating column I just read out. The parcel arrives in 1928. The sustained alchemical work does not begin until the two lectures of 1935 and 1936, seven years later. A hinge that takes seven years to turn is not a hinge. So 1928 is not the moment a man met a tradition and changed direction, and reading it that way is the melodrama. That takes the melodrama out without touching the order. The programme still arrives after the visions, and it was still built to justify something that had already happened to him.

The field itself is still openly split about what all this makes the book. One reading has it as the centre of the whole body of work, the private original that the published psychology is a public translation of. A second refuses that framing altogether, and says the honest move is to read it with no Jungian theory in your hands at all. Read it as a document of European religious crisis around 1900. That second position is the one almost nobody who quotes the book has ever tried.

None of this settles whether the word you reach for at two in the morning is any good. A name can be accurate whoever found it, and by whatever route he got there. What it does touch is the authority that word borrows when someone tells you the tradition confirms it. That confirmation was assembled backwards, by a man with a reason to want it, out of a corpus willing to agree to almost anything. Which makes everything now depend on the witness. Before we can ask whether those texts said what he needed them to say, we have to know what they actually were. And almost nobody who uses this vocabulary has ever been told.

00:41:31Part 4: The Men Whose Language You Borrowed

Georg Honauer entered the service of Friedrich, Duke of Wurttemberg, in 1596, presenting himself as a nobleman. What he was selling was a process. Eight hundred ducats' worth of fine gold out of a hundred pounds of common iron.47 At a rate, on a schedule, and he would put his name to it.48 What they signed was seven thousand gulden in the first three months, and thirty-six thousand ducats of gold every month after that. Then the Duke supplied the iron. He emptied the armoury at Mömpelgard of every piece it held, and carted it into Stuttgart as feedstock. Honauer was given a furnace and a quota to meet. That is a signed engagement with a man's name on it, an armoury emptied against it, and a target he agreed to hit. Whatever you thought the people who wrote your vocabulary were doing, this is the paperwork they left.

470:41:34fact
Georg Honauer contracted with Friedrich, Duke of Wurttemberg, to make gold from iron, failed, was convicted both of fraudulent alchemy and of passing himself off as a nobleman, and was hanged in Stuttgart in 1597 on a gilded iron gallows, with the execution publicised on printed broadsheets.
'Execution of an Alchemist' (1597), broadside/engraving, Science History Institute Digital Collections; corroborated by 'Hinrichtung des Georg Honauer 1597', Deutsches Apotheken-Museum im Heidelberger Schloss, museum-digital:baden-wuerttemberg object 752 (https://bawue.museum-digital.de/object/752)
Science History Institute catalogue record for the 1597 broadside (object m6b4hrj); museum-digital record 752
Read the passage
Tara Nummedal, Alchemy and Authority in the Holy Roman Empire (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2007)
p. 156 (printed) and fig. 12 caption at p. 157, chapter 6, "Betrüger on Trial"
print copy
How it holds
Recalled, not read. Nummedal's Alchemy and Authority in the Holy Roman Empire reconstructs the Honauer case from the Stuttgart records as the type case of the alchemist as contracted employee: a promise, a court patron, a quota, a failure, a criminal trial on two counts, and a punishment staged as public theatre using the iron he had been given. Part 4 needs it because it replaces the mystic-in-a-cellar picture with an employment contract.
What it does not say
Two things this does not license. First, Honauer's AGE — nothing in Nummedal; "twenty-four" stays out of the script. Second, "robes woven with gold brocade" — she has only "a golden garment," and only on the say-so of the woodcut's printer, alongside the gilding, the over-a-ton weight and the three thousand gulden cost. Those four details are a printer's claim on a commemorative broadsheet, i.e. the same commercial pamphlet culture the video is otherwise sceptical of; if the script states them flatly it is repeating publicity. The thirty-foot height and the iron's provenance are Nummedal's own and may be spoken plainly. "Taken out through the city" is also not in her account.

p. 155 (printed), chapter 6, "Betrüger on Trial," section "The Princes' Perspective"; source note at p. 228 n. 23
print copy
Tara Nummedal, Alchemy and Authority in the Holy Roman Empire (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2007)
p. 155 (printed), chapter 6, "Betrüger on Trial"; supply list at p. 115, chapter 4
print copy
How it holds
Nummedal gives exactly the figure: eight hundred ducats of "fine gold" from one zentner of iron, and she herself glosses the zentner as "one hundred pounds." She adds that Honauer said he could produce even more if the iron came from Hungary, and that he described the process as working "like smelting ... but in this case one separates Mars and Sun [i.e., iron and gold]."
What it does not say
Bars the tonnage. "Thirty-six hundredweight and eighteen pounds," and "nearly two tons," are not in Nummedal — her only itemised iron for Honauer is seven zentner (about 327 kg) in the 1597 supply order, and the shipment she describes from Mömpelgard is unquantified because the document is lost. The provenance clause ("from the ducal armoury at Mömpelgard") is fully hers; the weight is not, and must not be spoken on this book. Watch the spelling too: she prints "Mömppelgart" here and "Mömpelgard" on p. 90.

There are two pictures of those men available, and both feel reasonable before you look. In one, they are robed adepts who never meant metal at all, writing about the soul in a code of furnaces. In the other, they are credulous fools, boiling and burning for a prize that physics forbids. The archives support neither, and the reason is documentary rather than charitable. The reason is that these men kept accounts, and the accounts survive.

Start with the word, because the word is where the mistake was installed. There was no broadly accepted, sharp line between alchemy and chemistry until the eighteenth century.49 Seventeenth-century practitioners used the terms as synonyms. The two words named one and the same body of activities. The division arrives later, and when it arrives it sorts by reputation.50 The discredited material keeps the name alchemy, and everything still working takes the other name. Historians then read that boundary backwards into the sources, and it cut straight through single careers and single books. The older historiography described alchemy as a mystic science and a pathology of thought. It called alchemy the greatest obstacle to the development of rational chemistry.51 Every one of those phrases is a verdict pretending to be a description.52

490:43:23fact
There was no broadly accepted, sharp demarcation between alchemy and chemistry until the eighteenth century.
William R. Newman, 'What Have We Learned from the Recent Historiography of Alchemy?', Isis 102(2) (2011), 313-321
opening section, on the demarcation question
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the full text of Newman's Isis survey at journals.uchicago.edu. Newman writes that one thing learned in the last twenty years of scholarship is that no broadly accepted, sharp demarcation between alchemy and chemistry existed until the eighteenth century, and he refers the reader to his own etymological study with Principe for the justification of the actors' term chymistry. This is the historian's own statement of the thesis, not a summary of it.
What it does not say
It says the DEMARCATION was not broadly accepted; it does not say seventeenth-century writers had no disputes about whether transmutation was achievable. Newman's own point elsewhere in the piece is that the dispute was internal to one field. It also does not say the eighteenth-century split was a conscious professional manoeuvre; that reading is the video's, and the article does not supply a motive.
500:43:30fact
The division of alchemy from chemistry arrives in the eighteenth century and sorts by reputation: the domain of 'alchemy' was for the first time widely restricted to gold making and to the cheating practices of charlatans, while chemical practitioners legitimised their own discipline by divorcing themselves sharply from a tradition that had fallen into disrepute.
William R. Newman and Lawrence M. Principe, 'Some Problems with the Historiography of Alchemy', in William R. Newman and Anthony Grafton (eds), Secrets of Nature: Astrology and Alchemy in Early Modern Europe (Cambridge, MA: MIT Press, 2001), 385-431
pp. 386-387, section 'The Eighteenth-Century View of Alchemy'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the OPEN archive.org scan of the whole volume (opensource collection, unrestricted), at printed pp. 386-387 under the section heading 'The Eighteenth-Century View of Alchemy'. Newman and Principe show that the split is a professional manoeuvre of the early eighteenth century, not a description of what the actors were doing: the words had named one body of activities through most of the seventeenth century, and the new boundary sorted the discredited material under 'alchemy'. This is the sentence Part 4 voices when it says the division arrives later and sorts by reputation.
What it does not say
The five-item list of the field's branches (assaying, distilling, pharmacy, pigment-making, transmutation) that this source used to carry is NOT in this chapter and has been removed from the claim; the script does not voice it. The 1998 'Alchemy vs. Chemistry' article in Early Science and Medicine is a DIFFERENT paper and was not opened - brill.com returns 403 - so nothing may be cited to it. The chapter gives a motive for the split (status), which is more than Part 4's Isis source gives; voice the motive on this source, not on that one.

520:44:16fact
The corpuscular matter theory alchemists used held that particles are bonded in a very strong composition and that juxtaposed particles can be separated and recombined without losing their being, an operational atomism already present in the Summa perfectionis attributed to Geber.
William R. Newman, 'What Have We Learned from the Recent Historiography of Alchemy?', Isis 102(2) (2011), 313-321
section on chymical atomism and the Summa perfectionis
Read the passage
How it holds
Newman's full text describes robust intermediate constituents combined in a fortissima compositio, particles that can be separated and recombined without losing their being, and robust corpuscles that defied the corruption required by Thomistic theory; he locates this operational atomism, based on the resistance of materials to sublimation and calcination, already in the Summa perfectionis of Geber.
What it does not say
The identification of the Summa's author as Paul of Taranto is Newman's own proposal elsewhere and is contested; the passage read here says Geber. Do not present Paul of Taranto as settled authorship.

Now the physics. On the matter theory these men worked with, metals are not permanent kinds. They are compounds, generated underground from two principles later identified with sulphur and mercury. The metals differ from one another only in the proportion, the purity and the fixedness of those two. Transmutation, on that theory, means rectifying a metal's mercury and its sulphur and changing their relative quantities.53 And proportion and purity are exactly what calcination, sublimation and cupellation already alter, every working day, in front of you. Underground, nature ripens base ores toward noble ones, and they put that ripening at thousands of years.54 They did not claim to be copying nature's own principles. On their own account they could not. So the philosophers' stone is a medicine rather than a talisman. It strips off what has attached to a metal accidentally, and leaves the sulphur and mercury the metal is radically made of. The alchemist is applying the physics of his century, not defying it.

530:44:31fact
On the matter theory alchemists worked with, metals were held to be composed of two principles later identified with sulphur and mercury, and transmutation was conceived as rectifying a metal's mercury and sulphur and changing their relative quantities.
William R. Newman, 'What Have We Learned from the Recent Historiography of Alchemy?', Isis 102(2) (2011), 313-321
section on matter theory and the two principles
Read the passage
How it holds
Newman's full text states that metals are composed of two principles later identified with sulfur and mercury, and describes the transmutational programme as rectifying their mercury and sulfur and changing their relative quantities. This is the article's own account of why transmutation was a metallurgical target rather than a miracle: if metals differ by proportion of the same two principles, then the operations that alter proportion are the operations that would transmute.
What it does not say
Newman describes what the theory held; he does not endorse it, and he does not say the alchemists were therefore right. The article does not contain the further claims the video may want - that ores ripen in the earth, or that the philosophers' stone is best described as a reagent. Neither is in the passage read; do not source them here.
540:44:47fact
On the period's matter theory metals ripen underground, nature turning base ores toward noble ones, so the art only accelerates what nature does slowly, and the philosophers' stone is an agent of purification and fixation rather than a talisman.
Geber [pseudo-Geber], The Works of Geber, Englished by Richard Russell (London, 1678) - the Summa perfectionis in its 1678 English translation
Book I: ch. 'Of Things Perfecting and Corrupting Metallick Bodies'; 'A Confutation of the Reasons of Men simply denying Art'; 'Of Preparation and Melioration of Bodies in General' (OCR of the Early English Books scan)
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. The maturation model and the sulphur-mercury theory that carries it are set out in Newman's edition of the Summa perfectionis of pseudo-Geber, where the stone is a medicine or reagent that perfects imperfect metal by removing its impurities and fixing it. Part 4 needs it because it makes transmutation a reasonable inference from the theory rather than a wish.
What it does not say
Recalled: no page of the edition or its introduction was opened, so nothing may be quoted. This source exists because the Isis survey read for Part 4 does NOT contain these claims and must not be made to carry them, as Part 4's caveat already warns. 'Reagent' is a modern gloss on a period concept; do not present it as the alchemists' own word.

The chymists reasoned from mass balance, and they stated the principle plainly. Nothing comes into being from nothing. So if a body gains weight, that weight came out of another body weighing just as much.55 And here is a separate argument they ran in the vessel. Dissolve silver in a strong acid until it vanishes as a body, add what brings it back down, and you recover silver.56 The form of the silver was never destroyed, and that is only possible if parts of it persisted, hidden, through the change. That was worked out on a bench by people whose job description included making gold.

550:45:43fact
Chymists reasoned from mass balance, stating the principle that nothing comes into being from nothing and that weight therefore comes from another body weighing just as much.
William R. Newman, 'What Have We Learned from the Recent Historiography of Alchemy?', Isis 102(2) (2011), 313-321
section on mass balance and chymical atomism
Read the passage
How it holds
The mass-balance formulation is quoted inside Newman's full text as the chymists' own reasoning, in the passage arguing that alchemical practice supplied one of the foundations of later chemistry. Read at journals.uchicago.edu.
What it does not say
This is a principle stated in period sources and reported by Newman; it is not a measured result and Newman does not claim the chymists quantified it to modern tolerances. 'Among the first to enunciate' is Newman's historical priority claim about a discipline, not a demonstration that any particular experiment was accurate.
560:45:52fact
Dissolving silver in acid until it disappears as a body and then precipitating it back out at the same weight was run as an experimental argument that the silver's corpuscles had survived the dissolution.
'Daniel Sennert's Corpuscularian Reforms to Natural Philosophy', Histories 3:3 (2023), article 17 (MDPI, open access; author name not established from the text reached - cite by title and journal)
Section 5, 'The Development of Sennert's Matter Theory'
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. Daniel Sennert's reduction-to-the-pristine-state experiments, discussed at length in Newman's Atoms and Alchemy, use exactly this procedure as evidence for the persistence of atoms through chemical change, against the Aristotelian account of substantial change. It is Part 4's cleanest example of chymistry reasoning experimentally.
What it does not say
Recalled, and deliberately not attached to the Isis survey: Part 4's caveat records that Newman's text as read does NOT contain the Sennert weighing experiment, and it must not be put in that article's mouth. No page of Sennert or of Atoms and Alchemy was opened, so no quotation and no weight figure may be given, and 'the same weight' must be voiced as the claim the argument turned on rather than as a measurement anyone reported to a decimal.

Then the imagery, and here the correction is sharper. The green lions and the doves and the wedding are a secrecy system with working parts. Secrecy in a craft world where knowledge had cash value was ordinary professional practice. A cover-name has a determinate referent, and the referent varies by author, which is why Newton compiled concordances of the vocabulary. He drafted his Index chemicus four times over, with two supplements, and the final version runs to almost a hundred folios.57 Venus is usually copper, but in one branch of the literature it can stand for silver. Mars is usually iron. But to Newton, and to the author he read most closely, it can also mean a male sulphurous seed. That seed is found in iron, and in the regulus of antimony.58 And the cover-names were only one tool among several. There was syncope, silently leaving out steps and materials. There was parathesis, padding a recipe with terms and operations it does not need. And there was dispersion of knowledge, deliberately dividing a process and scattering it across different parts of a text, or into entirely different books.59 The result is that no single volume holds a procedure you could run. And the glass itself is part of the technique. The hermetic vessel is sealed. It works because it is closed: nothing escapes, and the work depends on holding what is inside under pressure for as long as the thing needs.

570:46:51fact
Newton spent decades compiling concordances of alchemical vocabulary because the same cover-name meant different things in different authors: he drafted his Index chemicus four times over, plus two supplements, and the final version swelled to almost a hundred folios.

How it holds
Newton's Index chemicus, a large alphabetical index of alchemical terms with references to the authors who use them, is the object Newman's Newton the Alchemist reconstructs as the core of Newton's reading practice: the decipherment problem was real, and Newton attacked it lexicographically. Part 4 uses it as the evidence that the Decknamen had referents worth reconstructing. Newton the Alchemist has now been read from the owner's copy and supplies the two figures the script states: four drafts plus two supplements, and almost a hundred folios in the final version.
What it does not say
Newman gives a folio extent and a count of drafts. He gives no count of entries, no count of authorities indexed and no dated span of compilation, so only the two figures he gives may be spoken. “Decades” remains a loose characterisation of the compilation period (he writes at p. 91 only that Newton compiled it “over a number of years”) and must not be sharpened into dates.
580:47:16fact
A cover-name has a determinate referent that varies by author: in the Chymistry of Isaac Newton Project's glossary, Venus is usually copper but in Philalethan chymistry can also refer to silver as a Deckname, and Mars is usually iron but to Newton, George Starkey and others in the tradition of Alexander von Suchten can also mean the male sulfurous seed found in iron and, by extension, in the martial regulus of antimony.
The Chymistry of Isaac Newton Project (Indiana University Digital Library, ed. William R. Newman), Alchemical Glossary
entries s.v. Venus and s.v. Mars
Read the passage
How it holds
Both glossary entries were read verbatim in the Indiana University scholarly edition's Alchemical Glossary. They are the concrete demonstration that these are code terms with right answers, decided by author and tradition, rather than free-floating symbols.
What it does not say
The glossary is a scholarly edition's reference apparatus; it records the editors' decodings, not a proof that every image in every text decodes. Note also that the variability cuts both ways: a term with several referents is exactly what makes the corpus hard to run.
590:47:37fact
Alchemical secrecy was a toolkit, not just cover-names: it included syncope, leaving out steps and materials; parathesis, adding unnecessary terms and processes; and dispersion of knowledge, dividing up processes and distributing them over different parts of a text or even into entirely different texts.
William R. Newman, 'William R. Newman on Newton the Alchemist', author essay, Princeton University Press
paragraph on Decknamen, syncope, parathesis and dispersion of knowledge
Read the passage
How it holds
Read verbatim in Newman's Princeton author essay, which lists syncope, parathesis and dispersion of knowledge with exactly these glosses, and adds that the bulk of Newton's reading notes consist of attempts to arrive at the correct meaning of terms, since the same term often meant different things to different authors - his Index chemicus listing multiple meanings for the term Green Lion, each linked to a specific writer.
What it does not say
This describes a literary practice of concealment. It does not by itself show that every obscure passage decodes, and Newman does not claim that. The inference the video wants - that no single book holds a runnable procedure - is Newman's description of the dispersion technique, not a survey result about the whole corpus.

The difference between a code and a symbol is that a code can be wrong. Melt antimony ore with iron, let the metal sink under a thick floating slag, and cool it slowly. The surface of the solid button sets into a radiating star.60 One reading takes that star as something surfacing in the work, a sign that the operator has been met halfway.61 The other takes it as a crystal habit produced by a controlled cooling rate. On that reading the star arrives only when the slow cooling under the slag has gone right, so its absence tells the operator something. Both readings explain the star. Only one of them tells you in advance when it will appear, and when the same materials will fail to produce it. Lawrence Principe's stated position is that this secret language can be deciphered. Sometimes it decodes into impressive feats of chemical experiment, which can then be run again in a modern laboratory. That is a test the spiritual reading cannot sit for.62

600:48:22fact
The chymists' reduction-to-the-pristine-state argument turned on the observation that metals can be incinerated, dissolved and sublimed but can always be reduced back into the original metal.
William R. Newman, 'What Have We Learned from the Recent Historiography of Alchemy?', Isis 102(2) (2011), 313-321
section on reduction to the pristine state
Read the passage
How it holds
Newman's full text gives this as the experimental argument for the persistence of a substance through change: decompose the compound, recover the ingredient, and the ingredient is shown to have survived inside it. He presents it as the ancestor of the chemical analysis/synthesis criterion.
What it does not say
The passage read reports the reasoning, not a specific quantitative trial. Newman's text as read does NOT contain the often-repeated Sennert story of silver dissolved in aqua fortis and precipitated back at the same weight; Sennert appears in connection with mineral acids generally. Do not put the weighing experiment in this article's mouth.

The Princeton University Library 'Philosophers' Glass' exhibition page, which two research files cite for the Starkey/Newton reproduction and Principe's reconstruction, was re-fetched and contains none of that material - do not cite it for the star.
620:49:18fact
Lawrence Principe's stated position is that the obscure secret language of code and metaphor routinely used by alchemists to hide their knowledge can be deciphered, sometimes into impressive feats of chemical experimentalism, and even replicated in a modern laboratory.
Lawrence M. Principe, 'The Secrets of Alchemy', Distillations (Science History Institute), 30 January 2013
paragraph describing what the book shows about alchemists' secret language
Read the passage
How it holds
Read verbatim in Principe's own Distillations piece for the Science History Institute, 30 January 2013, where he describes what The Secrets of Alchemy sets out to show. It is the clearest first-person statement of the decode-and-run programme available without the book.
What it does not say
This is Principe describing his own book in a magazine, not a demonstration and not peer review. The article as read does NOT describe any specific process he replicated, so it cannot support 'Principe grew the philosophers' tree' or any named reconstruction. Voice it as a claim Principe makes, and source the replications themselves elsewhere or not at all.

Which brings up the man everyone reaches for. You have probably heard that Isaac Newton wrote more words on alchemy than he wrote on physics. Here is what is actually under that. In 1936 Sotheby's catalogued three hundred and twenty-nine lots of Newton's manuscripts, and over a third were filled with content that was undeniably alchemical.63 He wrote and transcribed about a million words of it in his own hand, on one institution's estimate. Both verbs are load-bearing. Much of that million is Newton copying out other men's books, because copying was how you got a text you could not buy.64 So the figure measures how much alchemy passed through his hand, not how much of it he thought up. Nobody has kept the scoreboard the popular claim implies, and the honest version is smaller and stranger. The man who wrote the Principia spent a large part of his working life reading and recopying alchemical books. Keynes bought a share of the papers, and wrote the verdict everyone still quotes. It was written for the Royal Society's Newton tercentenary in 1946, and read out after his own death.65 Newton was not the first of the age of reason, he wrote, but the last of the magicians, the last of the Babylonians and Sumerians. That verdict is worth dating precisely.66 It is an economist's reaction to unedited papers, written before anyone had identified the tradition Newton was reading. Nobody yet knew that the author who most shaped him, Eirenaeus Philalethes, was the pen-name of a colonial chymist called George Starkey.67 The modern verdict from the manuscripts is flatter and harder. Newton did not pursue the stone for God, and not for physics either. He practised alchemy as an alchemist: as much literary decipherment of riddles as testing in the laboratory.686970 And in 1689 Parliament repealed the medieval felony against multiplying gold and silver.

700:51:49fact
The English statute of 1689 that repealed the medieval prohibition on alchemy is titled an Act to repeal the statute made in the fifth year of King Henry the Fourth against the multiplying of gold and silver, and its surviving operative clause provides that no mine of copper, tin, iron or lead shall be adjudged a royal mine although gold or silver may be extracted out of it.
Royal Mines Act 1688, 1 Will. & Mar. c. 30 (England, 1688/89)
long title and the operative section on mines of copper, tin, iron or lead
Read the passage
How it holds

The repealing Act is the only verified anchor in this project for the 1404 Act Against Multipliers: the later statute names the earlier one, its regnal year and its subject, which fixes the existence and date of the prohibition independently of any secondary account.
What it does not say
This establishes that Parliament legislated against multiplication and later repealed it. It does NOT establish the 1404 Act's own wording - that text was not obtained and must not be quoted - and it does not establish that Parliament believed transmutation worked. 'The law is evidence of plausibility' is the video's reading, not a historian's finding, and must be voiced as a reading.

So back to Stuttgart, and the iron stacked in the yard. The months passed, and the quota came round, and came round again, and nothing that left Honauer's furnace was gold. He failed. He fled, and was extradited, and was put back in front of the furnace, and still nothing came out of it. On the second of April 1597 he was hanged. The gallows stood thirty feet high in the Schlossplatz, built out of the very iron the Duke had given him. The printer of the broadsheet said it was gilded, and that Honauer went to it in a golden garment. They hanged him in a golden garment on a gallows made from his own feedstock, painted it gold, and sold the picture on broadsheets. That is the sociology of the people whose words you have been using for your inner life.

Now press the case that has just been built, because it has a real weakness and the field knows it. The programme selects. It works on the texts that can be decoded and run, and generalises from them over a much larger corpus. That corpus also contains visionary treatises, emblem books and devotional writing with no recoverable procedure in them at all. And the religious language is not packaging. The senior Renaissance scholar Brian Vickers made the charge concrete, over a passage of Paracelsus. His accusation is that the new historiography quotes selectively, dropping the religious frame from the end so that what is left reads like early natural science.7172 Vickers is a hostile party in this argument rather than a referee, and Newman published a reply. The charge still has to be answered. The specialist who works these archives has a different shape for it. Tara Nummedal puts these people at the intersection of faith, commerce and knowledge, all three at once. Some of them worked at the fire to understand God through his work in nature. What alchemy was FOR was exactly what they argued about, and the argument split them. Which reprices what you inherited. The spiritual reading you were handed is not a pure misreading of a lab notebook. Some of these men really did think the operation was religious. And that still does not give you the schedule back. A man who thought the blackening was a spiritual event had no more idea than you do how long it lasts. There is an error available in the other direction, and it is the same error. To claim that alchemy was really religion is to claim you know what it was underneath. To claim it was really science is that identical claim with the sign flipped.

720:53:37fact
Tara Nummedal's settlement of the alchemy-and-religion question is that the two interpenetrated one another throughout the medieval and early modern periods, so that religious language in alchemical texts was never merely decorative.
Tara E. Nummedal, 'Alchemy and Religion in Christian Europe', Ambix 60:4 (2013), 311-322, doi 10.1179/0002698013Z.00000000036 (guest editor's introduction to the special issue)
introduction, opening argument on alchemy and Christianity
Read the passage
Tara Nummedal, Alchemy and Authority in the Holy Roman Empire (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2007)
p. 9 (printed), Introduction, section "Alchemy in Practice and the Problem of Fraud"
print copy
How it holds
Recalled, not read. Nummedal's contribution to the Ambix issue on alchemy and religion argues against both reductions - alchemy as disguised mysticism and alchemy as proto-chemistry with religious ornament - and locates the interest in the fact that the two were not separable categories for the people doing the work. Part 4 uses her as the field's own position, and the value of the citation is that she reaches it without discussing Jung at all.
What it does not say
Does not license "alchemy and religion were not two categories that could be pulled apart," and does not license "the prayer at the end of the recipe is not a frame around the chemistry" as her position. Nummedal's book argues the opposite shape: whether alchemy had to include a spiritual project was the live dispute of the period, and by the late sixteenth century the prophetic strand was receding while the entrepreneurial and metallurgical strand advanced. Her fourfold terminology (medica, transmutatoria, technica, mystica, p. 15) is borrowed from Müller-Jahncke and Paulus and is presented as a way of holding a diverse field together, not as a claim that religion was constitutive of every practitioner's work. If the script wants the strong constitutive claim, it needs the Ambix editorial actually opened, not this book.

You already know the other half of this from earlier, and it applies here too. Two competent readers standing over the same yellow could not agree whether they were watching a stage or a counterfeit. That disagreement is not a failure of your reading. It is in the material.

A man under contract, with real technique and a false matter theory, is most of what the archive holds. It is not all of it. Nothing in a distillation requires a king in a bath, or a corpse, or a wedding, and those are on the same pages as the assays.

00:56:12Part 5: Why Kings, Why a Corpse, Why a Wedding

So why does the literature read like that. These were men under contract, with furnaces and balances and assays, working to a theory of matter they could state. And the books they left describe a king who walks into a bath and drowns. A corpse going black in a sealed glass. A wedding between a sun and a moon, and a child born out of the two dead bodies. Nothing in a distillation dictates a king. Nothing in a distillation dictates a marriage.

The obvious answer is secrecy, and it is a good answer. A man who has spent years learning to prepare one mercury does not print the recipe for his competitors. So he writes in cover-names, and the trade knows that the green lion is one particular reagent. But that explains one thing and leaves another untouched. It explains why the language is disguised. It does not explain why this disguise. A code needs opaque tokens, and any tokens will do. These men chose bridegrooms, widows, severed heads, and a child.

That is the gap Jung walks into, and his answer is worth building properly, because the popular version of it is not the one he defended. It runs in four steps. The first is that there is a part of the mind that makes images the ego did not author. The evidence is ordinary. You did not write last night's dream, you did not choose its cast, and you could not have predicted it at bedtime. Something in you composed it and did not consult you.

The second step is the one everything turns on, and it has a name in his work: projection. The claim is that this unauthored material does not stay inside. It attaches to whatever in the world is dark enough to take it. And then it is felt as a quality of that thing rather than as your own material. Jung's sentence about the man at the furnace is the compact version. He experienced his projection as a property of matter, but what he was in reality experiencing was his own unconscious. And a content that has moved out there is out of your reach. You cannot argue with it, because as far as you are concerned it sits in him, or in her, or in the country.

The third step is historical. For centuries these men worked on materials whose actual constitution nobody could know. No oxygen, no atomic theory, no way to analyse a substance into elements. One white powder could be told from another only by watching how it behaved. Matter, for them, was a genuine darkness. And they did not glance at it. They sat with it at a fire for weeks, alone, with everything they had staked on the outcome. So the fourth step follows. The sealed vessel was the largest projection surface a European mind had available: unknown, attended to for years, and loaded with hope.

Then the underdetermination premise. The imagery the corpus produced is not derivable from the chemistry it describes. The royal marriage, the putrefaction, the dismemberment, the raven, the child. None of that is entailed by heating a substance until it changes colour. So the images came from the operator. His thesis is that the alchemists filled the darkness of matter with contents of their own psyche. Then they read those contents back off the glass as properties of the substance. Which makes the corpus an involuntary record of an inner process, mistaken for a record of matter.73

731:00:22fact
Jung's thesis is that the alchemists, unable to know the constitution of the substances they worked on, unconsciously filled that darkness with contents of their own psyche and experienced them as properties of the matter, so that the alchemical corpus is an involuntary record of psychic process mistaken for a record of matter.
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, Collected Works vol. 12, trans. R. F. C. Hull (Bollingen/Princeton)
the passage on the projection of psychic contents into matter (quoted by Edinger, von Franz, Wehr and others from CW 12)
Read the passage
How it holds
From memory: this is the governing thesis of Psychology and Alchemy, argued at length in Part III, 'Religious Ideas in Alchemy', and restated across the alchemical volumes. Its structural consequence is the one the video must state clearly: because it is a PROJECTION thesis, it requires that the alchemists sincerely believed they were working on matter. A knowing spiritual allegory would refute it, not support it - which is why the documented spiritual-alchemical current is a rival to Jung rather than an ally.
What it does not say
Recalled, not read against a copy: the WebFetch route into the Collected Works scan truncates well before Part III, so no paragraph number and no wording are in hand. Do not quote Jung on projection, and do not attach a paragraph number. The second clause - that projection requires sincerity - is a logical point about the concept, not a sentence of Jung's; voice it as an inference.

Now the correction. Jung is not saying the alchemists were secretly doing psychology. He is saying the opposite, and he needs the opposite. Projection only works if the man at the fire sincerely believed he was working on lead. The moment he knows the marriage is a picture of his own soul, it becomes an allegory he wrote on purpose. A tradition of knowing spiritual metaphor would refute this thesis rather than confirm it.

The second argument is larger, and it starts from a question about Christendom. In the doctrine that ruled the Latin West, light and good stand on one side, darkness and evil on the other. Jung's complaint is that the opposition is simply left in open conflict. Christ represents good, and his counterpart the devil represents evil.74 The godhead is masculine, and he is explicit that the special veneration given to the mother of God is not a real equivalent.75 And salvation happens to spirit, not to matter. So a great deal has been shut out of the official picture, and the question is where it went.

741:01:25quotation
Jung's charge of one-sidedness against Latin Christendom is stated in his own words: “The opposition between light and good on the one hand and darkness and evil on the other is left in a state of open conflict, since Christ simply represents good, and his counterpart the devil, evil.”
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, Collected Works vol. 12, trans. R. F. C. Hull (Bollingen/Princeton)
Part I, Introduction, para. 22
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the Collected Works vol. 12 full text, Introduction, paragraph 22. It is the premise the compensation argument needs: a dominant collective attitude that excludes a determinate set of terms, named by Jung rather than supplied by the video.
What it does not say
This is Jung's characterisation of Christian doctrine, not a historian of Christianity's. It is a premise of his argument and should be presented as such, not as an agreed fact about medieval theology. The passage also does not mention alchemy; the bridge from this exclusion to alchemy's content is a separate move.
751:01:39quotation
Jung states the second exclusion in his own words: “The Trinity is therefore a decidedly masculine deity, of which the androgyny of Christ and the special position and veneration accorded to the Mother of God are not the real equivalent.”
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, Collected Works vol. 12, trans. R. F. C. Hull (Bollingen/Princeton)
Part I, Introduction, para. 25
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the Collected Works vol. 12 full text, Introduction, paragraph 25 - three paragraphs before the undercurrent sentence, so the two sit in one argumentative run: the excluded terms are named, then alchemy is proposed as where they surfaced.
What it does not say
Jung is arguing that the Marian cult does not compensate the masculinity of the Trinity; that is a contested theological judgement and the passage offers no evidence for it beyond assertion. Do not present it as a fact about Christian doctrine.

His principle is that what consciousness shuts out comes back from below, and that this holds for a culture as much as for a person. The counter-position has to surface somewhere, usually somewhere with no status. And there, for centuries, tolerated at the edge of the same Christian world, is a practice about matter. It begins in blackness and rot. It is presided over by the moon and by a female wisdom. It produces a son of the cosmos out of the base and rejected stuff. The excluded half, item by item. Which is where Jung puts it as an analogy. Alchemy runs under Christianity as an undercurrent, and it stands to that surface as the dream stands to waking consciousness. It fills in the gaps the Christian tension of opposites left open.76 The analogy and the mechanism are both in there. Dreams compensate the one-sidedness of the waking mind, and alchemy did that for a civilisation.

761:02:50quotation
Jung's own formulation of the compensation argument is: “The point is that alchemy is rather like an undercurrent to the Christianity that ruled on the surface. It is to this surface as the dream is to consciousness, and just as the dream compensates the conflicts of the conscious mind, so alchemy endeavours to fill in the gaps left open by the Christian tension of opposites.”
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, Collected Works vol. 12, trans. R. F. C. Hull (Bollingen/Princeton)
Part I, Introduction, para. 26
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in a full-text scan of the Collected Works volume 12 held at the Internet Archive, at numbered paragraph 26 of the Introduction. The two sentences carry both halves of the argument at once: the analogy (alchemy is to Christianity as the dream is to consciousness) and the mechanism (compensation). Earlier readings took this only from an unauthorised aggregator scan; it is now read in a copy of the Collected Works itself.
What it does not say
The copy read is an OCR text derivative of a scanned Collected Works volume, so the printing and the printed page number are not confirmed; the locator is the volume's own paragraph number. Note also what the sentence does not say: it asserts a compensatory relation, it does not demonstrate one, and the analogy's base term - that dreams compensate waking consciousness - is the part dream research has actually tested.

An analogy borrows its credit from the term you already accept. Here the term you are asked to accept is the dream half. And that half is the one piece of this entire structure a laboratory can get at, because dream reports can be collected and coded and counted. They have been. The psychologist William Domhoff has spent a career counting what is actually in dream reports. His verdict is flat. There is no systematic evidence for the compensatory function, and continuity with waking life is the usual finding across a wide range of studies.77 What people dream about is what they were already doing and fearing and wanting.78 Not the counterweight. The same thing. And when a Jungian prediction was made specific it came back empty. Coded for archetypal symbolism across a major life transition, the dreams showed no difference at all, and we will take that study apart later.79

771:03:39fact
Domhoff reports that the continuity between dream content and waking life is one of the most striking findings from the content-analysis studies of Calvin S. Hall and his co-workers.
G. William Domhoff, 'Moving Dream Theory Beyond Freud and Jung' (2000), dreams.ucsc.edu (University of California, Santa Cruz)

The frequently quoted Hall & Nordby sentence about wishes and fears determining dreams is NOT on the Domhoff pages read and must not be voiced.

The base term of Jung's compensation analogy is the one part of it dream research has measured, and it came out the other way: there is no systematic evidence for the compensatory function, and continuity with waking life is the usual finding across a wide range of studies.

How it holds
Read on G. William Domhoff's teaching page at the University of California, Santa Cruz. Under the heading 'Problems with the theory' the first bullet states that there is no systematic evidence for the compensatory function and that continuity with waking life is the usual finding in a wide range of studies. This bears directly on Jung's cleanest alchemy sentence: the analogy is only as good as its base term, and the base term is the leg that has been tested.
What it does not say
This is a summary judgement in a scholar's lecture notes, not a meta-analysis, and Domhoff is a partisan of the continuity hypothesis he is contrasting. It says the compensatory function lacks systematic support, not that dreams never compensate anything. And it is a finding about DREAMS; it does not directly test the claim about alchemy, it removes the analogy's support.
791:04:09fact
A Jungian developmental prediction that was operationalised and coded by a Jungian expert came back null: among women who were still menstruating, perimenopausal and postmenopausal, the codings showed no difference in the degree of symbolic expression across the three samples.
G. William Domhoff, 'Moving Dream Theory Beyond Freud and Jung' (2000), dreams.ucsc.edu (University of California, Santa Cruz)
section reporting the Jungian expert's coding of the menopause dream samples
Read the passage
How it holds
Read verbatim in Domhoff's 'Moving Dream Theory Beyond Freud and Jung'. The passage records thirty-two women still menstruating (average age 39.6), twenty-four perimenopausal (49.2) and twenty postmenopausal (58.6), and states that contrary to expectations the codings by the Jungian expert did not show any difference in the degree of symbolic expression in the three samples. The coder was sympathetic rather than hostile, which is what makes it a fair test.
What it does not say
A null result in one small three-group comparison, reported second-hand by Domhoff, who does not name the original investigators in the passage read. Say 'Domhoff reports'. A null is not disproof of compensation; it is the absence of an effect where the theory predicted one. Do not state the group sizes as a statistic on the strength of this source alone.

Alchemy is to Christianity as the dream is to waking life. One of those two relations has been measured. It is not there.

So the compensation argument is carried entirely by the leg nobody can weigh. And the projection argument is under attack at its third step, from the historians you met in the last part. The cover-names had determinate technical referents. The men using them had a working theory of matter, with tests inside it. If that is right, the darkness Jung needs was not dark in the way he needed. Where a decoded operation explains an image, projection is not required to explain it.80

801:05:03quotation
Jung states the projection mechanism generally in the same volume: “When an unconscious content is replaced by a projected image to that extent, it is cut off from all participation in and influence on the conscious mind.”
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, Collected Works vol. 12, trans. R. F. C. Hull (Bollingen/Princeton)
Part I, Introduction, para. 12
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the Collected Works vol. 12 full text, Introduction, paragraph 12. In the same paragraph Jung writes that where all the emphasis is on the outward figure - a more or less complete projection - the archetype is identical with externalized ideas but remains unconscious as a psychic factor. This is Jung's own statement of the mechanism the alchemical thesis then applies to matter.
What it does not say
The paragraph is about RELIGIOUS projection onto an outward figure, not about the retort. It supplies the mechanism, not the application. The step from 'projection fills a vacuum' to 'the alchemist's flask was a maximal projection surface' is not in the passage read and is sourced separately, from memory.

Take the hardest case for Jung. A mercury and silver amalgam, left sealed in a glass, puts out a growth of solid metal. Starkey wrote that it grows in the forme of a tree, with sprigs and branches, and that it is bright as burning silver. The name it carried was the Arbor Dianae, the tree of Diana, and the word for what it did was vegetation.81 Lawrence Principe has grown one. The plate in his book is captioned Philosophers' Tree, grown in the philosophical egg. The short trunk and the spread of branches are easily visible, and Starkey's own comparison of the thing to coral holds up in the photograph. On the facing plate sits the star regulus of antimony from the last part, prepared by him as well.82 He reached the tree by deciphering Starkey's cover-names. The recipe says to take our fiery dragon that hides the magical steel in its belly, and nine parts of our magnet. The underdetermination premise has to be read against that flask. The image is not hanging on an indifferent precipitate. The image is in the vessel, growing. A man who writes that a tree has sprouted in his glass is reporting what happened.

811:05:21fact
A mercury and silver amalgam, left sealed in a glass, puts out a growth of solid metal that the alchemists called the tree of silver, or Arbor Dianae, and described as vegetating; Starkey wrote that it grows in the forme of a tree, with sprigs and branches, and that it is bright as burning silver.
Jennifer L. Whyte, 'The roots of the silver tree: Boyle, alchemy, and teleology', Studies in History and Philosophy of Science Part A 85 (2021), 185-191 - author's accepted preprint (preprint pagination 1-32)
Preprint pp. 17-18, the section identifying Boyle's silver plant with Starkey's Philosophical Mercury

How it holds
The arbor Dianae is one of the standard reconstructions in Principe's laboratory work: a silver amalgam deposit that grows visibly as a branching tree in the flask, and it makes intelligible why practitioners wrote of metallic vegetation and of a dead body revived. Part 5 uses it to show that the vitalist language tracks something the practitioner actually saw.

The published ScienceDirect text was not opened - Elsevier returns 403 to every route tried - so the preprint and the published version have NOT been compared line by line.

Lawrence M. Principe, The Secrets of Alchemy (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2013)

Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the OPEN archive.org scan of the 2013 Chicago edition (identifier the-secrets-of-alchemy, opensource collection): the colour plate section is bound between printed pp. 90 and 91, and Plate 5 carries the caption verbatim. Part 5 needs it because it is the photograph of the thing: the underdetermination premise has to be read against a flask with a tree growing in it, not against an indifferent precipitate.
What it does not say
The plate section is unpaginated; 'p. 90' in earlier drafts of this entry was an approximation. The insert sits immediately after printed p. 90 (scan pages 101-108 of the archive.org item, with printed p. 90 at scan page 100 and p. 91 at scan page 109). Plate 3 (stibnite, the golden glass of antimony, and the star regulus, all prepared by the author) is on scan page 102 and Plate 5 on scan page 103, so they face each other but are NOT on the same page - do not say 'on the same page'. The decipherment of Starkey's recipe at pp. 161-163 is a separate matter and is not sourced here.

Jung has a reply, and the cost of it is visible. He can say that watching a metal vegetate and calling it a resurrection is more than seeing. The branching is in the flask. The resurrection is not. And the same reply generalises to the whole historians' case. Every cover-name can be granted its referent. That still does not explain why the referents were carried by bridegrooms and corpses and drowned kings. A code needs arbitrary tokens, and these are not arbitrary.

That reply holds. But listen to what happened to the conclusion while it was being defended. It began as the claim that the alchemical corpus is a record of the psyche. That is the claim which licenses reading those books as a map of what is happening in you. It has ended as the claim that the psyche shaped the choice of metaphor. The second claim is almost certainly true, and it is much cheaper. Metaphors come from somewhere, and the somewhere is people. It gives you no map.

One strand inside analytical psychology has largely stopped defending the historical claim, and its position deserves a hearing. It says, as its defenders describe it, that the point was never that alchemy was psychology underneath. It is about what alchemical language can do. Say putrefaction. Something is in the room. Say depressive episode instead, and it goes. That is what the language does that the concepts cannot: it holds the image instead of translating it. That concedes the historian everything and keeps the practice. It also quietly gives up what you were promised, because a language that is good at holding images is not evidence about you.

Which brings back the black crust. The blackening was a film of sulphides on a bronze platform inside a sealed pot, and hearing that felt like a demotion of your word. But the earliest alchemist we can read at any length describes that operation twice. Once as the vapour condensing on the metal. Once as a dream in which a man tears him apart, cuts off his head, and burns his flesh and bones in a transformative fire.83 Same man, same process, both pages. And the text never says which one is the metaphor. That refusal is not a puzzle the historians solved or Jung solved. It is the actual object both of them were handed.

831:09:16fact
The earliest alchemist who survives at length describes the same operation twice: once as a chemical blackening on the apparatus, and once as a dream in which a priest is torn apart, beheaded and burned, and the text does not mark either description as the metaphor.
Joshua Werrett, The Visions of Zosimos of Panopolis: Text, Translation, and Commentary, DPhil thesis, University of Oxford, 2024, doi 10.5287/ora-gab6xyvky
summary of the first vision (dismemberment and burning of the priest) and the thesis abstract's description of the text as a generic blend
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in Werrett's 2024 Oxford edition of the Visions of Zosimos. The commentary reconstructs the chemistry of the blackening on the kerotakis (see Part 2), and the summary of the vision reads: 'He says that a man tore him apart, cut off his head, mashed up his flesh and bones, and burned them in a transformative fire, such that he was reborn as a spirit.' The thesis characterises the text as 'a generic blend - part technical manual, part apocalyptic revelation, part Hermetic treatise, part philosophical allegory', which is exactly the refusal to separate the two registers that Part 5 turns on.
What it does not say
The dismembered figure in the vision is the priest of the sanctuary; Ion is named in the text but the identification of the dismembered man with Ion across the whole vision cycle is an editorial question, so do not build on the name. That the text does not mark either description as the metaphor is the thesis's framing of a generic blend, not a sentence in Zosimos; voice it as the modern editor's reading. The PDF was read through a text proxy, so the locator is section-level.

Without the compensation argument, the edifice rests on one thing, and Jung knew it better than his critics did. It rests on people. On the claim that men and women who never opened these texts, you among them, produce these images anyway, in the same order. He thought he had that case. He thought he had it from a man who could not possibly have known.

01:10:27Part 6: The Man Who Could Not Have Known

A young man of excellent scientific education comes into analysis in serious personal trouble. That is all the volume says about him. Trained in the natural sciences, formidably clever, and coming apart. Jung does not take him on. The dreams are written down over roughly ten months, and not under Jung's supervision. More than a thousand of them. Jung works through the first four hundred of them.84

841:10:52fact
The dream series in Part II of Psychology and Alchemy came to over a thousand dreams and visual impressions from a young man of scientific education; Jung worked on the first four hundred, covering nearly ten months, and he did not observe the case himself but handed it to one of his pupils, a woman doctor who was then a beginner.
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, Collected Works vol. 12, Part II, 'Individual Dream Symbolism in Relation to Alchemy', introductory section
scan pp. 72 and 125: 'over a thousand dreams and visual impressions'; 'the first four hundred dreams and visions, which covered a period of nearly ten months'; 'I asked one of my pupils, a woman doctor, who was then a beginner'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the scanned full text of Psychology and Alchemy through Archive.org's search-inside, which returned the sentences verbatim: 'The material consists of over a thousand dreams and visual impressions coming from a young man of excellent scientific education'; 'For the purposes of this study I have worked on the first four hundred dreams and visions, which covered a period of nearly ten months'; 'I asked one of my pupils, a woman doctor, who was then a beginner, to undertake the observation of the process'; and, later, 'That was why I even refrained from observing this particular case myself and entrusted the task to a beginner who was not handicapped by my knowledge.' This source replaces the figures that Part 6's caveat forbade, and it does it out of Jung's own text.
What it does not say
The design point is Jung's own and it cuts both ways: an observer chosen for ignorance cannot contaminate the record, and also cannot notice what a trained observer would. 'A woman doctor, then a beginner' is what the text says; the identification of the observer, and of the dreamer, is not in the volume and must not be asserted from it. The page numbers are the scan's indices (pp. 72 and 125), not Collected Works paragraph numbers, and the text was read as OCR through Archive.org's search-inside rather than off a printed page.

Here is what turns up in that record. A circle with a square inside it. A ring of four things, arranged so that the fourth completes something the other three could not. A snake. A house with a room in it the dreamer cannot get into. A woman who is not him and who knows the way. And a stone, which in the dreams is worth more than it should be.

Then Jung goes to his shelves. The books there are Latin compendia of the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries, printed in Basel and Strasbourg and Frankfurt. They are anthologies of alchemical treatises, printed in Latin for specialists.85 And this is where he says the two records meet. What follows is a handful of examples and not an inventory. The mandala, an image built around a centre. The quaternity, a four where three would not finish the job. The raw matter of the work, described as a hidden treasure, a treasure hard to attain. The next pair you would not find matched to any dream. He takes them from his survey of the alchemical writings. Mercurius, a paradoxical double substance, whose parallel is the serpent. Sapientia, wisdom in the shape of a woman. And the stone, which in one of these texts is identified with Christ. None of that is one dream matched to one picture.86 The claim is that a whole vocabulary turns up twice. They sit in the same functional places, doing the same jobs, in roughly the same order.

The alchemical parallels Jung sets beside the modern dream series in Psychology and Alchemy run across Parts II and III: the mandala built around a centre, the quaternity and the raw matter as a 'treasure hard to attain' from the dream commentary, and then the Mercurial serpent and Sapientia taken from his survey of the alchemical writings, with the stone identified in one of those texts with Christ.
