These are the recurring terms of Parts II and III of Psychology and Alchemy, where each dream motif is set beside an alchemical parallel drawn from the Latin anthologies. Part 6 needs the list only to make the matching concrete, so a listener can hear what 'the same image' is being claimed about.
What it does not say
Read: CW 12 itself, in the public archive.org scan, at the places listed - the squaring-of-the-circle passages, the quaternity passages, the 'treasure hard to attain' passages, the Mercurial serpent passage and the 'Aurora consurgens'/Sapientia section heading. Rothgeb's Abstracts digest was NOT used as the source and is now only a lead; nothing in this source rests on an abstract. Page numbers given are scan-page positions in that archive.org copy, not verified printed CW pagination, so cite by Part and motif on camera, not by page. No claim is made that any particular dream was matched to any particular motif.

The book he builds out of this is shaped like its own argument. Psychology and Alchemy comes in three parts. An introduction on the religious and psychological problems the material raises. Then a long central section on the modern dream series, run to its endpoint.87 Then a section on the alchemical material, laid out on its own terms and arriving at the same forms. Two series, gathered separately, meeting in the middle of one volume. Jung states the design point up front. The dreams were recorded outside his supervision, by a man with no idea what the symbols in them meant.88 Several hundred of them came in before there was any personal contact between the two of them. Only the last forty-five dreams happened otherwise. That way the record could not be shaped by whatever he was hoping to find.

871:13:07fact
The form of Psychology and Alchemy is itself the convergence argument: the volume is built in three parts, an introduction to the religious and psychological problems of alchemy, then Individual Dream Symbolism in Relation to Alchemy, then Religious Ideas in Alchemy.
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, Collected Works vol. 12, trans. R. F. C. Hull (Bollingen/Princeton)
table of contents, Parts I-III
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the table of contents of a full-text scan of Collected Works vol. 12. The middle part runs a modern dream series; the third assembles the alchemical material on its own terms. Two bodies of material, gathered by different means, presented separately and arriving at the same forms - the book's architecture stages the argument rather than merely stating it.
What it does not say
The table of contents establishes the architecture, nothing more. It does not establish that the two bodies of material were in fact gathered independently, and it does not establish that the correspondences hold; both are the claims the architecture is designed to make felt. The selection and the interpretation of both series were done by the same man.
881:13:33fact
Jung's stated design point for the dream series in Part II is that the dreams were recorded by an observer other than himself, so that the record would not be shaped by the analyst's own preoccupations.
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, CW 12, trans. R. F. C. Hull; and C. G. Jung, The Integration of the Personality (1940), the earlier English presentation of the same dream series
CW 12, the statement introducing the dream material; Integration of the Personality, the corresponding statement on the dreamer's contact with Jung
Read the passage
How it holds
From memory: in the opening of Part II of Psychology and Alchemy Jung explains that he did not conduct the analysis and that the material was set down by a third party, precisely to keep the record unprejudiced. This is the closest thing in his corpus to a control on analyst contamination and it is what gives the convergence argument whatever independence it has.
What it does not say
Recalled, not read: the fetch route into the Collected Works scan truncates before Part II, so no paragraph number is in hand and no wording may be quoted. Note also the exact scope of the safeguard - it governs the RECORDING, not the selection of which dreams to present or their interpretation, both of which Jung did himself. Do not state the number of dreams, the number examined, or the length of the period on the strength of this source: those are figures and a figure from memory is a guess.

Now the argument, with its joints showing. First premise: there is a determinate body of motifs in that Latin corpus. Those anthologies carry dates and places. Basel, 1593. Strasbourg, 1602. Frankfurt, 1678. Latin, addressed to specialists, and never put into a modern language. Not what a young physicist keeps on his shelf. Second: a modern individual with no access to it produced those motifs anyway, spontaneously, in the same positions and roughly the same sequence. Third: the ordinary explanations are blocked. It is not in his biography, because the material is not the stuff of a physicist's life. It is not cultural transmission, because the texts were in Latin and addressed to specialists.89 Fourth premise, and this is the one that does the lifting. Two series with no causal contact between them nevertheless match. When the match is not accidental, the best explanation is a common generator. Something in the manufacture of images, shared, producing equivalent pictures under equivalent conditions.

891:14:48statistic
The cultural-transmission rival to the archetype now has experimental support: in a reaction-time task, culturally representative archetypal images were processed significantly faster (mean 2,199.7 ms) than non-representative ones (mean 2,735.7 ms).
Wang W., Shen H., Zhang X. & Wen L., 'The silent influence: experimental validation of cultural unconscious and its psychological impact through Chinese archetypal imagery', Frontiers in Psychology 16 (2025), doi 10.3389/fpsyg.2025.1657224
Study 1, results: mean reaction times for representative versus non-representative images
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the full article at Frontiers in Psychology. Wang, Shen, Zhang and Wen run three studies testing Henderson's 'cultural unconscious': a reaction-time task, a supraliminal priming study with a situational choice task in which the experimental group chose options aligned with the images' symbolic meanings significantly more often than controls, and a subliminal priming word-choice task with the same direction of result. The premise Jung needs - that cultural transmission cannot explain the convergence - now has a measured rival mechanism standing against it.
What it does not say
These are reaction times in milliseconds on a categorisation task, not a measure of anything unconscious or archetypal in itself; the interpretation as a 'cultural unconscious' is the authors' framing. The stimuli are Chinese cultural imagery and the participants are Chinese, so the study shows a within-culture familiarity effect and does not test cross-cultural universality either way. Do not say it proves the cultural explanation; say it gives that explanation an experiment where it previously had only an assertion.

Understand what that argument is for, because the word you use in the bad months hangs off it. Without it, the claim that the alchemists were describing psychological processes is just a way of reading old books. It has no evidential standing at all. Any sufficiently rich text maps onto any sufficiently rich scheme, and a corpus of cookbooks read that way would yield archetypes too. With it, Jung is not interpreting. He is arguing to the best explanation from data he did not manufacture. That is the difference between a hobby and a finding, and it is why an attacker should come here first.

So come here first. Four pressures, in ascending order of how much they cost.

The first is scope. The safeguard is real, and it covers exactly one thing: the recording. It does not cover the selection, and it does not cover the interpretation. Both of those were done by the man whose thesis the material confirms.90 Four hundred dreams went in. Only some of them reach the page, and the choosing was his. The number of people in this study is one.

901:16:31fact
The same paper's priming studies found that participants exposed to culturally archetypal images chose options aligned with those images' symbolic meanings significantly more often than unexposed controls, both when the images were shown above and below the threshold of awareness.
Wang W., Shen H., Zhang X. & Wen L., 'The silent influence: experimental validation of cultural unconscious and its psychological impact through Chinese archetypal imagery', Frontiers in Psychology 16 (2025), doi 10.3389/fpsyg.2025.1657224
Studies 2 and 3, results sections on supraliminal and subliminal priming
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the full article. Study 2 combined supraliminal priming with a situational multiple-choice task and found the experimental group consistently reported significantly higher frequencies of choosing options aligned with the symbolic meanings of the images; Study 3 used subliminal priming in a word-choice task and found the experimental group significantly more likely to choose responses consistent with the symbolic meanings than a control group not exposed to the priming stimuli.
What it does not say
Priming effects on a forced-choice task in a laboratory. They show that culturally learned symbolism can bias choice without the chooser noticing - which is a mechanism for images arriving uninvited and feeling older than oneself. They do not show that Jung's patients were primed, and they say nothing about whether the alchemical motifs specifically are culturally transmitted. Priming research also has a contested replication record; do not present a single 2025 paper as settled.

The second is the dreamer. The book does not name him. The man is widely held to have been Wolfgang Pauli, one of the great physicists of the century, though that identification is reported rather than documented. Take it as given for a moment and see what it does. Pauli was a European intellectual of extraordinary reading outside physics.91 In a letter to Emma Jung in November 1950 he writes about the Grail legend, Merlin, and the three spindles of fate. In another he is on Plato and Plotinus, and the doctrine that evil is only a privation of good. He later wrote a long essay called The Influence of Archetypal Ideas on the Scientific Theories of Kepler. It is about the archetypal images organising Kepler's astronomy, and about Kepler's quarrel with the alchemist Robert Fludd.92 That breadth of reading is itself an open channel. And a plate series of European mandala pictures went into print with Jung's own commentary, in German in 1929 and in English in 1931. That is before the dream series was written up.93 The phrase that gives the whole case its force, that he had no exposure to the tradition, cannot be sustained.

911:17:00fact
Pauli read exceptionally widely outside physics, ranging over the history of ideas, philosophy, religion, alchemy and Jung's psychology, which is why he is a bad candidate for a mind uncontaminated by symbolic material.
Harald Atmanspacher and Hans Primas, 'The Hidden Side of Wolfgang Pauli: An Eminent Physicist's Extraordinary Encounter with Depth Psychology', Journal of Consciousness Studies 3:2 (1996), 112-126

Part 6 needs it as the transmission-channel objection to the CW 12 case.
What it does not say
Does not license the word 'mysticism' as a description of Pauli's reading. In this volume 'mysticism' appears as one pole of a pair of opposites he sets AGAINST science in his own function-schema list (p. 86: 'Mysticism-Science'), and as a problem he wants to think about (Letter 72, p. 157, on 'the relationship between science and mysticism'); Zabriskie quotes him hoping for 'a synthesis embracing both rational understanding and the mystical experience of unity' (p. xlvi). He engaged the category; the book does not show him reading mystics. Safest: say 'history of ideas, religion and alchemy' and drop 'mysticism', or say he wrote about the relation of science and mysticism.

Wolfgang Pauli's own published contribution alongside Jung is a long essay on Kepler, arguing about the archetypal images organising his astronomy and about his polemic with the alchemist Robert Fludd.

Pauli's essay examines the polemic between Kepler and Fludd and the irreconcilability of their premises. Part 6 needs it because the physicist in this story wrote history of science, not physics, when he wrote with Jung.

The essay is an argument about Kepler's images, and it is contested history of science; do not report Pauli as having demonstrated anything about archetypes. Nothing in it identifies Pauli as the dreamer of the CW 12 series, and this source must not be used to support that identification, which the script voices as reported testimony. 931:18:00fact
Mandala images were already in print and circulating in Europe before the dream series, in Jung's own circle and in theosophical literature.
Richard Wilhelm (trans.) and C. G. Jung, The Secret of the Golden Flower: A Chinese Book of Life, English trans. Cary F. Baynes (London: Kegan Paul, Trench, Trubner & Co., 1931)
title page and publication statement; table of contents, sections "Examples of European Mandalas" and "Description of the Plates", pp. 137-138
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. Jung's own commentary on The Secret of the Golden Flower, published in 1929 with European plates, is the obvious channel, and mandala imagery from Tibetan and Indian sources had been reproduced in theosophical publishing well before that. Part 6 needs it as the ordinary-transmission alternative to a collective source.
What it does not say
Recalled and NOT verified: no plate, no publication and no circulation figure was checked, and the further claim in the draft that the recorder of the dream series belonged to that circle is unsupported and must be cut, not hedged. Availability in print is not evidence that this dreamer saw any of it; the argument here is that the channel was open, which is all the script may say.

The third pressure is sameness, and it is subtler. A claim that the same image arose in two separated people is empty until somebody says when two images count as the same. A snake in a dream, and a serpent in a woodcut. A loose reading makes those one image, and the correspondences multiply. A tight reading makes them two, and most of the correspondences fall away. The whole result turns on that judgement, and the judgement was never spelled out. It has never been written down as a rule, handed to a stranger, and applied blind to material they had not seen labelled.

Which brings the fourth pressure. This one comes out of a laboratory. The standing rival explanation for anything a modern patient produces is cryptomnesia: material you encountered, forgot that you encountered, and later generate as your own.94 For a century that has been an accusation with nothing behind it. It now has a laboratory. Healthy undergraduates, tested one at a time, took turns with a computer partner finding words in a letter grid. Asked afterwards to write down only the words they had found themselves, they handed back their partner's words as their own.95 They were not copying, and they were not lying. Nobody had told them their memory would be tested, and they believed the words were theirs. That is Marsh and Bower's finding, from 1993, and it is the shape of a real effect rather than a possibility. So the accusation now has a rate attached to it.96 When the test came at the end of the session, thirty-two percent of the words people claimed as their own were their partner's. Asked straight away instead, seven and a half percent.97 The rate moves lawfully with how long ago they saw the material, and with how deeply they processed it at the time. Then somebody asked what it feels like from inside. People were less sure of the answers they had stolen than of the ones they had thought of. Less sure, and still sure. Some of the stolen answers were given with high certainty all the same.

C. G. Jung, General Bibliography of C. G. Jung's Writings, Collected Works vol. 19, Bollingen Series XX (Princeton University Press)

951:19:34fact
It now has a laboratory. Stanford undergraduates, tested one at a time, took turns with a computer partner finding words in a letter grid; when they were later asked to write down only the words they themselves had found, they handed back their partner's words as their own. They were not copying, and they were not lying: they had not been told anyone would ask, and they believed the words were theirs.
Richard L. Marsh and Gordon H. Bower, 'Eliciting Cryptomnesia: Unconscious Plagiarism in a Puzzle Task', Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition 19(3) (1993), 673-688
Experiment 1, Method ("Subjects", "Procedure"), printed pp. 674-675; design rationale p. 674
print copy
How it holds
The design is NOT a group. Sixty-two Stanford University undergraduates took part; 42 in the main conditions, and "All subjects were tested individually" (p. 674). Each subject alternated turns with a "computer partner" on 4 x 4 Boggle-style letter grids, the computer supplying three words for each one the subject found, twelve computer words to four subject words per puzzle (p. 675). The two test phases are called the recall-own task (type the four words you found in this puzzle) and the generate-new task, Marsh and Bower's relabelling of Brown and Murphy's recall-new (p. 674 n. 1, p. 675). Unintentionality is established two ways: delayed subjects were not forewarned that memory would be tested, and the authors treat the confidence data as proof - "We take this high confidence as additional evidence that these plagiarisms were true cryptomnesia in which subjects wrongly believed that they were generating items unique to them in the current context" (p. 678). The GROUP round-robin with three other people, which the current script describes, is Brown and Murphy 1989, summarised in this paper's introduction on p. 673 - a different study by different authors.
What it does not say
Do not say "in a group", "their neighbours", or "producing answers in a room together" on this source: Marsh and Bower's subjects were tested individually and their partner was a computer program. The authors say so explicitly in the General Discussion (p. 685): "Our paradigm, however, differed by not involving social interaction among the participants." A live-group version exists, but it is Brown and Murphy (1989), which this paper only summarises - citing it for a group design would be citing a source through a secondary description, which house rules forbid. Also do not say the plagiarism was proved unconscious in every case; the authors concede on p. 685 that complete item forgetting plus random reselection "cannot entirely" be ruled out.
961:19:56quotation
"It now has a laboratory. In a puzzle task, healthy volunteers reproduce other people's contributions as their own. The rate at which they do it moves lawfully with how long ago they saw the material, and with how deeply they processed it at the time. That is Marsh and Bower's finding, from 1993, and it is the shape of a real effect rather than a possibility."
Richard L. Marsh and Gordon H. Bower, 'Eliciting Cryptomnesia: Unconscious Plagiarism in a Puzzle Task', Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition 19(3) (1993), 673-688
p. 673 (abstract); Experiment 1 method p. 674 and results/summary pp. 676-678; Experiments 2a/2b pp. 679-682
print copy
How it holds
Both manipulations are in THIS paper, not in later Marsh/Landau/Hicks work. Experiment 1 manipulated retention interval and interfering material (immediate vs. delayed test, the delay being roughly 20 min of work on the other puzzles): delay raised recall-own plagiarism from 7.5% to 31.8% and generate-new partner plagiarism from 17.5% to 28.1%. Experiments 2a/2b manipulated degree (depth) of encoding via levels-of-processing orienting tasks: a superficial physical judgement (word four letters or longer) produced 11.3% plagiarism during generation versus 3.5% after a deep semantic judgement, F(1, 30) = 4.24, p < .05, and 19.1% vs. 8.2% in the generate-new task. Direction: more delay = more plagiarism; shallower encoding = more plagiarism.
What it does not say
It does not license saying the two variables were cleanly isolated. The authors themselves write that "delay, interfering material, or both are substantial contributors" (p. 677); the delayed group also learned incidentally while the immediate group knew it would be tested, which they flag on p. 676 as further obscuring the comparison. The encoding effect is also NOT general across tasks: in the recall-own task the levels-of-processing manipulation "did not reliably affect cryptomnesia", F(1, 30) = 1.64, p > .10 (p. 679). The delay is about twenty minutes inside one session, not days or years, so "how long ago they saw the material" must not be stretched to a lifetime. Do not say the rate is "lawful" in the sense of a fitted function - no dose-response curve is reported, only two levels per factor.
971:20:13fact
So the accusation now has a rate attached to it. In Marsh and Bower's puzzle experiment, when the test came at the end of the session rather than straight away, almost a third of the words people claimed as their own recollections - thirty-two per cent - were in fact their partner's, against seven and a half per cent when they were asked immediately. Nobody has ever estimated that rate for this dreamer.
Richard L. Marsh and Gordon H. Bower, 'Eliciting Cryptomnesia: Unconscious Plagiarism in a Puzzle Task', Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition 19(3) (1993), 673-688
Experiment 1, Results and Discussion, "Recall-own task", printed p. 676 (baseline comparison also p. 676)
print copy
How it holds
The single most quotable headline figure: in the recall-own task of Experiment 1, delayed-test subjects reproduced their partner's words as their own on 31.8% of responses (SEM = 2.3), against 7.5% (SEM = 2.2) for subjects tested immediately, F(1, 40) = 57.1, p < .001. The comparison baselines named on the same page are Brown and Murphy's 3.9% for semantic category generation and their adopted control repetition rate of 1.6%. During initial generation the rates were 10.3% (immediate) and 12.2% (delayed); in the generate-new task, partner plagiarism was 17.5% immediate and 28.1% delayed (p. 677).
What it does not say
This does not license "about 32 per cent of responses" as a general plagiarism rate. It is one task (recall-own), one condition (delayed), in Experiment 1; the immediate condition gives 7.5% and initial generation about 10-12%. It is also not a rate for spontaneous plagiarism in life: the material was seen minutes earlier, inside the same session, and subjects were REQUIRED to produce four words per puzzle, which forces guessing. Do not attribute the 31.8% to a social group - see the paradigm target. And do not call 3.9% or 1.6% "the human baseline": both are Brown and Murphy's figures for a different task, quoted here for comparison.

Nobody has ever estimated that rate for this dreamer. He could not have known is not a finding. It is an entitlement.

And there is a second common generator on the table now, which there was not in his day. Recent experimental work, which I will develop later, shows that installed cultural images work on people who never studied them. You have never studied them either.98 That is a channel he never had to rule out.

981:21:11fact
The same experimental literature finds that people are often confident about material they have in fact plagiarised unconsciously.
Serge Brédart, James M. Lampinen & Anne-Marie Defeldre, 'Phenomenal characteristics of cryptomnesia', Memory 11(1) (2003), 1–11; author manuscript deposited in ORBi, Université de Liège, handle 2268/1525 (file 'Phenomenal5.1.doc')
Experiment 1, results on confidence ratings; Experiment 2, memory-characteristics questionnaire results
Read the passage
Richard L. Marsh and Gordon H. Bower, 'Eliciting Cryptomnesia: Unconscious Plagiarism in a Puzzle Task', Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition 19(3) (1993), 673-688
Experiment 1, "Confidence ratings" and Table 1, printed p. 678; replicated in Experiment 2a, Table 2, p. 681
print copy
How it holds
From memory: Bredart, Lampinen and Defeldre studied the phenomenal characteristics of cryptomnesia and reported that although confidence was lower for plagiarised than for correct responses, participants nonetheless gave high certainty ratings to a large proportion of their plagiarised answers. That matters for Jung's cases because the patient's own conviction that the image was new is treated as evidence, and here conviction and plagiarism co-occur.
What it does not say
The figures are task- and condition-specific and must not be merged into one number: 40% is recall-own plagiarisms pooled, 60-80% is the generate-new task, and "positive or somewhat confident" lumps the top two rungs of a three-point scale together - only 12-16% of plagiarisms were rated flat-out "definitely sure". The scale is a 3-point self-report, not a calibrated probability judgement. Nothing here concerns dreams, historical cases, or knowledge the person could not have had; it is confidence in a word found in a letter grid minutes earlier. If the script keeps its present wording it needs no number at all - this source simply makes the sentence stand on a source that was opened.

Jung's own flagship instance of the impossibility of transmission was not this dream series. It was a patient at the Burghölzli. He told Jung to look up at the sun and move his head, and said he would then see the sun's phallus.99 Its swinging made the wind. An ancient Mithraic liturgy has something very like it, except that there the thing hangs from the disc of the sun like a pipe. That case has been fought over for thirty years. Richard Noll went after the date. Jung's own editors put a footnote in the Collected Works. It concedes that the 1910 edition he cited was the second, and that a first had appeared in 1903. The same footnote adds that the patient had been committed some years before that. Noll's reply is that a German reader did not need the scholarly edition, because the same material was circulating in widely available Theosophical books.100 And his sharpest question is about Jung. Jung kept telling the original story, in print and on air, after he had learned the date was wrong. Sonu Shamdasani answered him at book length four years later, and the quarrel has not closed.101 I am reporting a dispute, not settling one. But notice its shape. It is the one instance where the channel could actually be checked, and checking it did not go well.

991:21:34fact
Jung's flagship case for the impossibility of transmission was a Burghoelzli patient who saw a tube hanging from the sun, which Jung matched to a description in an ancient Mithraic liturgy.

How it holds
The solar phallus case is the single example Jung returned to for decades as proof that an uneducated psychotic patient produced an image that could only have come from a collective source, matched to Dieterich's edition of the so-called Mithras Liturgy. Part 6 needs it because everything about the cryptomnesia objection turns on this one case.

Shamdasani's own position is that the cult thesis is erroneous, not that the question is undecidable.

The most interesting response to all this comes from inside analytical psychology rather than from Jung's enemies. A historicising turn has been under way there for some years now. On that reading, the alchemical thesis is no longer defended as history. His reading is being reconstructed instead. Which volumes he bought, when he opened them, which preface he was following in which year. It converts the sentence Jung discovered the historical counterpart of his psychology into a different sentence.102 He went looking for a tradition that would answer his self-experiment, and he found the one he was already carrying.

1021:23:37fact
Jungian scholarship has largely turned from defending the historical thesis to reconstructing which alchemical volumes Jung bought, read and annotated, and when.
Christopher F. Wagner, Of Books and Fire: Approaching the Alchemy of Carl Gustav Jung, PhD thesis, University of Cambridge, 2018 (DOI 10.17863/CAM.37801)
Acknowledgements p. xv; Introduction pp. 1-3

How it holds
Recalled, not read. Haaning's article on Jung's quest for the Aurora consurgens and Wagner's Cambridge thesis are the type cases: both work on the books, the acquisitions and the reading chronology rather than on whether the alchemists were unconsciously doing psychology. Part 6 uses the shift as evidence about the state of the field.
What it does not say
Recalled: neither piece was opened; Part 3 rest on the parts of Wagner that were read, and this claim is not among them. 'Largely turned' is an impression of a literature, not a bibliometric finding, and must be voiced as such. Do not say Jungians have abandoned the historical thesis.

Which leaves a question about where his confidence came from. Because he did not walk into that Latin corpus cold, and neither did anybody else. The corpus he walked into was itself the end of a translation chain, and that chain has a first date in it. The date at the top of that chain is the next thing standing behind his confidence.

01:24:30Part 7: Does It Count That It Is Old

11 February 1144. That date stands in the last line of a short Latin book, and it marks a change in what Europe owned.103 The book is a dialogue. A king puts the questions, a Byzantine hermit answers them, and the subject is the stone and how it is made.104 It was translated out of Arabic. Before it, alchemy was almost completely unknown in Latin Europe. A few craft recipes had come down, one of them for Spanish gold, alchemical in the strict sense. Everything the West does with this material afterwards is downstream of that translation and the ones that followed it within a generation.105

1031:24:34fact
The Latin explicit is quoted verbatim: Explicit Liber Alchemiae de arabico in latinum translatus, anno millesimo centesimo octuagesimo secundo, in mense Februarii et in eius die undecimo - the eleventh day of February, year 1182 of the Spanish era, i.e. 1144 of the Christian era.
Marion Dapsens, De la Risalat Maryanus au De Compositione alchemiae. Quelques reflexions sur la tradition d'un traite d'alchimie, Studia graeco-arabica 6 (2016), 121-140
p. 128, n. 42 (the explicit of the Latin translation); conclusions at pp. 134, 136

Part 7 needs the frame because the first alchemical book in Latin is a conversation between a Muslim prince and a Christian monk, which is the chain in one image.

1051:25:02fact
The first Latin translation of an Arabic alchemical work is dated in its own preface to February 1144, and before it Latin Europe had no alchemical literature of its own.
Marion Dapsens, "De la Risalat Maryanus au De Compositione alchemiae. Quelques reflexions sur la tradition d'un traite d'alchimie", Studia graeco-arabica 6 (2016), 121-140
pp. 122, 128 (with Sebastien Moureau, Micrologus 28 (2020), pp. 103-104, 116, as the qualifying source)
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. The Liber de compositione alchemiae carries a translator's preface dating the work to 1144, and it is standardly treated as the beginning of Latin alchemy: there is no Latin alchemical corpus before the translation movement out of Arabic. Part 7 needs the date because the whole chain argument depends on Latin Europe receiving alchemy rather than inventing it.
What it does not say
Recalled: neither the preface nor Moureau's study of it was opened, so the exact day may not be pressed, no wording may be quoted, and the translator's name should not be asserted. The dating is the text's own claim about itself, which is evidence of what the translator wanted believed as much as of when the work was done; recent scholarship disputes both the attribution and the priority. The negative claim - no earlier Latin alchemical literature - is the field's standard position, not a proof.

The line holds up walked backwards. In Roman Egypt, around the year 300, a man called Zosimos was writing in Greek about furnaces, colour changes and visions.106 Material of that kind was read, copied and translated in the Arabic-speaking world for centuries. Arabic books were then carried into Latin in Iberia and in Italy, with routes running through Sicily, from the twelfth century on.107108109 Latin manuscripts were copied for five hundred years after that. In the 1680s or 1690s an English natural philosopher sat down and put one of them into English in his own handwriting. Two and a half centuries later a Swiss psychiatrist bought the printed versions and built a psychology on them. No link in that sequence is in dispute.

1061:25:31fact
Zosimos of Panopolis, the earliest alchemical author whose writings survive in quantity, worked in Roman Egypt around the year 300.
J. Werrett, 'The Visions of Zosimos of Panopolis: text, translation, and commentary', DPhil thesis, University of Oxford, 2024 (Oxford University Research Archive)

Nothing else in this part depends on the precise year.
What it does not say
FROM MEMORY, not checked against a copy. What I am NOT sure of is any narrower date than around 300 and any page in Mertens; do not put a year, a regnal date or a page on air. Ruska's 1926 book, which I did read, discusses Zosimos extensively but no dating sentence was captured from it.

1091:25:48fact
Euclid's Elements reached Latin Europe in the twelfth century by translation from Arabic, on the same route and in the same period as the alchemical corpus.
Menso Folkerts, 'Euclid in Medieval Europe' — the medieval list of Euclid manuscripts, with introductory account, edited/hosted by Bill Casselman, Department of Mathematics, University of British Columbia

How it holds
Recalled, not read. Adelard of Bath's Latin Euclid, made from Arabic in the first half of the twelfth century, is the standard example of the same transmission route, and Burnett's work on the introduction of Arabic learning documents the shared translators and patrons. Part 7 needs it as the control case: the road that carried alchemy also carried the geometry nobody calls superstition.
What it does not say
Recalled: no edition and no study was opened, so no translator's name should be leaned on hard, no manuscript cited, and no claim made that the same individual translated both. 'Same route, same period' is the defensible claim; 'sometimes the same translators' is stronger than this source supports and should be softened or dropped.

That is a real argument and it deserves better than a shrug. The vocabulary you reach for at two in the morning has a thousand years of handling behind it. Four languages, across cultures that were frequently at war. People who could not read each other kept writing down something that looks like the same thing. When a description survives that much use, the natural inference is that it is tracking something that was actually there. So walk the line, link by link, and ask exactly what walking it proves.

The most famous single page in the tradition is the Emerald Tablet. About a dozen lines, and almost everyone who has heard of it has heard that it is ancient Egyptian, cut into green stone, from Hermes himself. The earliest recoverable text of it is Arabic.110111 It sits embedded inside a larger book about the secret of creation, and that book is Arabic too. The dating of that book puts it no later than 750, and hardly earlier than the sixth century.112113 That book is ascribed to a Greek sage centuries dead by then.114 There is no Egyptian witness earlier than that, and no Greek, Coptic or Latin one either. No ancient author quotes it. Be precise here, because the opposite error is available and it is just as bad. The raw materials the text digests may well be older Hellenistic and Egyptian matter. What does not exist is any evidence for a pharaonic tablet.

1101:27:05fact
No surviving Greek alchemical text contains the Emerald Tablet's characteristic phrases, and no Greek original of it has ever been produced.
Julius Ruska, Tabula Smaragdina: Ein Beitrag zur Geschichte der hermetischen Literatur (Carl Winter, Heidelberg, 1926)
section beginning p. 37; statement at p. 46
Read the passage
How it holds
Ruska devotes a section of the book to the proposition, listed in his own table of contents as Die Tabula Smaragdina nicht griechisch S. 37. At p. 46 he states that within the alchemical literature preserved in Greek no text occurs that contains the Tablet's so characteristic turns of phrase collected together, and argues that deriving the Latin from a lost Greek manuscript would require explaining why such a version left no trace for roughly a thousand years.
What it does not say
This is an argument from absence made in 1926, and it is about the collected phrases as a unit: individual Hermetic motifs certainly do occur in Greek. It licenses the statement that no ancient witness quotes the Tablet, not the statement that nothing in the Tablet is Greek in origin.
1111:27:05fact

How it holds
Ruska's concluding chapter, Rueckblick und allgemeine Ergebnisse, states at p. 166 that the oldest notice of the inscription on the emerald tablet in the grave of Hermes is contained in one of the first writings of Jabir ibn Hayyan of Tus in Khorasan. The same chapter notes at p. 164 that the Book of Krates, at the earliest sixth century, already knows Hermes as an old man on a throne holding a shining inscribed tablet.
What it does not say
Earliest report is a claim about surviving Arabic witnesses as Ruska knew them in 1926, and it is entangled with the Jabir dating problem: if the Jabirian corpus is largely later than its ascription, then this earliest witness is itself of uncertain date. Do not present it as a fixed chronological anchor.
1121:27:24fact
Ruska concluded that the Arabic book carrying the Tablet was written at the latest by the year 750, and that its upper limit can hardly be pushed back beyond the sixth century.

Jahrhundert hinaufruecken koennen. This is the dating window the research notes had previously taken from an encyclopedia summary; it is now read in Ruska himself.

Jahrhundert hinaufrücken können. - 750 is the latest date at which the book (the Kitab sirr al-khalica carrying the Tablet) can have been written, and the upper limit can hardly be pushed back beyond the 6th century.
1141:27:24fact
The Emerald Tablet's text stands at the close of an Arabic cosmological book, the Kitab sirr al-khaliqa or Book of the Secret of Creation, ascribed to Balinas, that is pseudo-Apollonius of Tyana.
Julius Ruska, Tabula Smaragdina: Ein Beitrag zur Geschichte der hermetischen Literatur (Carl Winter, Heidelberg, 1926)
pp. 134 and 164
Read the passage
How it holds
Ruska's 1926 monograph, the study that established the Arabic transmission, gives the book's Arabic titles side by side at p. 134 - Inbegriff der Dinge, Buch der Ursachen, and sirr alhaliqa, Geheimnis der Schoepfung - as titles of the work translated under Balinas's name, and states at p. 164 that the Tablet's text forms the conclusion, bildet den Schluss, of a cosmological writing that Jabir ibn Hayyan already had in his hands. Read in German, in the 1926 text.
What it does not say
Earliest recoverable witness is not date of composition. Ruska's finding is that the text as we have it reaches us inside an Arabic book; it does not exclude older Hellenistic-Egyptian raw material, and it does not license sliding from the Tablet is Arabic to nothing in it is older.

The Egyptian story does come from somewhere, and the somewhere is inside the text. The book that carries the Tablet opens by telling you how it was found. A descent into a chamber beneath a stone statue, and a tablet in the hands of an old man seated on a golden throne. That scene is the evidence people give for the Tablet's antiquity. The document announces its own provenance, and the announcement gets treated as the proof.115 A text is dated by its earliest witnesses and by the vocabulary it presupposes, never by its own claims about itself. Apply that rule and the circle is closed and empty.

1151:28:25fact
The Arabic book that carries the Tablet announces its own provenance in a frame story: Balinas describes a stone statue on a golden pillar in his home town, a dream visitor who tells him to take a light in a glass vessel into a dark chamber beneath it, and an old man seated on a golden throne holding a tablet of green emerald, with a book before him inscribed as the secret of creation and the knowledge of the causes of things.
Julius Ruska, Tabula Smaragdina: Ein Beitrag zur Geschichte der hermetischen Literatur (Carl Winter, Heidelberg, 1926)
pp. 147-148, with pp. 122-123
Read the passage
How it holds
Ruska prints the German translation of the frame narrative at pp. 147-148: the Standbild aus Stein auf einer Saeule von Gold at p. 147; then the dream figure who says Ich bin dein eigenes vollkommenes, feines Wesen, and da fand ich einen Greis, der auf einem Thron aus Gold sass und in seiner Hand eine Tafel aus gruenem Smaragd hielt, with the book before him inscribed Dies ist das Geheimnis der Schoepfung und das Wissen von den Ursachen der Dinge. Ruska's Balinas material also records the priest Sagijus of Nabulus dictating an account of Balinas's entry into the dark chamber (p. 122) and a visit to a tomb-chamber protected by a talisman (p. 123). The document is its own and only evidence for its antiquity.
What it does not say
CORRECTION FOR THE WRITERS: in the recension Ruska translates, the tablet is held by a seated old man on a throne, not by a corpse. The tablet-in-the-hands-of-a-corpse version is the later legend, and saying corpse would misdescribe the text this claim rests on. Say an old man on a golden throne, or say a figure in a vault and leave it there.

The next link is larger and stranger. A vast body of writing circulates under the name of an eighth-century man, Jabir ibn Hayyan, and it cannot be his. Three things date it, on Kraus's case.116 That the philosophical vocabulary it uses was coined around the middle of the ninth century. That the Greek works it cites were not translated into Arabic until the end of that century. And that the Shi'ite religious philosophy running through it is otherwise attested only later still. The standard reconstruction is a school of writers working over generations under one shared pen name.117 Later scholars have pushed back on exactly that inference, from a large corpus to many hands.118 What is back in play is the man's life and the question of how many wrote under his name. Either way, the most productive link in the chain is a pseudonym.

1161:29:05statistic
Later Arabic authorities put the number of writings attributed to Jabir ibn Hayyan at three thousand, a figure Kraus treats as counting chapters and short treatises rather than independent books.
Paul Kraus, Jabir ibn Hayyan: Contribution a l'histoire des idees scientifiques dans l'Islam, vol. 1, Le corpus des ecrits jabiriens (Institut d'Egypte, Cairo, 1943)
Introduction, p. xxii
Read the passage
How it holds
In the introduction to the first volume of his study of the corpus, Kraus reports that Salimardan al-Razi and the alchemist Jildaki agree in estimating the number of Jabirian works at three thousand, and immediately qualifies the figure as counting chapters or brief treatises rather than long independent volumes; the catalogue he works from, Ibn al-Nadim's, lists on the order of 252 titled works arranged in successive collections.
What it does not say
This measures TITLES AND CHAPTERS IN A BIBLIOGRAPHY, not surviving manuscripts and not pages written. A listener hears three thousand books; the number counts entries, many of them a few pages long, and Kraus's whole point is that the count is inflated by the corpus's own habit of splitting a doctrine across many nominal titles. The commonly quoted figure of about six hundred titles was NOT verified.
1171:29:24fact
Kraus concluded that the Jabirian corpus was not written by one eighth-century man: its collections represent successive stages in the teaching of a school, and its religious doctrine refers to the gnostic teaching of the extremist Shiites, which is attested later than the man it is ascribed to.
Paul Kraus, Jabir ibn Hayyan: Contribution a l'histoire des idees scientifiques dans l'Islam, vol. 1, Le corpus des ecrits jabiriens (Institut d'Egypte, Cairo, 1943)
Introduction, pp. xxxiii-xxxv
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in French in the introduction to Kraus's first volume. At pp. xxxiii-xxxiv he states that the different collections of the corpus represent so many stages in the evolution of the teaching of a school; at p. xxxv he states that the religious doctrines particular to the Books of the Balances refer formally to the gnostic teaching of the extremist Shiites. His third argument, that the corpus cites Greek philosophical works only available in Arabic from the later ninth century, is made in the same introduction.
What it does not say
The exact pages for the terminology argument and the Greek-translation argument were not captured, so cite the school conclusion and the Shiite argument with pages and describe the rest as Kraus's case without page numbers. Note too that Kraus's inference from a large corpus to many authors is the joint later scholars attack (Sezgin, Nomanul Haq, Forster, Lory); none of those works was read here, so do not report the counter-arguments as though checked.

Hayyan will mean the plurality of the members of the Gabirean community. p. 52: The Gabirean corpus was a work in progress and the outcome of investigations carried out over approximately a century.

Which changes what 1144 is. The dialogue presents itself as a conversation from the first century of Islam. Recent scholarship holds that attribution to be probably false. The Arabic text behind it was written well after the lifetime it claims, and how long after is disputed.119 The founding document of Latin alchemy is itself an antedating.

1191:30:13fact
The Arabic original behind that first Latin book is dated by recent scholarship to the later tenth century, which puts the conversation it reports roughly three centuries earlier than the text that reports it.
Marion Dapsens, "De la Risalat Maryanus au De Compositione alchemiae", Studia graeco-arabica 6 (2016), 121-140
pp. 123, 135-136 (dating of the Arabic Vorlage); cf. Sebastien Moureau, Micrologus 28 (2020), p. 116
Read the passage
How it holds
Recalled, not read. The Masa'il Khalid li-Maryanus al-rahib has been redated on manuscript and textual grounds by Dapsens and by Moureau to well after the Umayyad period, so the dialogue is a literary construction set in the seventh century and written in the tenth. Part 7 uses it as the first demonstration that the chain's own origin stories are later than the chain.
What it does not say
Recalled and NOT verified: neither Dapsens nor Moureau was opened, so this dating must be voiced as what the recent scholarship argues, without a page, a manuscript or a named argument. Do not state a year. The three-century gap is arithmetic on two datings neither of which was read here.

Put those three findings beside each other. Somebody who tells you this tradition carries one eternal teaching needs a particular thing to be true. He needs the meaning to have travelled with the words. Copying preserves strings of words. The receiving culture supplies what the words mean. The same dozen lines were a cosmogony inside an Arabic account of how the world was made. They were a laboratory key in England in the 1680s. They were a psychological allegory in Zurich. The chain of books is documented and solid. A chain of teaching is a further claim, and nothing in those books establishes it.

There is an objection here. The argument proves too much. If reinterpretation on arrival destroys continuity, then nothing has ever been transmitted by anyone. Euclid arrived in Latin in the same century, largely through Arabic. Nobody says geometry is discontinuous. Nobody says the theorem about the square on the hypotenuse is a string that each culture filled with whatever it liked. So either the argument is wrong, or it is so strong it says nothing.

The reply is comparative rather than absolute, and it costs the argument some of its force. A mathematical text arrives with an external check attached to it. A reader who misconstrues a proof produces something that does not go through, and the next reader can see that it does not. That check constrains what reception is able to do to the text. The hermetic literature carries no such check, and its own conventions were built to remove one.

The Jabirian corpus states its method of composition outright. The dispersion of knowledge, it is called: the doctrine is scattered on purpose across many books, so that no single book contains it.120 Which means there is no page to be checked against. A reader of Euclid who gets it wrong produces a proof that fails, and the next reader watches it fail. A reader of this corpus who gets it wrong has done what the corpus told him to do. Sit with who that damages. Perhaps you have been handed one line out of this literature as though it were the key. That is the move the corpus was designed to break. The reader who believes the tradition carries a single eternal teaching picks up a page and infers the teaching from the page. The tradition's own building principle says that this inference is exactly what the books were made to defeat.

Paul Kraus, Jabir ibn Hayyan: Contribution a l'histoire des idees scientifiques dans l'Islam, vol. I, Le corpus des ecrits jabiriens (Cairo: Institut francais d'archeologie orientale / Memoires de l'Institut d'Egypte 44, 1943)

Kraus documents tabdid al-'ilm as a principle the Jabirian writings assert about themselves, and Lory's introduction to his selection treats it as the organising device of the corpus. Part 7 needs it because it explains why the corpus is enormous and why no single treatise is complete.
What it does not say
Recalled: neither Kraus nor Lory was opened, so no page, no Arabic phrase beyond the term itself and no quotation may be used. Part 4, which WAS read, attributes dispersion of knowledge to Newman's account of alchemical secrecy in general; it does not have the Jabirian corpus saying this of itself, so that source cannot carry this claim. Say that the corpus states the principle, not that any one named treatise does.

Which brings us to a desk in England in the 1680s or 1690s, and a man with a Latin page in front of him. Newton is putting the Emerald Tablet into English in his own hand, five written pages, with a commentary of his own in Latin beside it.121 On one sheet the Latin runs, Pater omnis perfectionis totius mundi est hic.122 On another, in his English, the father of all perfection in the whole world is here.123 And the closing line, the one that says what all of this has been about, he renders like this.124 That which I have said of the operation of the Sun is accomplished and ended. An operation is a procedure.125 A procedure is something a man does at a furnace. That is the word he chose, out of a Latin page that did not choose it for him. Nothing in the centuries of copying behind him made him reach for the furnace.

That is the chain, seen at the one point where we can watch it working. It carried names and hardware across four languages for a thousand years. It did not carry a teaching. In the one place where you can watch a man receive it, he decides what it means with a pen.

So leave the page on the table, because it is not an ornament. The age of the vocabulary you use is not an argument for the vocabulary. It is evidence that a great many people handled it, and every one of them decided what it meant on arrival. Somebody in that line decided last, a great deal more recently than anyone expects. Newton's sheet, still in Cambridge, shows the Latin beside the English he chose for it. It is not the last sheet in this story.126

01:35:53Part 8: The Ancient Tradition That Was Sixty Years Old

Two books on a table, published within a few years of each other in the middle of the nineteenth century. The first is English, printed in London in 1850, and it went out with no author's name on it. The title is A Suggestive Inquiry into the Hermetic Mystery, and the woman who wrote it was Mary Anne Atwood.127 Something under a hundred copies reached libraries and buyers. Then, shortly after it appeared, her father abruptly stopped the book.128 He had the whole remainder called in, over the publisher's protest, at a cost to himself of 250 pounds. It was carted from London to the family house at Gosport. On the lawn there the volumes were stacked and burned, together with the unfinished manuscript of his own poem. She was part of that, and for years afterwards she bought back stray copies, sometimes paying ten guineas for one.129 The second book is American, published in Boston in 1857, and it too went out anonymous, signed with three initials. Behind them was Ethan Allen Hitchcock, a serving officer who ended a major-general in the United States Army.130131132 The two books make the same claim, and it is the claim that eventually reached you. The alchemists were never really talking about metal. The gold was the soul, the furnace was the man, and the literature is a coded account of an interior process. Each book says it has found the key that opens the corpus. And they name two different keys.

1271:35:57fact
Newman and Principe hold that the spiritual reading of alchemy is a nineteenth-century construction, traced to Mary Anne Atwood in England and Ethan Allen Hitchcock in the United States in the 1850s, and that the evidence for a continuous spiritual-alchemical tradition running before 1850 is conjecture for which there is presently no clear historical evidence.
William R. Newman and Lawrence M. Principe, 'Some Problems with the Historiography of Alchemy', in William R. Newman and Anthony Grafton (eds), Secrets of Nature: Astrology and Alchemy in Early Modern Europe (Cambridge, MA: MIT Press, 2001), 385-431
pp. 388-389 (Atwood and Hitchcock, the 1850s) and p. 400 (the conjecture of a tradition persisting to the nineteenth century)
Read the passage
Tara Nummedal, Alchemy and Authority in the Holy Roman Empire (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2007)
p. 7 (printed), Introduction, section "Alchemy in Practice and the Problem of Fraud"
print copy
How it holds
Read in the OPEN archive.org scan of the whole volume, not through a proxy: pp. 388-389 carry the Atwood and Hitchcock passage ('The two most seminal figures in the history of the “spiritual” interpretation of alchemy date from the middle of the nineteenth century'), and p. 400 carries the conjecture sentence about a tradition persisting at some low level through the eighteenth century and seeding nineteenth-century occultism. Part 8's whole argument rests on this pair, which is why it is sourced separately.
What it does not say
Does not license "unambiguous." Nummedal says the reverse about exactly these records: criminal and interrogation records are "notoriously difficult to work with," the voices come to us "in highly mediated form," and confessions taken under threat of torture are "particularly problematic" (p. 7); she quotes Natalie Zemon Davis on stories told to save one's neck. Nor does she anywhere weigh this archive against a thinner spiritual or psychological record — the asymmetry is still the video's own construction, and she must not be cited for it. "Notebooks with dated entries" is also not her material; day-book-style laboratory notebooks in this book belong to George Starkey in the seventeenth century (p. 121), not to the Wurttemberg record.

Recalled, not read. The withdrawal is the best-known fact about the book: Atwood and her father recalled it from the booksellers and burned the stock, on the ground that it revealed too much, which is why first editions are scarce and a reprint had to wait decades. Part 8 needs it because a book withdrawn by its own author is the strongest possible signal that the spiritual reading was being treated as an initiation rather than an argument.

The motive usually reported - that she had disclosed a secret - comes from later accounts by her circle and is not independent testimony.
1291:36:40fact
The Suggestive Inquiry into the Hermetic Mystery was originally published anonymously in 1850 by the London house of Trelawney Saunders. A few copies - something under one hundred in all - had been either sent to public libraries or sold to purchasers, when the further issue of the book was abruptly stopped by Mr. South. The remainder was called in, under considerable protest from the publisher and at a cost of 250 to Mr. South, and was brought from London to Gosport, where on the lawn of Bury House the volumes along with the uncompleted manuscript of Mr. South's poem were stacked and a bonfire was made of them. The authoress afterwards spent years buying back outstanding copies, often paying as much as ten guineas for it.
Mary Anne Atwood, A Suggestive Inquiry into the Hermetic Mystery, 2nd edn (Belfast: William Tait, 1918)

Ethan Allen Hitchcock was an American army general.

How it holds
Recalled, not read. Hitchcock was a career officer of the United States Army who reached the rank of major general and wrote his books on alchemy, Swedenborg and Spenser alongside that career. Part 8 needs it for one reason: the second spiritual reading of alchemy was written not by an occultist but by a serving soldier, which is part of the evidence that this is a period habit of mind rather than a transmitted doctrine.
What it does not say
Recalled: the title page and preface read carry no rank and no name, only the initials E. A. H., so this is not supported by the book itself. Give the rank as background, not as something the source shows, and do not attach dates or campaigns to it.

That sentence about the gold and the soul is not a footnote in the history of an obscure craft. It is the load-bearing beam under everything you use the word nigredo for. If the alchemists were doing metallurgy and only metallurgy, a state of mind has no business borrowing their vocabulary. You were offered that vocabulary because somebody established, or seemed to establish, that the operations were about a person. So the question of when that was established is not antiquarian. It is the question of how old your own map is. And that map was drawn a great deal later than medieval.

Here is the test the historians propose, and it is a fair one. A spiritual reading might really have been the alchemists' own understanding of their work. If it was, it should be documentable in their own texts, continuously, before 1850. Not inferred from the strangeness of the imagery. Written down, and traceable across the centuries in which they were actually working. That test runs against an archive the last few parts have already put in front of you. Tara Nummedal built her book out of the paperwork: letters, laboratory reports, supply orders, and inventories taken when a patron died. Work contracts too, like the one that put a man in front of a furnace to make gold out of iron for a duke. And when a project failed there was more paper still, testimony and interrogation transcripts and official reports. Failure, she says, generated even more of it than success. On the spiritual side, the historians' charge is that the pre-1850 evidence for continuity is conjecture rather than clear historical evidence. It was occultists, writing in the 1800s, who decided that the operations recorded in these texts were never physical at all. That, the historians hold, is not what the historical record shows the alchemists doing, and they reject it as history. A specialist surveying the field calls their critique a powerful one, and the spiritual interpretation unsustainable against the record.

What is documentable, precisely and datably, is what those two books did. And here the detail matters more than the accusation. Atwood's key was Mesmerism, the animal magnetism of her own century. An operator puts a subject into a trance, and the subject reports knowledge nobody gave them. She did not have to be caught bringing that to the texts. Her own chapter synopsis announces the book as connecting together Alchemy and Mesmerism, also, with those preliminary Rites.133 Hitchcock brought a moral and religious key instead.134135 Through that key, the alchemists become writers on the perfection of man. They encode themselves, on his reading, to escape the stake.136137 The following year he turned the same key on Swedenborg, in a book announced as a sequel.138 Both readings are careful. Both are learned. Both are honest attempts on the same texts, made at almost the same moment, by two people working independently.

Hitchcock's explanation of alchemical obscurity is religious persecution: the alchemists lived in an age when open expression of their opinions would have exposed them to the stake, so they wrote in symbolic form to hide the subject from the crowd and to screen themselves.
Ethan Allen Hitchcock, Remarks upon Alchemy and the Alchemists (Crosby, Nichols and Company, Boston, 1857)

How it holds
Read in the Preface at pp. viii-ix of the 1857 edition, where Hitchcock writes that they lived for the most part in an age when an open expression of their opinions would have brought them into conflict with the superstition of the time and thus exposed them to the stake, and that the volumes in which the thought of the time was enshrined were written in symbolic form to hide the subject from the crowd and to screen the authors from persecution.
What it does not say
This is the interpretive key an American army general brought TO the texts in 1857, imported from his own Swedenborgian moral religion; it is not a finding about why any particular alchemist wrote as he did. How it holds
The 1858 title page reads Swedenborg, a hermetic philosopher: being a sequel to Remarks on alchemy and the alchemists. Showing that Emanuel Swedenborg was a hermetic philosopher and that his writings may be interpreted from the point of view of hermetic philosophy. With a chapter comparing Swedenborg and Spinoza, and names the author only as BY THE AUTHOR OF REMARKS ON ALCHEMY AND THE ALCHEMISTS; the Advertisement opens I published last year a small volume of Remarks upon Alchemy, the object of which was to show that the so-called Philosopher's Stone ... was a mere symbol, the genuine Alchemists being in fact Hermetic philosophers.

The two results go side by side. The alchemists may have left a doctrine in their texts. If they did, two careful readers reading those texts should have come out holding the same doctrine. They did not. She came out holding Mesmerism. He came out holding the moral perfection of man. Each went into a corpus of half-decipherable images and recovered the tradition's secret. And each secret was the one the reader walked in with. That is the signature of projection. And the party doing the projecting is the modern reader.

What happened next is a chain you can follow. The reading passed into the occult revival at the end of the century. There it was repeated and expanded and treated as recovered knowledge, rather than as one recent proposal by two Victorians.139 In 1914, in Vienna, a psychoanalyst inside Freud's circle named Herbert Silberer took a Rosicrucian allegory and read it psychoanalytically. He separated the psychoanalytic level of meaning from what he called the anagogic level.140141 The anagogic sense is the forward-pointing one, in which a text is about where a person is going rather than where they have been.142 That move takes alchemy's structure, its stages and its direction toward a goal, and puts it into psychological language. Jung read him, and cited him by name in Psychology and Alchemy.143 So the tradition Jung believed he had rediscovered under fifteen hundred years of dust was about sixty years old when he met it. And it had been read out of the occult revival's corpus, not out of the working archive.

1421:42:45fact
Silberer's book distinguishes three readings of the same alchemical allegory: the psychoanalytic, which leads to the depths of the impulsive life; the hermetic-religious, which he names the anagogic; and the chemical, which lies midway and is ethically indifferent.
Herbert Silberer, Problems of Mysticism and its Symbolism, trans. Smith Ely Jelliffe (Moffat, Yard and Company, New York, 1917)

How it holds

The problem of holding them together is the book's stated subject: at p. 249 he asks how one and the same series of images can harmonise several mutually exclusive interpretations, and singles out the psychoanalytic and the anagogic as the two antithetic ones. The German edition poses the same question at its p. 19.

What it does not say
This documents citation and use, not the famous credit sentence. The often-quoted line in which Jung calls Silberer the first to find the threads leading from alchemy to the psychology of the unconscious was NOT located, and its volume and paragraph are disputed between CW 12 and CW 14. Do not quote it. Note also that the copy read is an Internet Archive upload, so paragraph numbers are citable and page numbers are not.

That is the case, and it has a soft place in it. Somebody has now pushed on that place very hard indeed. The load-bearing premise is a negative one. It is the claim that nothing continuous was ever found in the record before 1850. A claim that something is absent from an archive is only as good as the search that went through the archive. And alchemy's archive is one that historians themselves describe as still largely unread. In 2021 Oxford University Press published a book that treated the charge as a research assignment rather than a verdict. Its author, Mike Zuber, says he worked through obscure manuscript sources. He argues that he traced a line of people who read the work as a work on the soul.144 On his account it runs from the circle around Jacob Boehme in the seventeenth century to the Englishwoman whose copies went onto the bonfire.145 If that holds, she inherited a category rather than inventing one, and your vocabulary is older than the charge allows. The debunk as usually stated is then too strong, and the defensible line is narrower. It has to be said in these words. The universalising of it was Victorian. On Zuber's account, Boehme's people said that their own work was on the soul. They were a devotional current, and they knew they were one. What 1850 added was the word every: that this is what alchemy had always been, in every workshop, under all the metallurgy. That claim is sixty years old, and the vocabulary you reached for comes down from it.

How it holds
The title page reads Spiritual Alchemy from the Age of Jacob Boehme to Mary Anne Atwood, 1600-1900, Mike Andre Zuber, Universiteit van Amsterdam, 2017. Chapter IV says that beyond the pompous verbiage and occasional references to magnetism, its views are quite traditional and in many ways aligned with Christian mysticism, as well as Boehme's theosophy of rebirth in particular.

That rescue cuts the other way as well. Suppose the continuity is real. Suppose there was a Christian devotional current, from Boehme's people onward, saying plainly and in public that the work is on the soul. Those men are not evidence for Jung. They are his rivals. His mechanism requires that the alchemist did not know what he was doing. Unconscious material falls into the flask because the man staring at it has no vocabulary for his own interior. A knowing allegory has no vacuum in it. Somebody writing an allegory of the soul on purpose, in a devotional idiom, and meaning it, is doing something Jung's theory cannot describe. Nothing there is being projected, and nothing there is unconscious. So the strongest defence against the Victorian charge is bought at a price. It saves the antiquity of the spiritual reading by handing it to men who were doing deliberate theology.146

What it does not say

Also, this article never mentions Jung and never uses the word spiritual, so it cannot be cited as Newman writing about Jung.

Which leaves Jung's alchemy in a stranger position than either side usually puts it. On Wouter Hanegraaff's account, Jung is not an outside observer of esoteric traditions. He is inside that history, one of the people who shaped what the modern West takes esotericism to be.147 On that reading, the alchemical volumes are first-rate evidence about a chapter of modern religious history. They are very poor evidence about medieval men with furnaces. Which is worth knowing when the word you use at two in the morning is handed to you as ancient.

Esotericism and the Academy argues that the modern study of esotericism has been shaped by 'religionist' scholars who were themselves participants, and Jung is one of the cases: his psychological reading of alchemy is not a description of esotericism from outside but a stage in its transformation into psychology. That term must be voiced as Hanegraaff's category, unquoted, and the claim should rest on the Eranos/central-actor framing that was read.

And this is not a difficulty Jung missed. A European tradition that reaches you already interpreted proves nothing. You cannot tell the tradition's own voice from the voice of the last person who explained it. What the argument needs at that point is a witness who had never met Atwood, or Hitchcock, or the occult revival, or Freud's Vienna. He needed a text from outside Europe altogether, made by people sealed off from every one of these readers. In 1928 exactly such a text arrived in Zurich, in a parcel.

01:48:17Part 9: The Sealed Book That Was a Seance

Men at a Chinese altar, some centuries ago, and it is worth telling slowly, because it is not an argument. It is a scene. The altar was devoted to Lü Dongbin, an immortal of the Tang dynasty. He was addressed as a god, and was believed to speak. The technique was spirit-writing. Two men hold a forked stick of willow or peach over a tray of sand, and they are not supposed to be steering it. A reader calls off the characters and a scribe writes them down.148 The god is the author. The men at the altar are the pen. The year was 1668, the altar was at Piling, and a man named Pan Yi'an received the transmission there along with six others. Over sessions, a text arrived that way, in pieces. More than twenty years passed before one of those seven men compiled and revised what had been written down.149150

1481:48:45fact
Spirit-writing of this kind works by a Y-shaped stylus, commonly a forked branch of willow or peach, held by two spirit-writers who trace characters in a tray of sand, incense ash or water while a reader calls the characters out and a scribe records them, with the revelation ascribed to the deity rather than to the participants.
'The Spirit-Writing Movement in the Chaozhou Region: Response to Modern Crises (1840-1949)', Religions 14:4 (2023), art. 429 (open access, CC-BY)
footnote 1, the definition of the fuji seance and its personnel
Read the passage
How it holds
Fuji is a well-documented Chinese religious technique: a forked stick or planchette held by two people traces characters in sand, an attendant calls them out and a recorder writes them down, and the resulting text is treated as the god's speech. Part 9 needs the mechanism because the listener has to be able to picture the room in which the text Jung called ancient was actually produced.
What it does not say
Supports the mechanism as claimed, with one correction of detail: the stylus is HELD BY TWO PEOPLE (a forked willow or peach branch), not 'suspended', and the tray may be sand, incense ash or water. The reading-off and recording are two distinct roles, a reader and a scribe, not one scribe. The article is about Chaozhou 1840-1949, not about the Golden Flower, so it licenses the technique only in general terms and no dynasty or region may be attached to the Golden Flower's own production from it. The article's author was not established; cite by title and journal.

NO SCHOLARLY EDITION OF THIS ARTICLE WAS FOUND - the source still rests on a project server at edu.hk.

Long after those sessions, that text reached Zurich. A parcel arrives there in 1928. Inside is a German typescript, a translation of a Chinese text, sent by a sinologist who had spent years in China. Jung reads it, and writes a psychological commentary on it within the year. Within about two years he has stopped working on the red folio he had been painting and calligraphing for a decade and a half. His own epilogue to that book names 1928, the year the text arrived, as the beginning of the end of it. Whatever else that parcel is, it is the hinge of the entire story.

So take seriously why a man in his fifties would drop his own book of visions because a package came in the post. By 1928 he had a problem that no amount of further painting could solve. He possessed an enormous private visionary corpus, and he regarded it as knowledge rather than as illness. Part three left him at the wall that fact runs into: a man who has had visions cannot cite himself. What he needed was a witness.

Not just any witness. The Latin material could only take him so far, because Jung was a European and the Latin material was European. A Swiss doctor finding his own images in a Basel printing of 1593 has found something. But the transmission line between those two is short and crowded. What settles a convergence argument is a witness who could not possibly have been in contact. Different continent, different language, different religion, no shared library. If that witness produces the same figures, the shared source cannot be culture. The only candidate left is something in the structure of the mind itself. That is a real argument, and if you have ever taken comfort in the fact that this vocabulary shows up everywhere, that argument is the reason.

The parcel appeared to be exactly that. The text is the one known in English as The Secret of the Golden Flower. It was presented, then and ever since, as an ancient Chinese classic.151 The frame of the text ascribes it to Lü Dongbin, an immortal of the Tang dynasty.152 That puts it roughly a thousand years before anybody in Zurich was born. The translator's 1929 introduction carried that framing, and the popular retelling still carries it now. Jung read it and found circular figures, a centre, a flowering light, the squaring of a circle. In his own commentary, published in 1931, he called the Golden Flower a mandala symbol. He had often met with it, he wrote, in the material brought to him by his patients.153

The claim that the book is an ancient Chinese classic is Wilhelm's own framing: he attributes the teaching to Lu Yen, also called Lu Tung-pin, said to have been born in A.D. 755, and traces the oral tradition to the T'ang-period religion of the Golden Elixir of Life.
Richard Wilhelm (trans.) with a commentary by C. G. Jung, The Secret of the Golden Flower: A Chinese Book of Life, English version by Cary F. Baynes (Kegan Paul, Trench, Trubner and Co., London, 1931)
Wilhelm's introduction, section Origins of the Book, pp. 15-16 of the Internet Archive scan
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the same section of Wilhelm's introduction: the teaching is attributed to Lu Yen, whose other name was Lu Tung-pin, who lived at the end of the eighth and beginning of the ninth century and was born in the year A.D. 755; the tradition is traced back to the Chin Tan Chiao, the religion of the Golden Elixir of Life, which developed in the T'ang period in the eighth century; and the book is said to come from an esoteric circle transmitted first orally and then in writing. Wilhelm's own text therefore contains both the eighth-century claim and, four sentences away, the seventeenth-century printing.
What it does not say
Wilhelm is reporting the tradition's own account of itself, and hedges it: the founder is SAID to have been Lu Yen. The source shows where the popular ancient-Taoist-classic-by-Lu-Yen framing comes from. It is not evidence that the text is eighth-century, and it is not a scholarly dating.

Part four left a piece of glassware on the bench, the sealed vessel, the pot that works only because nothing gets in or out. Jung's evidence has the same shape. The whole evidential value of that Chinese text lies in its being closed, in nothing having passed between it and him.154 Break the seal and the witness is worth nothing, because a witness who has been in the room is not a witness at all.

1541:52:54fact
The same structural charge can be made on both sides of Jung's evidence, because on the European side his own framing is that alchemy is an undercurrent compensating what Christianity left out, and on the Chinese side the text reached him through an admittedly defective Chinese edition and a German translation whose renderings Cleary calls contaminated.
C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, Collected Works vol. 12, trans. R. F. C. Hull (Princeton/Bollingen)
Introduction, para. 26
Read the passage

How it holds
This joins three sources already established rather than adding a new source: Jung's undercurrent framing at CW 12 para. 26 (Part 7); Wilhelm's own account of the incomplete seventeenth-century woodblock copy completed from a friend's book; and Cleary's verdict on the Wilhelm/Jung/Baynes edition.
What it does not say
This is the video's synthesis, not a finding by any of the three sources: none of Jung, Wilhelm or Cleary draws the parallel between the Latin florilegia and the Chinese recension. Voice it as the argument the video is making. In particular, the claim that Jung's Latin sources were anthologies stripped of laboratory procedure is NOT sourced in this part and must be carried by another part's evidence or dropped.

Now go back to the altar this part opened on, because that is the document. It is not ancient. It is late, and it was produced by spirit-writing at altars devoted to Lü Dongbin.155 Seven men at an altar at Piling in 1668. A compilation more than twenty years later, a second transmission in 1692, and then printing-blocks passed to another man. A rival lineage claims the text went only to its own disciples from 1688, at an altar on Mount Jin'gai. Men held a forked stick and let it write, session after session. That is what reached Zurich in the post. Carl Jung read it and stopped writing his own book of visions, because at last he had found somebody who was not him.
