print copy
How it holds
Kirsch states all four elements in one sentence-chain: von Franz was eighteen and on a class trip from school when she met Jung; the year is given explicitly as 1933; she wanted analysis with him but could not afford it; and in exchange for the analysis she did translations of Latin and Greek texts he needed for his alchemy research. He does NOT say the outing was to Bollingen or to the tower — the words are only "on class trip from school", with no destination named.
What it does not say

(Bollingen appears in Kirsch only as Jung's own refuge on p. 1 and, separately, as the site von Franz later bought for her own tower, p. 11.) He also gives no interval between the meeting and the start of analysis, so 'not long after' is an addition. And he does not connect the translations to Mysterium Coniunctionis: he says the work she based on them was an exposition of the visions of St Perpetua. The draft's line naming Mysterium Coniunctionis is not carried by this passage.

How it holds
Read in the OCR full text of The Logic of Scientific Discovery, chapter 1, section 2, 'Elimination of Psychologism', where the sentence opens the section, and where Popper goes on to say that every discovery contains an irrational element or a creative intuition and that there is no such thing as a logical method of having new ideas. This is the first horn of Part 13's dilemma in its author's own words: if the origin of a hypothesis is outside logical appraisal, then 'it came from a misreading' is biography - and the alchemical pedigree is equally inert as support.
What it does not say
Popper is talking about the logic of scientific method, not about psychology or about Jung, and the point is about JUSTIFICATION, not about whether origins are interesting. Cite the section, not the page.

How it holds
Read in the full text of Rampling's Ambix article, whose statement of purpose is: 'In this paper, I argue that alchemical practitioners trained themselves through both reading and practical experience to identify and replicate the chemical phenomena - the signs and tokens - described in their written sources.' This is what the corpus looks like to a current historian of alchemy: technical literature about observable effects, read by people trying to reproduce them. It is the ground for Part 13's central move - if the corpus is that, then it is not a second, uncontacted sample of the psyche, and convergence with it is not corroboration.

It licenses 'a body of technical literature', not 'nothing but chemistry' - the same journal literature holds that religion and alchemy interpenetrated. The article was read through a text-extraction proxy of the paywalled publisher page, which does not preserve pagination, so locators are section-level only.

Nothing in that is dishonest. By every account she was formidable, and she outlived him by decades doing her own work. Her name was Marie-Louise von Franz. The point is narrower and colder, and it is about who was in a position to check whom. The construal of the Latin, the decision about what a sixteenth-century sentence actually says, came from a student. She was paying for her own analysis in translation.206 The passages reached him already sorted, by somebody deciding which ones he would want. She was working on texts he had chosen, toward a thesis he had already published.

2062:43:08fact
The construal of the Latin, the decision about what a sixteenth-century sentence actually says, came from a student who was paying for her own analysis in translation.
Thomas B. Kirsch, The Jungians: A Comparative and Historical Perspective (London: Routledge, 2000)
Printed p. 3 [PDF p. 30], Chapter 1, section "The early training of analysts", first paragraph; with the von Franz arrangement at printed p. 11 [PDF p. 38]
print copy
How it holds
Kirsch does describe the economics: in the Zurich of the 1920s and 1930s people wrote to Jung, came for varying lengths of time, and the length of an analysis was governed partly by what they could afford. He gives concrete instances of analysands who could not pay — von Franz herself (p. 11) and Michael Fordham, who 'could not afford to have an analysis with Jung' and whose analyst Baynes 'charged little for the sessions, but in return received permission from Fordham to publish his drawings and active imaginations' (p. 45). So payment in kind for analysis was a real practice in this circle. What Kirsch never uses is the language of debt: his phrase for von Franz is 'In exchange for analysis' — a barter struck at the outset, not a sum owed.
What it does not say
'In her analyst's debt' overshoots the source in two ways. Kirsch describes an exchange agreed up front, not an obligation she was carrying; and he nowhere says Jung held anything over her or that the arrangement was unequal, exploitative or resented. The passage licenses the structural point that the Latin came from someone working off the price of her analysis; it does not license any word implying she was indebted to, or under obligation from, the man interpreting the material.

And you can hold the object that arrangement produced. The companion volume to Jung's last book is a medieval Latin treatise called the Aurora consurgens. He had her edit it. It travels under the name of Thomas Aquinas. Aquinas died in 1274.207 The oldest and most spectacular surviving copy dates from the 1420s. Keep the honest limit out loud. A manuscript of the 1420s dates the witness and not the text, and a work can outlive every early copy of it.208 The attribution to Aquinas is generally rejected, and the defence of it was written by von Franz herself, inside that same volume. Her edition went out under a title that claims exactly as much as the attribution is worth. A document attributed to Thomas Aquinas on the problem of opposites in alchemy.209

2092:44:21fact
The Aurora consurgens was edited by von Franz at Jung's request as the companion work to Mysterium Coniunctionis, and the defence of its attribution to Thomas Aquinas was written by von Franz herself, in that volume, where she offers it as a hypothesis she defends against a sceptical reception rather than as an established attribution.
Marie-Louise von Franz (ed., trans., comm.), Aurora Consurgens: A Document Attributed to Thomas Aquinas on the Problem of Opposites in Alchemy, trans. R. F. C. Hull and A. S. B. Glover (Bollingen Series LXXVII, Pantheon, 1966)
title page and Foreword, p. vii; the discussion of the attribution in the introduction, ca. p. xi
Read the passage
How it holds
Von Franz's edition, translation and commentary appeared as the companion volume to Jung's last large alchemical work, and her introduction argues for taking the ascription to Aquinas seriously. Part 13 needs it because the strongest defence of a contested attribution was produced inside the school that needed the attribution to hold, which is the structural point the part is making about motivated scholarship.
What it does not say
Supports the claim, with one softening: von Franz presents the Aquinas ascription as a hypothesis she defends rather than as an established attribution, and she herself records the sceptical reception. So 'the defence of the attribution comes from inside the Jungian school' is right; 'she asserts Aquinas wrote it' would overstate her.

So the psychology survives, stripped to a single leg, and the leg it stands on is clinical.

Which brings back the question you came in with. Somebody handed you a word for your fourteenth month and told you it was trustworthy because it is old. Older than psychiatry. Tested by centuries of men who knew something we have forgotten. There are only two views you can take of where an idea comes from, and the tradition loses on both of them. One view says the road an idea travelled is beside the point, and only the evidence for it counts. The other says the road counts, before any evidence is in. On the first view, the whole question goes to the evidence, and the road is filed under biography. On that view, a misdated Egyptian corpus back at the origin does the word no damage at all. But the same knife cuts back in the same stroke. If the road is irrelevant, then the age of the tradition is not a reason to trust the word either. It is only where the word happened to be lying when somebody picked it up.

Now the second view, the one where the road does count. It counts at the front end, before anybody has tested anything. An idea arrives with a rough standing, a sense of how seriously it deserves to be taken at all. A bad road lowers that standing rather than leaving it untouched. And this road was bad twice.210 It came down a line that read a late corpus as ancient. Then it ran through one man who spent decades in those books and heard them back from his own patients. So the two views agree about the only thing you needed them for. On the first, the tradition's age was never a reason for anything, because ages never are. On the second, this particular age is a mark against the word. Either way you lose the sentence you were handed with it. It is older than psychiatry, so it must be tracking something. Nobody can say that to you again, and nothing in the clinic changes when they stop.

2102:46:16fact
The distinction between the context of discovery and the context of justification was introduced by Hans Reichenbach, who assigned epistemology only the second and described its work as a rational reconstruction of how thinking ought to proceed rather than a description of how anyone actually thinks.
Hans Reichenbach, Experience and Prediction: An Analysis of the Foundations and the Structure of Knowledge (University of Chicago Press, 1938)
section 1, 'The three tasks of epistemology', pp. 5-6
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the OCR full text of Experience and Prediction, section 1, 'The three tasks of epistemology', pp. 5-6, where Reichenbach introduces the two terms to mark the distinction, says epistemology is only occupied in constructing the context of justification, describes its constructions as how thinking processes ought to occur if they are to be ranged in a consistent system, and states that it can never be a permissible objection to an epistemological construction that actual thinking does not conform to it. This is the philosophical warrant for the 'origins are irrelevant' horn.
What it does not say
Reichenbach's own use of the distinction is narrower and more complicated than the later disciplinary slogan built on it - the Stanford entry that points here says as much - so the script should say 'the distinction associated with Reichenbach', not 'Reichenbach proved that origins do not matter'. Nothing in the passage says that a hypothesis's pedigree is evidence FOR it either, which is precisely the symmetry Part 13 exploits.

What survives is smaller and it is real. A trigger does not load the gun, it only chooses the moment. The alchemical corpus did not put the psychology into Jung. It fired something already there. That is a claim about one man's working life, and no claim at all about the men at the furnaces.

Do not let that harden into the comfortable version, where everything turns out to have been worth it. Take the verdict from part four and date it properly. In 1946 an economist who had bought a share of Newton's papers called him the last of the magicians. That line has carried Newton's alchemy in front of general readers ever since. It was written before anyone had identified the books Newton was actually reading. When the manuscripts were edited, the author who had shaped him most turned out to be a working chymist writing under a pen-name. The verdict did not survive that. Nobody thinks the sentence was worthless. And nobody doing the history now cites it as evidence about Newton. Both halves are true simultaneously, and they are the exact halves at issue here. Productive stimulus, yes. Supporting testimony, no.

And run the same case one generation later, with Jung's own reading inside it. In 1975 Cambridge published the first full-length study of Newton's alchemy, by a serious historian called Betty Jo Teeter Dobbs. The section where she sets out what alchemy was for is headed, the nature of the older alchemy and its soteriological function: the Jungian model. She calls the approach a really promising one, and she builds on it.211 What she says Newton was hunting is a mediator, a middle nature required for the joining of disparate substances, and the active principles behind it.212 That book made the manuscripts respectable and produced decades of real scholarship. It was then taken apart by closer material reading, with a manuscript she never saw, and with the experiments run again on a bench.213 The interpretation that was dismantled had been a Jungian one.

What it does not say
She expounds and adopts Jung's model as a framework; she does not become a Jungian, and later in the same chapter she corrects his history. ch. 6, “Newton's Integration of Alchemy and Mechanism”, Conclusion, p. 230

How it holds
Gives the restored Dobbs beat something specific to name rather than a thesis gestured at, and it is the honest content of her 1975 reading: a mediator between substances, not a divine agent in matter.
What it does not say
“Mediator” here is a chymical concept, a middle nature that joins disparate substances; it is not a theological claim and this book must not be quoted for one. Her Epilogue at p. 233 states Newton's aim as probing the internal structure of the particles of universal matter to find the forces by which they are impelled towards or repelled from one another.
2132:49:34fact
That reading was later taken apart by closer material reading: at least a million words of alchemical writing in Newton's hand survive, edited only in the last decade, and reading them means looking at the properties Newton ascribes to each material and the protocols he applies to it in the laboratory.

Newman's Newton the Alchemist argues from a far larger manuscript base, much of it unavailable or unread when Dobbs worked, and from hands-on replication of Newton's procedures, that the alchemy was chymistry pursued for chymical ends rather than a coded theology. Part 13 needs it as the correction, and as evidence that where an origin story is testable, the test is done on the work rather than on the motive.
What it does not say
Newton the Alchemist has now been read from the owner's copy and both halves of the sentence hold. Newman names B. J. T. Laboratory replication is his own declared method (pp. 8 and 10), and the products he reports replicating include Newton's liquor of antimony and “volatile Venus”; it is his own bench work, not a re-run of every Newton experiment. The publisher's author essay stays in this source as the source of the summary verdict only.

Which returns us to the man reading Greek in 1614. A whole philosophy had been built on a corpus everybody took for ancient, and when the dating collapsed the philosophy did not evaporate. What stopped was citing Hermes as a witness. That is the operation now due.

Which leaves exactly one thing unanswered, and it is the thing you brought with you. What the alchemists meant does not settle it, and neither does anything Jung proved. The question is what you are entitled to say to yourself, tonight, in the fourteenth month.

02:50:44Part 14: A Name Without a Date

It is the fourteenth month, and nothing has moved. You still have the name for it, and the name still fits. But a second thought has been arriving underneath the first one for a while now, and it is not about the state at all. I should be through this by now. That sentence has a shape worth looking at, because it is a comparison. It sets where you are against where you were supposed to be, which means that somewhere in your head there is a schedule. You never agreed to it. You cannot remember anyone handing it to you. So this last stretch is about where it came from, and what you are entitled to do with it.

The route is completely traceable, and there are no villains anywhere along it. In 1985 a Jungian analyst published a book that took the alchemical operations and gave each one a chapter of its own. Calcinatio, the burning down. Solutio, the dissolving.214 Coagulatio, the hardening back into a form. Then four more, each with its own chapter, ending on coniunctio, the joining. Before that book, the vocabulary sat inside sixteenth century Latin compendia and the commentary on them. After it, the vocabulary was a working language a clinician could learn and use on a Tuesday afternoon. That is what made it usable, and being usable is what made it portable.

2142:51:45fact
In 1985 the Jungian analyst Edward F. Edinger published a book that took the alchemical operations - calcinatio, solutio, coagulatio, sublimatio, mortificatio, separatio, coniunctio - and gave each one a chapter of its own.
Edward F. Edinger, Anatomy of the Psyche: Alchemical Symbolism in Psychotherapy (Open Court, La Salle, Illinois, 1985; the scan read is the sixth printing, 1994), xviii + 260 pp., ISBN 0812690095
Contents and List of Illustrations (front matter)
Read the passage
How it holds
Anatomy of the Psyche: Alchemical Symbolism in Psychotherapy (Open Court, 1985) is the book that turned Jung's alchemical material into a working clinical vocabulary by giving each operation its own chapter and its own set of examples. That is the step Part 14 turns on: making the vocabulary usable is what made it portable, and the popular arc is built on Edinger's shelf, not on Jung's.
What it does not say
READ, replacing an earlier note of 'Recalled, not read.' What was actually opened is the FRONT MATTER of a scan of the Open Court edition: title page, copyright page, Contents and List of Illustrations. The body text was not reached. So the source licenses the book's date, publisher and chapter-per-operation structure, and nothing about the contents of any chapter. The stated locator 'ch. 2 Calcinatio (p. 17) and ch. 6 Mortificatio (p. 147)' was NOT verified and should come out of the locator.

The argument that book is making is short. The alchemical texts describe recurring operations under stable names. Patients keep producing the same patterns in dreams and symptoms, things dissolving, things burning, things rotting, things joining. A therapy needs shared words for what is happening to the person in the room. So the operations can serve as a descriptive vocabulary for psychic process. Something is missing from those steps. There is no claim that the operations run in a fixed order. There is no claim that completing them delivers you anywhere.

Add one line, and the whole thing changes character. The line is: the operations occur in a fixed sequence, and finishing the sequence produces a settled end state. With that premise in place, a vocabulary for describing where you are becomes an itinerary for where you are going. A word that told you what this is now tells you how far along this is. That is the smuggled premise, and everything sold downstream rides on it. Nothing in the alchemical record establishes it. The tradition could not even agree whether the yellow was a stage at all.215 And nothing in the clinical record establishes it either, which is what the falsifiability question came down to earlier.

2152:53:57fact
The Latin alchemical tradition inherited from late antiquity a fourfold colour progression - black, white, yellow and red, nigredo, albedo, citrinitas and rubedo - not the three-stage arc the popular version uses.
Jennifer M. Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71:1 (2024), 73-97, doi 10.1080/00026980.2024.2309059
section 'The decline of citrinitas in alchemical philosophy'
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in Rampling's Ambix article: 'From these late antique roots, Latin alchemists eventually inherited the fourfold colour progression of black (nigredo), white (albedo), yellow (citrinitas or citrinatio), and red (rubedo).' Part 14 needs this because the arc the market sells is a three-beat one; the tradition it claims descent from starts with four terms and then argues about them.
What it does not say
The correction is 'no canonical count', not 'there were really four'. Rampling's whole article is about how contested the fourth term was, and the same tradition organised the work by twelve operations elsewhere. Section-level locator only: the article was read through a text proxy that does not preserve pagination.

It is the fourteenth month, and this is not the first stretch like it. The blackness recurs. In some lives it never lifts at all, and any clinician can tell you so. The tradition offers two readings of a life like that. Under the first you are behind. Under the second there is nothing to be behind. Here is the first one. The schedule rests on the claim that the black is a station you pass through. So a life that keeps coming back to it is a failed run of the process. Here is the second. On this reading darkness is a permanent dimension of psychic life, and it carries a light of its own. Stanton Marlan named that image the black sun, and read it as an image of the non-self.216 It turns up at the worst experiences a person has, and at the most exalted ones.217 On that reading, that is not a contradiction to be resolved. Now set the two readings side by side. Something odd shows up. They recommend nothing different. Neither one tells you to do anything you were not already doing. They differ entirely in what they say about you, in the fourteenth month. So the promise that you get through the nigredo and then you arrive is not a friendly simplification of the tradition. It is a different doctrine wearing the tradition's words.

What it does not say
This is the publisher's summary, not Marlan's prose; it is the press vouching for the book, and it cannot support a quotation from Marlan or a page reference. The research notes' other Marlan phrases - 'a darkness that shines', 'intrinsic importance to the human psyche' - were NOT on the page read and must not be voiced as his words.

Against systematising the vocabulary at all, there is a serious position, and it was held by a serious person. Start with a black crust on the inside of a pot. It has a colour, and a smell, and it resists the tool you scrape it with. Now call that the depressive phase of individuation. The phrase is accurate, and something has gone out of the room. James Hillman built a whole position on that loss, and its premises are short. A concept is an abstraction, and it is thin by design, because thin is what lets it cover many cases. An image is a particular, and the particulars were what worked on the person. Alchemical writing is unusually dense with them, which is why Hillman treats it as a language rather than a theory. So rendering the crust as a phase does not clarify the image. It throws away the one property that was doing the work.218 On this view the concept is always poorer than the picture it replaces, and the trade always runs one way.

2182:56:49fact
James Hillman argued the opposite case: that alchemical language is itself the psychology, so that rendering an image as a psychological concept loses the particularity that made the image work.
James Hillman, 'The Therapeutic Value of Alchemical Language: A Heated Introduction', in Alchemical Psychology, Uniform Edition of the Writings of James Hillman, vol. 5 (Spring Publications, Putnam, Connecticut, 2010), ISBN 978-0-88214-550-1
Opening essay of the volume, the passage on what happens when alchemical images are rendered as psychological concepts
Read the passage
How it holds
Hillman's alchemical writing holds that the therapeutic value of alchemical language lies in its imagistic density - colours, substances, operations kept as particulars - and that translating those particulars into developmental concepts thins them into abstractions. It is the serious position against systematisation, and its cost, which Hillman accepts, is that it cannot be transmitted as technique, which is why his frame could never have produced a workbook and Edinger's could.
What it does not say
READ, replacing an earlier note of 'from memory, not checked against a copy.' The opening essay was opened and read in a scan of the Uniform Edition vol. 5 and both passages above were read there; the edition was identified from the scan's own copyright page (Spring Publications, Putnam, Connecticut, 2010, ISBN 978-0-88214-550-1). ONE LIMIT: the scan's printed folios were not legible in the text extraction, so NO PAGE NUMBER may be given. The ledger's 'the essay begins at p. 9' could NOT be confirmed - the extraction placed the essay's opening at its own page 1 and the White Queen passage at its own page 7, which are scan positions, not printed folios. Cite the essay and the passage, not a page. Note also the internal ellipsis in the quotation above, where a clause about the hermaphrodite and the golden head was elided.

There is a price for that position, and Hillman paid it knowingly. A psychology that refuses to be translated out of its images cannot be taught as a technique. You cannot put it on a card. You cannot test whether a practitioner has it. You certainly cannot number it and print it. Which is why one of these two frames could become a numbered workbook on a shop shelf and the other never could. That difference has nothing to do with which one is true.

Here is my reading of what happened next, and it is mine rather than any scholar's. The alchemical vessel worked because it was closed. The closure was the technique itself. Nothing was permitted to escape, and the matter stayed under pressure for exactly as long as the matter needed, which was however long it took. The vessel had no view about the calendar. The vocabulary got out. Out in the open air it can be circulated, taught, priced, and completed in thirty days. The one property it had that mattered is the property it cannot have in public.

One complication, and it goes against the easy version of this story. It would be convenient to say the market invented the ladder and Jung is innocent. He is not. His own book on alchemy reports that the colours were later cut down to three, and that the yellow fell out of use.219 He goes looking for the reason inside the psyche rather than inside the laboratory.220 He presents colour stages, and he presents them as what the alchemists held. So the sequence is not the market's innovation. The market added two things the sequence did not come with, which were the guarantee and the deadline. What got dropped in transit was the conditions. Another person in the room. Duration measured in years. No assurance about the outcome. And the risk has a name. Confronting something difficult only teaches you anything if you can do two things at once, feel it, and think about it while you feel it. A journal of shadow work prompts assumes you already have that pairing.221 It does not build it. The unit of measurement changed too. It went from hundreds of dreams across ten months, interpreted by somebody who was not you, to one page a night, scored by yourself. The second practice is fine on its own terms. It kept the first one's name and the first one's promise, and that is the problem.

2192:58:46fact
The medieval and early modern authorities disagreed at the root about the colour scheme: the pseudo-Lullian Testamentum recognises only three principal colours, black, white and red, the Codicillus counts yellow among four principal colours, John Dastin treats the yellow as transient and unstable and valuable only as a sign to keep working, and George Ripley groups citrine with unsatisfactory intermediate colours and calls it a false citrine.
Jennifer M. Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71:1 (2024), 73-97, doi 10.1080/00026980.2024.2309059
sections 'The decline of citrinitas in alchemical philosophy' and "George Ripley's 'falce citryne'"
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in the full text of Rampling's Ambix article, in the sections 'The decline of citrinitas in alchemical philosophy' and "George Ripley's 'falce citryne'". The article states that Latin alchemists inherited the fourfold progression of nigredo, albedo, citrinitas and rubedo, and then documents the disagreement over whether yellow was a principal colour at all. This is the evidence that the alchemical record contains no fixed itinerary to complete: competent readers of the same tradition could not agree on how many stations there were.
What it does not say
The disagreement is about the STATUS OF YELLOW inside colour schemes, not a demonstration that alchemists had no sequences - they plainly had several, and other texts organise the work by twelve operations rather than by colours at all. The honest line is 'no canonical count', not 'the alchemists had no stages'. Read through a text-extraction proxy of the paywalled publisher page, so the locators are section headings and no page numbers were captured.
2202:58:54fact
Jung himself is one source of the ladder: Psychology and Alchemy records that the colours were later reduced to three and that the yellow fell into disuse, and Jung looked for the reason in inner psychological causes rather than practical ones.
Jennifer M. Rampling, 'Citrination and its Discontents: Yellow as a Sign of Alchemical Change', Ambix 71:1 (2024), 73-97, doi 10.1080/00026980.2024.2309059, footnote 16, quoting C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, trans. Adler and Hull (Princeton University Press, 1968), para. 333
section 'The decline of citrinitas in alchemical philosophy', footnote 16
Read the passage
How it holds
Read in footnote 16 of Rampling's Ambix article, which quotes Jung directly - 'later, about the fifteenth or sixteenth century, the colours were reduced to three, and the xanthosis, otherwise called the citrinitas, gradually fell into disuse or was but seldom mentioned' - citing C. G. Jung, Psychology and Alchemy, trans. Gerhard Adler and R. F. C. Hull (Princeton University Press, 1968), at 333, and adds that 'Jung sought the answer in inner psychological reasons, rejecting a practical explanation for declining interest in citrination.' This is the honest complication Part 14 must state: the sequence is not the market's invention, it is in Jung's own book, and what the market added was the guarantee and the deadline.
What it does not say
Jung's page was NOT opened; what was read is a historian quoting him, with the locator 333 (a Collected Works paragraph number, not a page). So the script may report what Jung wrote as Rampling prints it, and should not read the sentence aloud as a verified quotation from a Princeton copy. Note also what this does and does not show: Jung reporting a reduction from four colours to three is not evidence that the alchemists ran a fixed programme, and Rampling's own article is the argument that they did not.
2212:59:42fact
A psychologist writing about the shadow-work trend states that learning from a confrontation with something difficult requires two capacities at once, feeling the negative emotion and being able to think about it enough to reach a more helpful perspective, and that commentators remain divided over whether Jung's own confrontation was spiritual enlightenment or a psychotic breakdown.
Roger Bretherton, 'Shadow work: why you should be cautious of the TikTok self-help trend', The Conversation, 10 October 2023
the section explaining what learning from the shadow requires, and the paragraph on Jung's own confrontation
Read the passage
How it holds
The article was read in full. Roger Bretherton, Associate Professor in the School of Psychology at the University of Lincoln, writing on 10 October 2023, gives the dual capacity verbatim - 'we must be able to do two things at the same time: feel the negative emotion and be able to think about it enough to come to a more helpful perspective' - and writes that 'Commentators are still divided on whether this was a kind of spiritual enlightenment or a psychotic breakdown.' It supports Part 14's list of what was dropped in transit: a prompt on a page assumes that dual capacity rather than building it, and no journal supplies the second person who used to hold it.
What it does not say
This is an academic's commentary piece in a general-audience outlet, not a study: it reports no measurements, and the article as read contains NO view counts and NO sales figures - the circulating 'billions of views' and journal-sales numbers are not in it and are not sourced anywhere in this part. The one number on the page, that ninety per cent of the shadow is pure gold, is introduced by Bretherton as something Jung 'apparently said' and must not be voiced as either Jung's or a finding.

Everything so far assumes that the vocabulary is a legitimate instrument that got damaged on the way out. There is a reading on which it was never an instrument at all. It begins from a rule Jung himself taught. Taking an image literally is a mistake with a definite shape. A dream of a flood is not a weather report. Reading it as one is the literal reading, and Jung spent a career refusing that move. Now run the same test on the black crust in the pot. Jung read it as a picture of what happens inside one person over the course of a life. So he took an image made by a whole age, across centuries, and made it a report on private inner weather. That is a literal reading too, at the only level that matters, and the charge there is mine. The one on the record is heavier, and it belongs to Wolfgang Giegerich, a Jungian analyst, which is why it lands where it does. His word for what those images once had is in-ness. Meaning exists, he writes, if the meaning of life is as self-evident as the in-ness in water is for fish. He means the groundedness of existence, a sense of embeddedness in life, which is a condition rather than an opinion.222 A fish holds no view about water. That condition ended, on his account, and the ending does not run backwards. Once the sugar cube has been dissolved in the coffee, it cannot be made undissolved. On the cultural level, he writes, there is no way back to the gods, and we cannot go back to a symbolism that is gone.

2223:01:39fact
Giegerich argues that meaning was a condition of embeddedness - in-ness, as self-evident as the in-ness in water is for fish - that this condition has ended irreversibly and cannot be got back on the cultural level, and that the search for meaning is therefore the symptom of its absence rather than the cure for it.
Wolfgang Giegerich, 'The End of Meaning and the Birth of Man: An essay about the state reached in the history of consciousness and an analysis of C. G. Jung's psychology project', Journal of Jungian Theory and Practice 6/1 (2004), 1-65
§1, p. 3 (the self-contradiction in the search for meaning); §3 'The end of in-ness', p. 9
Read the passage
How it holds
'The End of Meaning and the Birth of Man' argues that meaning was a property of being embedded in a world, that modernity has ended that condition irreversibly, and that the demand for meaning is the symptom of the loss rather than its remedy; individuation, on that reading, is a private consolation for a public condition. Part 14 needs it as the strongest version of the case that the whole apparatus is a comfort rather than a method.
What it does not say
READ, replacing an earlier note of 'Recalled: the article was not opened.' The essay text was opened and read through the r.jina.ai text extraction of the author's PDF at jungpage.org; pp. 3, 9, 10-11 and 21-22 were the passages examined. Two limits: (i) jungpage.org is not a scholarly host, so the claim is read but does not yet stand on a source good enough to ship; (ii) the script's relocation clause is NOT supported by this essay and must not be voiced on this source. 'Consolation prize' remains the video's phrase, not Giegerich's.

There is a step past that, and I am the one taking it. What alchemy was doing happened outside anybody's head. It was how a whole civilisation understood matter, worked out over centuries in furnaces and workshops and the books written about them. Jung moved that indoors, into one person's lifetime, where it turns into a story with a hero and a set of stages. That whole relocation is my reading and not his. And Giegerich holds that the world those images belonged to is gone, and cannot be got back by anybody sitting in a consulting room. On that view the hunger for meaning is not the cure for its absence. It is the symptom of the absence, and treating it as a cure is what keeps it going. Asking whether your life has meaning already puts you outside your life, with the whole of it in view. The question gets asked from the bank and not from the water. Put in my words rather than his, individuation comes out as a private consolation prize. The shadow work product is that same structure with the price tag left on.

That is not the end of it, because the charge has an answer, and I am going to make it myself. It assumes that Jung set out to bring a dead symbolism back to life. The order of events in this video says otherwise. The self-experiment starts in 1913, and the alchemical programme starts about fifteen years after it. He went to the corpus looking for a witness, because a man who has had visions cannot cite himself. That is a smaller project than restoration, and a stranger one. It is one man trying to get a private event admitted as evidence. So the two cases stand against each other, and I am not going to settle it here. The hardest thing anybody has said against this material was said by somebody who practises it.

What survives both readings is the smallest thing in this video and the only thing that was ever load bearing. The word itself is the place to end. Melanosis was a black film of sulphides on a piece of metal on a perforated bronze platform, produced by vapour rising from underneath. When it first arrived in this video it sounded like a demotion of your own experience, and it was. It described a state. It said nothing whatever about how long the state would last. The men using the word were looking into a pot and not at a calendar. That poverty is the part that survived the whole descent. A description of a surface cannot tell you that you are late.

So here is the yield, and it is small. The vocabulary gives you a way to read where you are. It does not give you a date.

What goes is the order, the finish line, the thirty days, and the sense that a slow month reports on your character. The naming is honest work and you can keep doing it without embarrassment. The counting was never anybody's to give you. And the fourteenth month is still an unread interval, so whatever you bring to it is what it will say back. It is still the fourteenth month. Nothing about the state has changed. The only thing that has been taken off you is a deadline.
